Compendium · built 2026-10-03

eden4, in one page

eden4 is Eden as one persistent, shared, simulated world. Its inhabitants, Souls, are owned by people, live all the time, and are steered (never driven) by their owners or by their owners' own AI agents. Sim is the running shell (sim.eden.art): the log, the minds, sync, accounts and the agent interface. The Mars engine moves the bodies. Between them sits the kernel: a small grammar of meaning (things, links, time, five kinds of change) in which every action is a card and every play is a line in a replayable ledger.

The designHow eden4 and Sim are built, and what is proposed. R1 Prior art for the kernel · R1 Illustrated tourAgent worlds and gamesInhabited worlds from 1986 to 2026: patterns, trade-offs, traps. R2 The landscape of agent worlds · R2 Illustrated tour · R6 Spore · R7 The Sims · R8 Second Life · R9 SpawnThe numbersVolumes, costs, replay times and engine limits. R3 The ledger on chain · R4 Cheap dialogue · R5 The Mars campus, measuredWhere it standsWhat is live, what is broken, what comes next. Sim status · Resume note

This compendium gathers the design notes, eight research reports, two illustrated tours and the current state. Every page below this one summarises what is under it. Nothing here is decided unless a design note says Gene decided it.

Five things it adds up to

  1. The combination is new. No surveyed project combines owned, always-on inhabitants, server-run minds, outside steering and a public replayable record. The nearest relatives are a six-week experiment (Aivilization) and a text-only agent MMO with direct control (SpaceMolt). Agent worlds
  2. What is missing is a reason to care. Every lasting world gave people an economy, something to make, or a life to follow. Spore, The Sims, Second Life and the 2026 agent worlds all say this in different words. Sim has the ledger and the interface and none of the three yet. Agent worlds
  3. The kernel's weakest point is consent and control. An agreement has no content and cannot be withdrawn, and control is all-or-nothing. Fixes are well understood (commitment protocols, revocable powers) and the same parts give a design for scenes. Ideas for the kernel
  4. The expensive things have cheap first steps. A verifiable, chain-ready ledger costs about a week of work and under $15 a year. Dialogue can be about fifty times cheaper by rendering only what someone sees. The numbers
  5. The real Mars campus works. Souls walk, sit, gather and talk there; real time holds to about 100 people, and one engine hot spot sets that limit. The numbers

Decisions waiting for Gene

Decision Options in brief Where
Where the Mars engine's code lives after the takeover copy into the Sim repo at the fork (recommended), or keep it separate 19 §9
What a scene is a practice built from kernel parts, staged by an engine coordinator (recommended) ideas, 19 §5
How places get furnished an authored file as a starting state, or built by Souls through cards 19 §9, R5
How agreement works requests with content and a lifecycle; revocable powers instead of all-or-nothing control R1 ideas 1–2
How much the Soul does alone a per-Soul autonomy dial plus a list of acts a Soul never does alone R7
Whether presence may ever pay every world that paid for presence got bots; three eden4 notes currently do agent worlds
What is in the public record include decisions (61% of events) or only plays and mints; publish the log or only commitments; which keys sign R3 §8
What owning a body conveys define it before the first sale R8
How an exploit is answered in a history that never changes decide the remedy (claw-back, levy, re-mint) before manna exists R7

The kernel and cards

18 — A small kernel, an open deck, and a world that can change itself

3,351 words · 18-KERNEL-CARDS-AND-SELF-CHANGE.md · updated 2026-10-01

In short

The world's grammar: things, links, time and five kinds of change, with everything else as open vocabulary. Covers cards, decks, plans, minting and speech, what running it with live models taught, and how the world could safely change its own software.

Rounds 16–18, 2026-10-01. Status: sections 3, 4 and 5 are built and running (Sim repo, packages/kernel, tested on the toy engine and on the Mars engine); sections 6 and 8 are still proposals. Section 3 was rewritten after Gene's round-17 correction (the first draft's nine kinds were vocabulary, not fundamentals), and sections 4, 5, 9 and 10 were brought up to what was built and what running it with live models taught. How this compiles down to the Mars engine is in 19-MARS-BRIDGE.md.

It answers Gene's round-16 brief: reconcile a structured, immutable ontology with open-ended AI-generated content; keep a Soul cheap to run; let the world be more expressive than the engine can yet depict; and ask whether Souls could change the software that runs the world.

Gene's goals, in his words (round 16):

  1. "a ledger which records actions over time, one that we can potentially put on chain … the generator/world model is upgradeable over time -- you are saving an action space and making those declarations valuable. immutable also."
  2. "a boundary between the agents who control souls / steer them, and between whatever it is that converts mushy intents from agents into actions that predictably alter or upgrade the world"
  3. "world is upgradeable -- possibly via code, or in a controlled ontology"
  4. "ontology is small and easy to comprehend/intuit albeit even if kind of abstract"
  5. "cheap to run a soul passively … 1 soul costs <$1 per month to run passively, while allowing soul owners to expend their own AI tokens to increase their expressivity? should this be allowed? i'm undecided."
  6. "world is very dynamic and expressive, even if the 3d engine / capabilities lag behind depicting it … 'go to rob's party, and ask him if you can DJ' -> soul goes, talks to rob, setups DJ kit, and dance party breaks out. it just creates animation todos which we will get to"
  7. "can we be even changing the software that runs the world via souls or is that intrinsically insecure or likely to lead to some catastrophic outcomes"

1. The move: structure the effects, leave the content open

The struggle between "define an ontology" and "let models generate" dissolves if the two are assigned to different things:

  • Effects are structured. There is a tiny, fixed set of ways the world's state can change (about a dozen primitives). Everything that happens, however novel, is a short program over those primitives.
  • Content is open. What things are called, what they mean, how they are described and depicted is text and media, generated freely, attached to the structured record but never needed to replay it.

This is how Infinite Craft works: unlimited named things, one fixed operation. It is also Gene's round-6 position (verbs closed, nouns open) and his round-7 one (the input is not mushy by the time it reaches the center), carried one step further: the closed part shrinks from "a list of verbs" to "a list of effects", and verbs themselves become open, minted content.

2. Three layers of truth

Layer What it holds Who writes it Canonical?
Meaning (the kernel) Who is where, with whom, in what role; what exists; who knows, owes, promised, made what deterministic code applying logged actions yes: this is the ledger's world
Depiction (the engine, later a world model) Positions, motion, animation, meshes, light, sound Mars engine today; could be a generative world model no: derived from meaning, may lag, upgradeable freely
Interpretation (models) Suggestions: which action, what new action could exist, what words were said, what the story is Jev, small LLMs, owners' agents no: models propose, the kernel disposes

Consequences:

  • The world can be richer than the engine can show. If Wren becomes the DJ and the engine has no DJ animation, the meaning is still true and logged; the engine shows a fallback (standing at the kit, a label) and the missing animation goes on a depiction backlog, ranked by how often it is needed. Gene's "animation todos" become a literal, automatic list.
  • A pixel or mesh world model (GWM-style) fits as the depiction layer, conditioned on meaning. It cannot be the ledger: a latent cannot be replayed, verified, owned or upgraded without losing history.
  • The Mars engine keeps its job (physical feasibility, movement, embodiment) and gains a clear boundary: it answers "can this body do that here, and what does it look like", not "what happened".

3. The kernel: a grammar, not a list of nouns

Gene, round 17: "promise, card, gathering, bond? these feel not fundamental as time, position, identity, motion, immutable attributes, intents." He was right. The first draft listed nine kinds of thing; all of them were vocabulary. What is fundamental is smaller:

Fundamental In the kernel
identity a Thing: an id, a kind (a word), a name, who made it Wren; the campfire; Rob's gathering
immutable attributes a Thing's traits Wren's description; a place's spot on the ground
mutable attributes a Thing's states music: playing
position a Thing's at: another Thing Wren is at the campfire, which is in the center
relation a Link: a kind (a word), from one Thing to another, optionally with a lifetime Rob hosts the gathering; Wren asked Rob
time the tick, and lifetimes on links an agreement stands for thirty minutes
motion, and every other event five changes below

The five changes are the only ways state can alter:

Change Says
appear / vanish a thing comes to exist, or stops
set one state of one thing takes a value
move a thing is now at another thing
link / unlink a relation begins or ends
say someone says an act (greet, ask, agree, decline, invite, tell, chat) to someone, about something

Everything else is vocabulary, and vocabulary is open: kinds of thing (gathering, DJ kit, kite), kinds of link (hosts, attends, knows, dj of), manners (warmly, shyly), and cards. A gathering is a Thing whose kind is the word "gathering"; a promise is a link; a bond is a link; a role is a link. None of them is known to the kernel's code. The way to hold it in mind: the grammar is fixed, the vocabulary is open, and an action is a sentence: who, in what manner, which card, on what.

One rule: an actor acts only for itself

Gene on consent, round 17: the state "has to be somehow emergent from their combined conceptions… coherent global state as a result of otherwise discorded actions." So the rule is about coherence, and it is one rule:

An actor changes only itself, what it controls, and what it made. Anything shared happens because separate actions fit together.

Precisely:

  • A Soul can move, set states on and speak for only itself. No change can target another Soul.
  • A link must start from the actor.
  • A thing that is not a Soul can be changed by whoever controls it (holds a link whose kind is marked as giving control: hosts, made, holds, dj of). If nobody controls it, only its maker can. Places the world made are nobody's to change.
  • A control link can be taken only on something the actor made, or when someone who already controls the thing has agreed.
  • What was said, asked and agreed exists only because it was said. Saying "ask" leaves an open question until it is answered; saying "agree" is what creates an agreement. No card can write or erase these directly.
  • Bounds: a Soul may have 24 things of its making and 400 links at a time.

Joint outcomes therefore emerge. Wren becomes the DJ at Rob's party through four separate plays by two Souls: Rob hosts, Wren asks, Rob says yes, Wren takes the role (which the world allows only because the host agreed). Nobody acted on anyone.

4. Cards: the action space as minted content

A card is a named, reusable action:

{ "name": "ask to be the dj",
  "summary": "Ask the host of a gathering to let you play the music",
  "target": { "kind": "gathering" },
  "derive": { "host": { "link": "hosts", "to": "$target" } },
  "when":   [ { "at": ["$actor", "$target"] }, { "noneLinkedTo": ["dj of", "$target"] },
              { "notLinked": ["asked", "$actor", "$host"] } ],
  "then":   [ { "say": ["$actor", "$host", "ask"], "about": "being the DJ at $target.name" } ],
  "approach": [ { "verb": "walk_to", "params": { "to": "$host", "standoff": 1.3 } } ],
  "during":   [ { "verb": "wave", "params": { "at": "$host", "seconds": 2 } } ],
  "wants": ["hand gesture while asking"], "takes": "seconds" }

when is conditions on the kernel; then is a short list of the five changes; approach and during are how the body shows it (before and after the changes take effect); wants names motions the engine lacks. The full grammar is served at /cards.md and is the compiler's prompt.

What is built:

  • A deck per Soul, starting from fifteen base cards (go to, rest, wait, wander, greet, talk with, chat, say yes, say no, host a gathering, join, leave, end the gathering, perform, dance), plus three manners chosen to suit its description (one small Jev call at birth).
  • A plan is an agenda of cards. An owner's wish becomes an ordered list of steps, each a card and optionally a target, or of someone for a thing that does not exist yet ("Rob's party" before it starts).
  • Minting. When the vocabulary lacks a card, a compiler model (Haiku) writes one. It is checked (grammar; the one rule; link kinds declared; a card that changes its target must say where the right comes from; the engine's verbs; size) and, if valid, minted: hashed, logged with its author, added to a shared library and dealt to the Soul. Owners' agents can propose cards the same way, with no model involved.
  • The ledger records plays and mints. A play is (card hash, who, on what, in what manner). Replay applies the card's changes with deterministic code and calls no model. The kernel replays from the log without any engine. Checked on the live world on 2026-10-01: 11,939 events replayed in 35 ms and reproduced the running server's kernel hash exactly.
  • Three tiers decide. Reflex (code, free): a question is answered before anything else; when cards serve the owner's plan the Soul chooses only among them; a Soul that asked waits for the answer. Mid-brain (Jev, ~$0.0001): a real choice among several cards, with manner and pace. Compiler (Haiku, ~$0.01): only when an owner states a wish.
  • Looking one step ahead. If the plan's next step cannot be played, any card that would bring it closer serves the plan (go there, join, end what stands in the way). Nobody writes travel. A step that cannot be played is passed over when a later one can; a plan impossible for ten minutes is given up and noted.

What running it with live models taught (each is now a rule in the code):

Seen Rule
Two Souls replied to each other's small talk for ever one talking play is a whole exchange; a pair does not start another for two minutes
An asker wandered off before the answer whoever asked waits; whoever was asked answers first
A host left their own party to chat elsewhere hosts talk to who is there
"Rob's party" was bound to a party Rob only attended, or to an invented id "his" means he hosts, made or holds it; unknown ids become of
A compiled card set the music without holding any right to refused at minting, with the reason given back to the compiler
A later remark overwrote an unanswered question a question is its own link until answered
An impossible choice was retried every two seconds a failed option is not offered again for a minute

5. Speech: acts are state, words are depiction

Built as proposed. The act (greet, ask, agree, decline…) and what it is about are kernel state and drive what can happen next. The words are written afterwards by a small model from the act, the two personalities and the manner, saved beside the record and never replayed. One play of a talking card renders a short exchange in both voices.

Gene chose on-demand rendering "while I can." It costs about $0.002 an exchange, so the speech budget goes first to questions, answers and Souls with owners; small talk between natives is rendered by a model only while someone is watching, and otherwise gets a plain line.

Still open (Gene: dialogue is the hardest part): whether a Soul can say something new that becomes state (a promise with content, a rumour, a name for a place), and how what was said is remembered beyond two minutes.

6. Knowing at several scales

Following Gene's answer on fog of war, a Soul's knowledge is layered, and an agent sees what its Soul knows plus public news:

Layer Content Freshness
Here who and what is in reach, what was just said to me live
Around the place and its neighbours in summary seconds to minutes
Atlas places I have been or heard of, as I last knew them; named places I know only by reputation hours to days, with age shown
News scheduled events, notable things today public bulletin
People my Bonds and what I remember of them as of last meeting

Travel takes real time, so distant knowledge is stale unless someone brings news. Memories are capped per Soul; old ones fade or are kept by a slow reflection step.

7. What a Soul costs

Measured on 2026-10-01 with live models (eight Souls, the DJ story, three minutes): a mid-brain choice $0.00009; a reflex decision nothing; attuning manners at birth $0.00003; an exchange of words $0.002; compiling a wish $0.007 to $0.012 (about 7,700 input tokens, a third of that when the prompt is cached). One dollar a month still buys a choice every one to three minutes around the clock, and reflexes now take many decisions for free. The two costs that scale with people are words and wishes, and both are metered per tier.

8. Can Souls change the software that runs the world?

Yes, at the right layer. The danger is not self-change; it is self-change in a language that can do anything, with authority it was never given.

Level What changes Who may change it Guard
0. Direction intents, notes owners, agents closed schema
1. Cards the action space Souls' minds, owners' agents, the compiler a small language with no loops and no access to anything but the effects; validation; consent; conservation; cost ceilings; quarantine of new cards
2. Dials and content prices, caps, schedules, places proposals by Souls or owners → a public decision (the daily ritual) every change is a logged rules event
3. The kernel and engine new effects, new kinds, real code proposals as code changes written by agents; never applied by Souls replay the entire ledger on a shadow copy; invariants and tests must hold; soak on staging; a signed epoch; rollback by construction

Why this does not brick the world: the ledger plus the hash of the code that ran it is always enough to rebuild the last good state, so a bad change is an epoch that gets reverted, not a loss. Why it resists malware: model output is always data; the kernel has no network, files or keys; level-1 programs cannot express anything outside the effects; and nothing a Soul says can reach level 3 without passing through replay and a signature.

The ambitious version: agents continuously propose level-3 changes, an automated pipeline replays history on a fork and scores them, and the daily ritual ratifies at most one. "The day glass was invented" becomes something the world does to itself, under rules it cannot skip.

Real risks to name: injected text steering the compiler (bounded by validation and consent); card spam (bounded by mint cost); cards that are valid but degenerate, such as loops of mutual gifting to farm standing (needs monitoring and the ability to retire a card); and slow drift in what the compiler considers normal (needs a fixed test set of intents).

9. Where the tranche stands

Step State Where to see it
1. Kernel built grammar tab: things, links, vocabulary; every play as a sentence with the changes it made
2. Decks built my soul: plan, manners, each card and whether it can be played now, and why not
3. Minting built (compiler, validator, agent proposals) grammar: the card library, click a card for its conditions and changes. A list of failed compile attempts is in the brain tab, not yet a foundry view
4. Gatherings and speech built watch: overheard; my soul: what was said
5. The DJ test passes: by agenda with a fake brain (toy and Mars), and from two plain wishes with live models on the Mars engine apps/server/src/sync.test.ts, mars.test.ts
6. Knowing not built still section 6
7. Depiction backlog built grammar: animation to-do, ranked by how often cards wanted a motion
8. Self-change first step built: a world's base vocabulary is written into its own log, replay starts from it, and adopting a newer set is a logged rules event (level 2 of section 8). Proposals, shadow replay and epochs are not built adopt_vocabulary in the admin API
Notebook built notebook tab: six open questions beside live evidence

10. Gene's answers (round 17) and what is open now

  1. Meaning first: yes.
  2. Consent: reframed as coherence: the state must emerge from separate actions. Built as the one rule in section 3.
  3. Cards: "each soul has its own cards." Built as a personal deck over a shared library.
  4. Owners' agents proposing cards: "would be fun and powerful." Built.
  5. Words: dialogue is the hardest part; on-demand for now.
  6. Self-change boundary: fine as proposed.
  7. The DJ scenario as the acceptance test: yes. It passes.
  8. Money: not yet.

Open now, in the order they will bite:

  1. Scenes. Several bodies in one motion (a class, a meal, carrying a beam) against a kernel where each acts only for itself. Proposal in doc 19.
  2. Retiring and revising cards. Nothing can be un-minted. Who may retire a card; can a card be improved without changing what past plays meant?
  3. Things that last. A card can make a thing; only "with another thing" ends it. Do made things decay, get owned, get traded?
  4. Locality. Any card can target anything anywhere; only its own conditions stop it. Should reach be a default rule?
  5. Memory. said fades in two minutes and knows is for ever. Section 6.
  6. Cost of wishes. A cheap matcher for wishes that existing cards already cover, keeping the compiler for new ones.

The Mars bridge

19 — The Mars bridge: how the Eden runtime compiles down to the Mars engine

2,891 words · 19-MARS-BRIDGE.md · updated 2026-10-01

In short

How the shell drives the real Mars engine through a body language of nine verbs, how Mars's own activities become vocabulary, the proposed scene layer, and what the takeover has to change in the engine.

Contents
  • The short versionThe whole Sim shell (event log, kernel, cards, minds, speech, client sync, agent interface) now runs with the real Mars engine moving the bodies.
  • 1. Three levels, two translationsThe body language is the stable middle. Cards are written against it, so a card minted in one world works in another.
  • 2. Three rules the adapter addsThe Mars runtime was built to rehearse and record bounded scenes that a caller choreographs.
  • 3. How Mars concepts map to the kernelA table.
  • 4. Mars's activities as special casesGene: the Mars world "has a specific set of activities which are supposed to become special cases of your ontology." Mars has five hand-built activity coordinators.
  • 5. The missing layer: scenesToday each Soul's depiction is its own program. That is enough for hosting, joining, asking and talking.
  • 6. What the takeover has to change in the engineFound by building the adapter, in rough order of importance.
  • 7. Running Martian simulations after the forkGene: "still be able to run martian sims this way in the mars world." Because the adapter wraps the engine and changes nothing inside it, both ways of running stay available on the same code.
  • 8. Proposed first daysDay 1. Decide where the engine lives (recommendation below).
  • 9. Questions for GeneScenes (section 5): is "a kernel Thing staged by one coordinator, and re-staged from the kernel after a restart" the right way to keep Mars's choreography without making it the truth?

Status (2026-10-01): proven on the proving ground, planned for the campus. Written the day before the Mars engine is taken over. Everything under "Proven" was run today against the Mars checkout at commit 9638489 (release 27), imported read-only; nothing in the Mars repository was changed. The code is packages/engine-mars in the Sim repo. Everything under "Plan" is a proposal for Gene.

The short version

The whole Sim shell (event log, kernel, cards, minds, speech, client sync, agent interface) now runs with the real Mars engine moving the bodies. Start the server with ENGINE=mars and six Souls are born on the Mars proving ground, walk Mars's routed paths, sit on Mars's benches, take places at Mars's campfire and stand in Mars's conversation circles. The DJ story passes there: Rob hosts at the fire, Wren walks over and asks, Rob says yes, Wren becomes the DJ.

Nothing in the kernel or the cards knows which engine is underneath. That is the claim of doc 18 ("meaning replays without the engine"), now tested with two different engines.

Test on the Mars engine Result
Engine conformance (determinism, seeds diverge, mid-action restore, hashes, verbs, clean failure) 6 of 6
A program of body steps runs in order, with progress events and no physical violations pass
An impossible first step is refused without disturbing the actor pass
A conversation borrows a free body; that body's own program takes it back pass
A world restored from a snapshot mid-program carries on identically pass
Replay of the event log from genesis reproduces the live world; the kernel replays with no engine at all pass
Restart from a snapshot plus later events equals the uninterrupted world pass
Two clients fed keyframes agree with the server's view hash pass
The DJ story through the real server, with Mars bodies pass
The DJ story from two plain-language wishes, live models, Mars bodies (compiler mints the cards; Rob agrees by reflex) pass, $0.04
A world's own verbs (mars:take_slot, mars:face…) used directly as steps pass
One of Mars's own authored scenarios (four people take bowls and spoons, serve themselves stew, sit round the fire and eat) run unchanged as four programs pass
20 people strolling 0.36 ms per tick (budget: 50 ms)

1. Three levels, two translations

   MEANING            a Soul plays a card on a target            kernel: things, links, five changes
      │                 (ask to be the dj → Rob's gathering)      engine-independent, replays alone
      │  the card's depiction: a short list of body steps
      ▼
   BODY LANGUAGE      walk_to · sit · stand · rest · wave ·       what ONE actor's body does
      │               converse · gather · perform · wander        nine verbs, the same for every engine
      │  the adapter lowers each step when it begins
      ▼
   ENGINE VERBS       Mars: walk_to{x,z} · face · sit · take_slot ·     routes, slots, reservations,
                      converse{actors} · gather · campfire_circle …     pre-emption, collisions

The body language is the stable middle. Cards are written against it, so a card minted in one world works in another. An engine adapter is the only code that knows engine verbs. A world can also expose its own verbs for vocabulary that belongs to it: on Mars they appear as mars:serve, mars:eat, mars:carry and so on (52 of them), and a Mars card may use them in its depiction.

How each body step lowers on Mars today:

Body step Mars actions Notes
walk_to {to, standoff} walk_to {x, z} then face The adapter picks a reachable standing point near the target, trying eight directions.
sit {on} sit {fixture} Mars includes the approach, the turn and the sit-down. The seat is kept after the step ends.
gather {at} campfire_circle, or gather {fixture}, or gather {region} Whichever the target supports; if there is no place to take, stand near it.
converse {with} converse {actors: [me, them]} Only if the other body is free. Otherwise face them and wait.
wave {at} face then wait No waving in Mars yet.
rest, perform wait No such motion in Mars yet.
ambient_wander several walk_to and pauses Mars's own ambient_wander cannot be resumed from a snapshot, so the adapter builds strolling from walks.
stand stand

2. Three rules the adapter adds

The Mars runtime was built to rehearse and record bounded scenes that a caller choreographs. A world that never stops, with many independent minds, needs three things the caller used to guarantee by hand.

  1. One actor, one program. A Soul's decision replaces whatever its body was doing. The adapter keeps one program per actor, runs its steps in order, reports each step and the ending, and releases any seat or circle before a step that needs the body free.
  2. Joint staging is borrowed. Many Mars verbs take several actors in one action (converse {actors}, carry {crew}, the classroom). In the kernel an actor changes only itself. The bridge between the two: a depiction may borrow another body while that body is free, the other Soul's own program takes its body back at any time, and the borrower treats that as the scene ending, never as a failure. Mars's pre-emption (a directed action releases a social hold) is exactly the mechanism this needs.
  3. Ask before acting. In Mars a refused instruction still pulls a person out of what they were doing. The adapter asks verb.plan() first (it has no side effects) and only then submits. The same call answers the card system's question "is this physically possible right now?".

3. How Mars concepts map to the kernel

Kernel Mars engine State of the mapping
Soul (a Thing) a human entity; traits.body holds the entity id done
Place (a Thing) a region (polygon) or a fixture entity; traits.point or traits.entity done through anchors()
Region Mars regions are sparse islands, so the adapter adds one region for "everywhere else" done; real maps need districts
at derived from where the play happened (kernel), never read back from poses done
Card when kernel conditions, plus verb.plan() for the first body step done
Card then (the five changes) nothing: Mars has no notion of roles, agreements or ownership by design: meaning lives in the kernel
Card depiction a program of body steps done
A play takes effect when the approach steps have finished (step progress events) done
Gathering (a Thing with hosts, attends) separate holds per person at the same fixture works; a scene would stage it better (section 5)
say a conversation circle if the listener is free, else facing them; the words are rendered separately done
Manner (warmly, shyly…) nothing yet an animation todo
Control links (hosts, holds, made) nearest thing is reservedBy, which is per action and not social kernel only
Things made by cards (a DJ kit) no entity is created needs "appear" to place a catalog item (section 6)
wants on a card nothing this is the list of motions to build next, ranked by how often cards ask

4. Mars's activities as special cases

Gene: the Mars world "has a specific set of activities which are supposed to become special cases of your ontology." Mars has five hand-built activity coordinators. Each is a small state machine that owns several people. Here is what each becomes.

Mars coordinator What it hard-codes In the kernel
classroom_session with classroom_control {join, depart, question, advance, cancel} host, presenters, audience, seats, a queue of questions, presenter rotation, a talk afterwards A Thing of kind class. Links: hosts, presents at, attends, wants to ask. Cards: start a class, take a seat, present, raise a hand, call on, leave. Each control intent is one Soul's own play. The phases are no longer a script; they are whatever the plays add up to.
shared_food_break two named people fetch utensils, serve, eat, return A meal gathering. Cards: serve yourself (mars:take_item, mars:serve), eat with (sit, mars:eat). Any number of people.
purposeful_routine with its control verb join a session, then a meal, then rest, then finish notes; pause and resume An agenda. This is the one Mars coordinator the shell already replaces entirely: an ordered plan a mind follows, re-thinks and resumes.
gathering_continuation "after this gathering, do that" An agenda step that follows a leave, or a step with of bound to the gathering.
setup_room move one chair and one table to an authored layout A card set up the room (mars:move_fixture), allowed to whoever controls the room.

Construction (carry {crew}, raise_upright, connect_beam, hand_up…) is the harder family: one Mars action needs two to four bodies working as one. The kernel form is a crew: a Thing that Souls join by their own play (join the crew), with a card (carry the beam) that becomes playable when the crew has the people the Mars verb needs. That is the same shape as asking a host and being answered: joint outcomes emerge from single-actor plays.

So the claim holds in both directions. Every Mars activity can be written as vocabulary (a kind of Thing, a few links, a few cards). And the Mars coordinators remain useful as staging: a well-choreographed way to depict a class or a meal once the kernel says one is happening.

5. The missing layer: scenes

Today each Soul's depiction is its own program. That is enough for hosting, joining, asking and talking. It is not enough for a class with seating and a question queue, or for four people carrying a beam.

Proposal. A scene binds one kernel Thing (a gathering, a class, a crew) to one Mars coordinator action:

  • When the Thing appears and enough members have joined, the adapter starts the coordinator (classroom_session, carry).
  • When a Soul plays join or leave, the adapter sends the matching control intent (classroom_control {join}), or re-stages.
  • When the Thing vanishes, the coordinator is cancelled.
  • A member's own program always wins: Mars releases that member (canYieldMembers, releaseMembers) and the scene goes on without them.
  • After a restart, scenes are re-staged from the kernel. Most Mars coordinators cannot be resumed from a snapshot, and they do not need to be: the kernel knows who is in the class, so people are simply seated again.

This is the answer to "the eden world runtime needs to reduce or compile down to that": cards compile to body steps for one actor, and kernel Things compile to scenes for many. Scenes are not built. The only instance so far is the smallest one: a conversation borrowing a free body.

6. What the takeover has to change in the engine

Found by building the adapter, in rough order of importance.

  1. A way to add and remove people while running. There is none; the adapter edits world.entities and restores canonical order, which is what the Mars world editor does offline. It needs to be a logged, checked operation.
  2. Retention. Runtime.records and Runtime.events grow for ever. Fine for a five-minute take; a world of 25 Souls adds roughly 70,000 records a day. The adapter prunes them from outside.
  3. Resumability. Only eight verbs resume from a snapshot exactly (walk_to, take_slot, sit, stand, face, converse, gather, campfire_circle). The body language uses only those. eat, serve, carry, every coordinator and Mars's own ambient_wander do not. Either they become resumable, or (preferred, see section 5) the world accepts re-staging from the kernel after a restart.
  4. Solo expression. There is no way for one person to wave, talk without a circle, sing or dance. The wants lists on cards will rank these by demand.
  5. Movement cannot be predicted from a decision. Mars walkers yield, queue and re-plan, so a client cannot extrapolate a path the way it does with the toy engine. The adapter broadcasts a keyframe per moving body four times a second (the same PoseSample format Mars's take recorder uses) and clients draw a quarter of a second behind. The keyframe interval must divide the heartbeat interval, so the view hash still compares like with like.
  6. Things that cards create. A card can make a Thing (a DJ kit). Nothing appears in the engine. Needs: "appear" may name a catalog item, and the adapter places it at a free spot.
  7. The campus is unfurnished. The real 2026 campus loads in 3 ms as walls, decks and nine regions, with six people and no fixtures at all. Benches, chairs, tables and fires exist only inside scenario files. A Mars world for Eden needs a furnishing file: which fixtures stand where.
  8. Levels. The proving ground is flat. Decks, stairs and rack beams (level, climb_frame, beam_walk) are untested through the adapter.

7. Running Martian simulations after the fork

Gene: "still be able to run martian sims this way in the mars world." Because the adapter wraps the engine and changes nothing inside it, both ways of running stay available on the same code.

Mars studio today Mars as a world in Eden
World a campus year file, forked into a workspace the same file plus a furnishing file, as one world's genesis
Who decides a caller submits verbs or a sequence template Souls' minds choose cards; owners set agendas
Scripted scenes mars-sequence/1 templates, rehearse then execute a cast with fixed agendas and no model: a Soul whose mind is its script. Or a program whose steps are raw mars: verbs, for exact choreography.
Activities five coordinators a Mars vocabulary pack (cards for class, meal, building a bay, riding), staged by those coordinators as scenes
Output a take (pose tracks, events) the same keyframes, recorded; a take is a slice of the world's stream
Time bounded, as fast as it computes continuous at 20 ticks a second, or a private instance run fast

A "Martian sim" in the new terms is: start an instance of the Mars world with a cast and their agendas, run it for a while, keep the recording. The meal scenario already runs this way: its authored steps, addressed as mars: verbs, become one program per person, and the adapter's program runner replaces the scenario's hand-written step driver. The history years, the museum, the evidence and provenance rules and the cinematography stay Mars's own and are untouched by any of this.

8. Proposed first days

  1. Day 1. Decide where the engine lives (recommendation below). Run staging with ENGINE=mars on the proving ground so Gene can watch real bodies in the existing pages. Fix what the first hour of watching shows.
  2. Day 2. A furnished world: the 2026 campus with a first set of fixtures, its regions as kernel places, an arrival point. Souls living on the campus.
  3. Day 3. The first scene: a gathering staged by one Mars coordinator, with join and leave as control intents. Then a Mars vocabulary pack: cook dinner, give a talk, build a rack bay as cards.
  4. After. Engine changes from section 6, in order, each behind the conformance suite.

Where the engine lives. Three options: keep importing by path (works today, but two repositories drift), copy the engine into the Sim repo as packages/engine (one repository, one history from the day of the fork), or make Sim a consumer of a separate engine package. Recommendation: copy it in at the moment Mars development stops, keep packages/engine-mars as the only code that touches it, and keep the conformance suite as the wall between them. The Mars repository is local-only and has never been pushed, so this is also the moment its engine first reaches GitHub; that is Gene's call.

9. Questions for Gene

  1. Scenes (section 5): is "a kernel Thing staged by one coordinator, and re-staged from the kernel after a restart" the right way to keep Mars's choreography without making it the truth?
  2. Body language: is a fixed, small, engine-independent set of body verbs the right middle layer, with raw engine verbs allowed only in vocabulary that belongs to one world?
  3. Furnishing: should fixtures be placed by a file someone authors, or by Souls through cards (the eden4 idea of building), with the file only as a starting state?
  4. The engine's home: copy into the Sim repo at the fork, or keep separate?

Parameters and trade-offs

13 — Parameters and trade-offs

1,681 words · 13-PARAMETERS-AND-TRADEOFFS.md · updated 2026-09-29

In short

Every number the world will need (arrival, the ritual, steering and idle life, economy, space, engine), what each end of its range gives, a lean, and whether Gene has shelved it for the community.

Contents
  • Philosophy: shape now, numbers laterFew kinds of things, many dials. A new mechanism is expensive forever; a new value for an existing dial is one rules event.
  • 1. Arrival (how bodies enter)Gene, round 8: newcomers must be able to enter right away. Waiting for a daily ceremony means losing most of them.
  • 2. The daily ritual (what it is for)Gene wants "one something fundamental a day" to build a ritual around, but it can no longer gate births.
  • 3. Steering and idle lifeGene, round 8: "letting go" was meant to solve idle owners' bodies still feeling alive and influential, not to make steering lose meaning.
  • 4. EconomyA table.
  • 5. SpaceA table.
  • 6. Simulation and engineA table.
  • Which to decide now vs. laterDecide early because they change the shape: arrival pricing form (§1), what the daily ritual is (§2), flat steering (§3), idle influence (§3), derived aggregates and intent time (§5).

Round 8, 2026-09-29. Gene: "i want to shelf some of the final decisions on some of these and possibly just let them happen organically depending on what early community likes. i dont need to design everything now."

This file is the working method that follows from that. Fix the shape of the world (what kinds of things exist and how they change). Leave the numbers as dials that get set by rule versions, informed by what the early community actually does. Versioned rules (03) make every dial change a legitimate, logged event rather than a retroactive fix, so shelving a decision costs little.

Each dial below has: what it controls, what each end of its range gives, Claude's current lean (not decided), and a status:

  • Shelved — Gene has chosen to let this settle with the early community.
  • Open — still needs a design answer, not just a number.
  • Shape leaning — the form is proposed; only the number is shelved.

Philosophy: shape now, numbers later

  • Few kinds of things, many dials. A new mechanism is expensive forever; a new value for an existing dial is one rules event.
  • Start loose, tighten on evidence. It's easier to add friction when something is abused than to remove friction nobody enjoys. Where a dial has a "generous" and a "strict" end, v1 sits near generous.
  • Never make the future artificially harder. (Gene, round 8.) The world and its rules must stay easy to evolve. Friction belongs to places and prices, not to the ability to change the rules.
  • Watch, then set. Early days are measurement: who steers, how often, what gets built, where bodies go. Dials are set from that record, and the log makes the record exact.

1. Arrival (how bodies enter)

Gene, round 8: newcomers must be able to enter right away. Waiting for a daily ceremony means losing most of them. "There could be a birthing room but it's not tied to a time, it's just new members falling in whenever."

Dial One end Other end Lean
Birth rate Fixed, 1/day (Nouns). Scarcity, a clean 30-year arc, the growth rate is the scaling roadmap. Caps the world at ~365/yr forever. Unlimited, flat price. Fast growth, no scarcity, spam risk, compute arrives before design does. A price curve over a target schedule (VRGDA-style): each body's price rises when births run ahead of the target and falls when behind. Continuous, instant, one knob. The target curve itself is shelved.
Target curve Linear (N/day). Exponential or logistic. Shelved. Start near linear, loosen if demand is real.
Cap / end Hard cap (~10k) and an ending that echoes Abraham's release. Open-ended. Shelved. The cap was a consequence of 1/day, not a goal of its own.
Arrival place Center: first view is the famous city and the ritual ground. Frontier: fits "poor frontier", room to build at once, worse first five minutes. Center, then walk outward. Open.
Entry price floor Real money for every body (checkout friction). Free or near-free when births are behind target. Open. The next attrition point after waiting. A free spectator mode would be a new mechanism, so not proposed yet.

Rejected in round 8: a daily batch ceremony that settles births at the ritual (Claude's first proposal) because it makes newcomers wait.

Owner ↔ body configurations (Gene asked to go through them):

Configuration Gives Costs
One owner, one body (current) Your own body; your agent attaches to it. The hook. Supply is gated by births.
Many owners share a body (a DAO wraps it) Collective play, clubs, cults. Already decided as possible. None new. It's opt-in.
Influence without ownership (many people nudge unowned bodies) Anyone can play without buying; spirits and fauna become communal. No "mine"; no agent attachment; needs a way to weigh many voices.
Owner decoupled from body entirely Radical flexibility. Loses the core hook.

Lean: keep one-owner-one-body as the base. Shared control stays opt-in (DAO wrap). Influence-without-ownership, if wanted, lives on unowned creatures through scene intents rather than on citizens. Open.

2. The daily ritual (what it is for)

Gene wants "one something fundamental a day" to build a ritual around, but it can no longer gate births.

Option Gives Costs
The manna fall (Claude's lean): at the lunar-rotating time, the day's UBI falls at the center; the ground grows its daily ring; everyone who arrived since the last fall is welcomed and named aloud; rule upgrades take effect. Bundles things that already happen daily onto one clock. Biblical rhyme with manna. The world's biggest daily crowd. Needs nothing new; the risk is it feels administrative unless staged well.
A daily chosen one: one arrival per day is elevated (auctioned or blessed) and gets the center moment. Keeps a Nouns-style prestige object and Abraham's blessing. A class distinction and a second mechanism.
One new thing a day: a material, a verb, a biome. Ties the ritual to the world's evolution. Pressure to invent daily; verbs should stay rare.

Attendance: attending should count as attention (feeds inertia), not as income. Making manna physically collectable only at the center would give a free center-rich gradient but breaks UBI and punishes idle bodies. Lean: don't.

Ritual time: rotates with the lunar calendar (decided for the birth ritual; presumably carries over to whatever the ritual becomes).

3. Steering and idle life

Gene, round 8: "letting go" was meant to solve idle owners' bodies still feeling alive and influential, not to make steering lose meaning. He sees a trade-off, and he wants the world to evolve without it becoming artificially harder.

Dial One end Other end Lean
Steering price vs. age Rises with age (old "letting go"): bodies drift toward autonomy, the world "becomes itself". Punishes engaged owners. Flat forever: steering always matters. Flat. Aliveness comes from the standing intent, not from making steering expensive. Rate limits for fairness only (Q26).
Identity edits (name, description, appearance) Never (one-shot birth description is the whole identity). Any time. Birth description fixed; cosmetic edits allowed. The doubling/Fibonacci schedule is dropped unless the community wants it. Shelved.
Intent expressiveness (Q22) One goal (simple; steering stays meaningful). A routine of timed clauses (expresses a life; one message can run a body for a year). Small fixed routine: a few timed clauses plus a fallback. Open. Note this is also what makes an idle body feel alive.
Idle influence Idle bodies are scenery. Idle bodies are the audience and the patrons: presence counts as attention, and unspent UBI flows by default to what they attend or follow. Patrons. Solves idle-rich (decided must not happen) with the default spend already listed in 04.

4. Economy

Dial Range Lean
UBI rate Low (scarcity, building feels earned) ↔ high (abundance, fast frontier churn) Shelved. Tie to the materials price list, not a number chosen now.
Demurrage None (savers win, idle-rich) ↔ steep (use it or lose it) Mild, or replaced entirely by the idle default spend. Shelved.
Inertia (base × age × attention) Flat (everything stays editable; no famous center) ↔ steep (the center freezes early) Keep the shape (decided that the center hardens). Coefficients shelved. Keep inertia per piece so things glued to a famous structure don't borrow its fame.
Harberger premium Off ↔ required for protection Optional premium on pieces only. Q14b still open; shelved until people are actually building.
Manna on-chain In-game ledger ↔ ERC-20 In-game first (04). Shelved; a bridge can come later.
Treasury split — Shelved until there is revenue.

5. Space

Dial Range Lean
Ground growth Fixed ring per day ↔ keeps pace with population density Density-driven. Works with any birth curve. Shelved number.
Starting size ~100 × 100 m (decided). —
Verticality Ground only ↔ free building up Ground first (decided); when height opens is a rules event. Shelved.
Interior size (TARDIS rooms) Exterior = interior ↔ unlimited Open (Q10).
Aggregates (buildings, groups) Declared objects in state ↔ edge names only Derived, not declared: a structure = a support-connected component; a group = bodies whose intents share a target. Open (Claude, round 8a; Gene hasn't answered).
Intent time Timeless intents ↔ recurring (daily, lunar) A recurrence slot on the intent, shared with the ritual's own schedule. Open.

6. Simulation and engine

Dial Range Lean
Tick rate of the always-on world Seconds ↔ minutes (decided range) A rule, not a promise; slow under load (EVE-style time dilation).
Steering rate limit Arrival order ↔ per-body limit ↔ price (Q26) Per-body limit. Open.
Nouns intake Curated ↔ anyone can ingest Anyone, validated affordances + hard floor (05).
Verb additions Frequent ↔ rare and ceremonial Rare. A candidate for "the day X was invented" at the manna fall.

Which to decide now vs. later

Decide early because they change the shape: arrival pricing form (§1), what the daily ritual is (§2), flat steering (§3), idle influence (§3), derived aggregates and intent time (§5).

Let the early community settle: every number in the tables, the cap, the treasury split, demurrage, Harberger's scope, verticality timing.
