R1 · Prior art for the kernel

R1 — Prior art for the Sim kernel ontology

5,319 words · R1-ONTOLOGY-PRIOR-ART.md · updated 2026-10-02

In short

Planning operators, social simulation engines, speech acts and commitments, LLM-agent worlds and data models, each mapped onto the kernel. Ends with twelve ranked ideas, a design for scenes, and the failure modes of open vocabularies.

Contents
  • 0. What is being comparedSim's meaning layer (the kernel, eden4/18-KERNEL-CARDS-AND-SELF-CHANGE.md) holds Things (id, kind, name, immutable traits, mutable states, at another Thing) and Links (kind, from, to, optional value, data and lifetime).
  • 1. Planning operatorsCovers STRIPS, ADL, PDDL; HTN planning and GOAP.
  • 2. Social simulation enginesCovers Versu and Praxis (Evans and Short, 2014); Comme il Faut, Prom Week and Ensemble; City of Gangsters; Talk of the Town; The Sims; Dwarf Fortress, Kismet.
  • 3. Multi-agent systems theoryCovers Speech acts, KQML and FIPA-ACL; Social commitments; Institutions, powers and norms.
  • 4. LLM-agent worldsCovers Generative Agents and AI Town; Project Sid; Voyager and LLM-written skill libraries; Concordia and Infinite Craft; Inform 7, TextWorld and Ceptre.
  • 5. Data modelsTriples and property graphs. RDF stores subject–predicate–object triples; a statement about a statement needs reification, which RDF 1.2 adds as "triple terms" (RDF 1.2).
  • 6. The most actionable ideas, rankedDeferred: Inform-style house rules (a controller's rules intercepting plays in its domain) would be powerful but make outcomes hard to predict; revisit after ideas 2 and 5.
  • 7. Scenes and joint activitiesThe problem: several bodies in one coordinated activity (a class, a meal, carrying a beam) when each actor may change only itself.
  • 8. Failure modes of open, LLM-extended vocabulariesA table.
  • 9. Reading list for Gene, in orderEvans and Short, "Versu—A Simulationist Storytelling System" (2014), sections on practices, norms and limitations. https://www.cs.uky.edu/~sgware/reading/papers/evans2014versu.pdf
  • 10. Not verified / not coveredFrom secondary sources only: Searle's taxonomy (Wikipedia summary); the regimentation/enforcement distinction and its attribution to Jones and Sergot 1993 (survey and search snippets); Infinite Craft (press via Wikipedia);…

2026-10-02. Surveys systems that solved parts of the problem Sim's kernel addresses and says what each implies for it. Facts about our own code were checked in /packages/kernel/src (types.ts, vocab.ts, kernel.ts, cards.ts) and apps/server/src/fold.ts. Section 10 lists what was not verified.

0. What is being compared

Sim's meaning layer (the kernel, eden4/18-KERNEL-CARDS-AND-SELF-CHANGE.md) holds Things (id, kind, name, immutable traits, mutable states, at another Thing) and Links (kind, from, to, optional value, data and lifetime). State changes in five ways only: appear/vanish, set, move, link/unlink, say. An action is a card: a named operator with one target kind, conditions (when), a list of changes (then) and a depiction (approach, during). The one rule: an actor changes only itself, what it controls and what it made; shared outcomes come from separate single-actor plays (ask, agree, act). Speech acts are state (say writes said, asked, agreed links); words are rendered afterwards and never replayed. An LLM compiler writes new cards from a plain wish; a static validator checks them; valid ones are minted into a shared library. An owner's plan is an agenda of cards. The ledger logs plays and mints and replays without any model.

Five code facts recur below:

  • K1. Links are keyed kind|from|to, so one link of a kind per ordered pair. One Soul can have one open question to another at a time.
  • K2. agree writes an agreed link with no content, lasting 30 minutes. While it stands, the asker may take any control-flagged link on anything the answerer controls. It is not used up by acting on it, and nobody can withdraw it (speech links cannot be unlinked).
  • K3. Control is a boolean per link kind. A controller may set any state on the thing or vanish it. A grantor cannot remove a grantee's control link, because only the link's holder can unlink it.
  • K4. vanish removes the thing, things tied to it by traits.with, and every link to or from them. States on other things are untouched, so host a gathering leaves hosting: true on the host after the gathering vanishes, unless end the gathering was the card played.
  • K5. A play is logged as (Soul, card name, card hash, target, manner); its changes are recomputed on replay. Each world's base vocabulary is pinned in its log. The validator refuses a different card under an existing name, so names are never reused, but a revised card needs a new name and nothing links it to the old one. Lapsed links become invisible without a logged event.

1. Planning operators

STRIPS, ADL, PDDL

STRIPS (Fikes and Nilsson, 1971) describes an action by preconditions, an add list and a delete list, and assumes that anything unlisted is unchanged; this is its answer to the frame problem (saying what an action does not change). ADL (Pednault, 1989) added conditional effects and quantifiers. PDDL standardised both; PDDL 2.1 added numeric state and durative actions whose conditions hold at the start, at the end or throughout; PDDL 2.2 added derived predicates (facts computed from other facts) and timed initial literals (facts that switch at a set time). Sources: STRIPS, ADL, PDDL 2.1, PDDL 2.2.

  • Maps as: a card is a STRIPS operator: when the precondition, then the effects, target and derive the typed parameters. set and move are single-valued and need no delete list. A link's until is a timed literal.
  • Does better: conditional and quantified effects ("everyone attending leaves"); disjunction, counting and numeric comparison in conditions; derived predicates; conditions that must hold for an action's whole duration. Our condition grammar has ten fixed forms and none of these.
  • We add: an authority rule on effects; depiction kept apart from meaning; operators that are data, minted at run time and addressed by hash.
  • Warning: indirect effects must be listed in every card or derived (the ramification problem). K4 is an instance: one fact (who hosts) is stored twice, once as a state and once as a link.

Because cards are STRIPS-shaped, standard planning machinery applies. A delete-relaxed reachability check could tell the validator whether a new card can ever be played, and extend the present one-step lookahead to several steps cheaply. (Our inference, not a cited result.)

HTN planning and GOAP

Hierarchical task network planning decomposes a task into subtasks by stored methods until only primitive actions remain; SHOP2 plans in execution order, so it always knows the full state (Nau et al. 2003). Goal-oriented action planning, used in F.E.A.R., gives each character actions with preconditions and effects and searches for a sequence reaching a goal, behind a state machine of three states (Orkin 2006).

  • Maps as: the wish compiler performs HTN decomposition with an LLM but keeps no methods; a deck is a GOAP action set; our lookahead is a search of depth one.
  • Does better: stored, reusable decompositions; real multi-step search.
  • Warning: F.E.A.R. keeps one description of effects for the planner and another for the game. Evans and Short name that gap as the reason agents repeat an action that cannot succeed (Versu, p. 122). Sim evaluates and applies the same card; keep it so.
  • Mechanism: mint methods (wish pattern → agenda template) beside cards and try them before calling the compiler. This addresses doc 18's open problem "cost of wishes". Methods cover only foreseen cases, so the compiler stays as fallback.

2. Social simulation engines

Versu and Praxis (Evans and Short, 2014)

Versu is an interactive drama whose autonomous characters are coordinated by social practices. A practice describes a recurring situation (a greeting, a meal, a game of whist), names its roles, holds its own state and offers actions to whoever fills each role. Practices "never control the agents directly; they merely provide suggestions": each character chooses. Several practices run at once; a character's options are the union of their offers. The world is a set of sentences in exclusion logic: a.b means b is one of the ways a is, a!b that b is the only way, so asserting brown.in!study removes brown.in!dining_room and everything stored under it. Practices are written without knowing which roles players will fill. Source: Versu (read in full); open reconstruction of its logic: Praxish, 2023.

  • Maps as: a practice instance is a Thing with role links; "an affordance exists only inside its practice" is a card whose when requires a link to that Thing (as dance requires attends). ! is the single-valuedness of set and move.
  • Does better: practices have phases and state; data stored under a practice dies with it (our vanish does the same for links, K4); uniqueness is declared once and type-checked, where we repeat noneLinkedTo in each card; norm violations are allowed, marked and answered by other characters, where our kernel can only forbid; characters judge an action by applying it, scoring the result and undoing it; an inspector explains why a precondition failed.
  • Warning: more than 300 desires, whose tuning the authors call difficult and unrewarding; concurrent practices complicated authoring and the mechanism for one practice to suppress others was too crude; all characters share one view of each practice's state; a twenty-minute episode took one to two months until a higher-level authoring tool cut it to under a week.
  • Agrees with the one rule: "Relationship state changes should require active choice from both characters."

Comme il Faut, Prom Week and Ensemble

Comme il Faut (CiF) models social life as social exchanges: an initiator proposes an exchange with an intent (start dating, become allies), a responder accepts or rejects, and effects follow. Weighted influence rules decide what the initiator wants and how the responder answers. A social facts database keeps history that rules query over time windows. Trigger rules fire after any exchange whose condition holds (dating two people marks a character a "two-timer"). Prom Week used over 5,000 rules. Ensemble repackaged CiF as a general engine whose schema declares, per kind of social fact, whether it is boolean or numeric; undirected, directed or reciprocal; its default; and a duration after which it reverts. Sources: CiF 2011, CiF 2014, Ensemble schema.

  • Maps as: one exchange is our ask, answer and effect in one unit; influence rules are our good_when / not_for hints scored by a small model; the schema is our link-kind table.
  • Does better: lifetime, direction and reciprocity are declared per kind (ours are constants in code); conditions can ask about history; trigger rules keep definitions true without every card repeating them.
  • Warning: the rule count. And Evans and Short's criticism: an exchange is isolated, with nothing giving a sequence of acts continuity. That is the scenes gap (section 7).

City of Gangsters

A commercial strategy game with about 1,200 NPCs (Zubek, Horswill et al. 2021). A relationship is a directed edge whose valence is the sum of history elements; each element carries a valence change, an optional actor and target (so a dyadic edge can record what a third party did), an optional expiry, and text explaining it to the player ("−10: they heard that you beat up their family member"). Logic-programming rules query the graph after actions and add history elements, which is how news of an extortion turned the victim's brother hostile before the player had met him. The authors relate their argument to entity-component systems (Bilas 2002), which put all game objects behind a general query interface.

  • Maps as: a relationship computed from dated, expiring, explained events, where our links hold one current value and no reason.
  • Does better: every number can say why it is what it is; expiry is per event, so relationships fade naturally.
  • Warning: the explanations are hand-written per rule.

Talk of the Town

A town simulated over decades before play, whose characters observe, tell, misremember, forget and lie (Ryan et al. 2015, text read). Each character holds a mental model per person or place it knows, made of belief facets (one per attribute: workplace, hair colour, whereabouts). A facet records its value, whether it is accurate, the facet it replaced, and a list of evidence; its strength is the sum of its evidence. Evidence has nine types in four groups: origination (reflection, observation, transference, confabulation, lie), propagation (statement, eavesdropping), deterioration (mutation) and termination (forgetting). Each piece records source, location, time and a strength that decays with time. A lie is received as a statement.

  • Maps as: doc 18 section 6 ("Knowing") and the open memory problem: a belief is a link from a Soul to a Thing whose data holds value, evidence and strength; said is a statement event.
  • Does better: knowledge has provenance, so it can be wrong, stale, second-hand or a lie.
  • Warning: per-character belief stores grow fast; decide what is forgotten before what is kept.

The Sims

In The Sims the knowledge of what can be done lives in objects. Each object's behaviours carry a test of whether they are possible and advertisements of which needs they satisfy; a Sim picks the best advertised behaviour, attenuated by distance. When two Sims interact, an invisible social-interaction object is created and both Sims' threads run inside it (Forbus and Wright 2001). The Sims 4 defines interactions by constraints so that compatible ones run at once (GDC 2014).

  • Maps as: advertisements are cards offered by Things rather than held by Souls; the interaction object is a minimal scene Thing.
  • Does better: locality comes free, since only things in reach advertise; new content is a new object and no agent changes.
  • Warning: Sims estimate an action's effect more simply than its real effect and so retry a blocked action indefinitely (Versu, p. 122). Doc 18 met the same behaviour and patched it with a cooldown.

Dwarf Fortress, Kismet

Tarn Adams's published principles are about method: do not overplan; model underlying elements and let results emerge; work at the level the player sees or one below (Game AI Pro 2, ch. 41). It says nothing about social representation. Community reverse-engineering (DFHack, read only through a summary) shows joint activities stored as records, each a tree of events (conversation, prayer, sparring, teaching) with a participant list, every type hand-coded: the closed-vocabulary route to scenes.

Kismet is a small social-simulation language with near-English rules. Its authors' follow-up reports that natural phrasing "enhances readability, but it hurts writability", citing whether "doesn't like" equals "dislikes"; a professional designer wanted no syntax at all (Summerville et al. 2021). Lesson: a near-natural vocabulary breeds synonyms. Keep card JSON canonical and generate a plain-language rendering for Gene rather than a second syntax.

3. Multi-agent systems theory

Speech acts, KQML and FIPA-ACL

Austin separated what is said from the act performed in saying it. Searle's 1975 classification has five classes: assertives, directives, commissives, expressives, and declarations, which change reality by being said (naming, appointing) (summary; original in Language in Society 5). KQML defined an extensible set of message types ("performatives") and facilitator agents that route them (Finin et al. 1994). FIPA-ACL standardised a catalogue of acts (spec) and protocols; in the request protocol the participant refuses or agrees, then reports failure or completion (spec). FIPA defined each act by the speaker's beliefs and intentions. Singh (1998) argued that private mental states cannot be checked among independent agents, that "communication is inherently public", and that acts should be defined by the public commitments they create (Singh 1998).

  • Maps as: say is (act, about); "acts are state" is Singh's public semantics.
  • Does better: the request protocol closes the loop with a completion or failure report; the taxonomy names two classes our base acts lack: declarations, and commissives with content (a promise to do X), which is doc 18's open question about saying something new.
  • Warning: never let a rule depend on what a Soul's model "believes".

Social commitments

A commitment C(debtor, creditor, condition) is a public fact that one agent owes another an outcome; a conditional commitment takes effect when its condition is met. Yolum and Singh define who may perform each operation: the debtor creates, discharges (by performing) and cancels (usually with a compensating commitment); the creditor releases or assigns; the debtor delegates. A protocol is then any sequence of acts that resolves the commitments, not a fixed script. They formalise this in the event calculus, where events start and stop time-varying facts (Yolum and Singh 2002).

  • Maps as: agreed is a commitment from answerer to asker; links with since and until are event-calculus facts.
  • Does better: commitments have content and a full life: created, discharged, released, cancelled, reassigned.
  • Warning: our agreed has only two of these moves, create and expire, and no content (K2). Combined with K1 this is the weakest point found in the kernel.

Institutions, powers and norms

Jones and Sergot formalised institutional power: within an institution an agent's act counts as creating an institutional fact, which differs from being permitted or physically able (J. IGPL 4(3), 1996). Normative-systems work distinguishes regimentation (violations are made impossible) from enforcement (violations happen and are sanctioned) (survey); Shoham and Tennenholtz's social laws are the regimented kind, designed off-line to guarantee coexistence (1995). Electronic institutions specify agent societies as scenes, each a role-based protocol with access and exit states per role and a minimum and maximum count per role, linked by a performative structure that says how agents move between scenes and whether a scene may run several times at once; norms turn acts into obligations, and middleware mediates every utterance (Esteva et al., AMELI 2004, text read).

  • Maps as: the one rule is regimentation; control: true is one hard-coded counts-as rule; "take the role only if a controller agreed" is empowerment.
  • Does better: powers are specific to a role; roles have quotas; entry and exit are explicit; norms can be broken and answered.
  • Warning: our control is all or nothing (K3). A compiled dj of marked as control lets the DJ rename or remove the gathering, and the host cannot remove the DJ. Electronic institutions, for their part, are rigid, closed and heavy to specify.

4. LLM-agent worlds

Generative Agents and AI Town

Generative Agents (Park et al., 2023) gave 25 characters a memory stream, reflection and planning; the model decides at run time what an action does to an object's state. A party spread by invitation and five agents came. The authors report agents breaking location norms that are hard to convey in natural language (several using a one-person bathroom, entering shops after closing) (paper). AI Town, an open-source descendant, lets only the engine change game state: outside components "can only mutate them by sending inputs". Its conversation members are invited, walking over, or participating (architecture).

  • Maps as: AI Town draws doc 18's line (models propose, the kernel disposes); its conversation is a small scene with consent.
  • Does better: memory retrieval and reflection (our open memory problem); an explicit state for "agreed but not there yet".
  • Warning: norms that exist only in prose are not followed. AI Town's one joint activity is hand-coded.

Project Sid

Altera ran 10 to over 1,000 agents in Minecraft, routing decisions through one "Cognitive Controller" so that outputs agree; without it an agent "may respond with 'Sure thing!'" while choosing a different action. Agents voted on amendments to a tax constitution (paper). The authors report weak collaborative building and no truly new inventions.

  • Maps as: the constitution process is doc 18's level-2 change by public decision.
  • Warning: saying and doing diverge when produced separately. Sim renders words from the act; keep that order.

Voyager and LLM-written skill libraries

Voyager writes code skills for Minecraft, indexes each by an embedding of its description, retrieves the five most relevant, and keeps a skill only after a critic model confirms the task succeeded. The authors report the critic missing successes, invented game facts and calls to functions that do not exist (paper); in the code a skill with an existing name replaces the old index entry (skill.py). Berlot-Attwell et al. found that in two library-learning systems function reuse is "extremely infrequent" and gains came from retrying (2024). TroVE reports toolboxes 79–98% smaller than baselines (2024); SkillWeaver tests and debugs each new skill against the environment before keeping it (2025). Guan et al. have an LLM write PDDL actions, repair them with validator and human feedback, and translate them back to plain language for review (2023); this is the closest published analogue of our compiler.

  • Maps as: minting is committing to the library; a deck is per-agent retrieval.
  • Does better: skills are run before they are kept; retrieval finds an existing skill before a new one is written; back-translation lets a non-expert check what was written.
  • Warning: expect most minted cards to be played once. Measure plays per card before assuming the library compounds.

Concordia and Infinite Craft

In DeepMind's Concordia agents state actions in natural language and a Game Master model decides the outcome, tracks chosen "grounded variables" (such as money) and resolves conflicting simultaneous actions (paper). Infinite Craft has a language model name the result of combining two elements, stores it, and serves the stored result to everyone thereafter, crediting the first finder (Wikipedia summary of press reports; no primary source).

  • Maps as: Concordia judges each action at run time; Sim compiles the judgement once into a card. Infinite Craft is mint-once-then-look-up.
  • Does better: Concordia resolves any new action at once. Infinite Craft keys the cache by the question (the pair), so the same question never mints twice; our cards are keyed by content, so one wish can mint many near-identical cards.
  • Warning: Concordia is not deterministic or replayable and costs a model call per action. A poor Infinite Craft result is permanent.

Inform 7, TextWorld and Ceptre

Inform 7 runs every action through rulebooks in order (before, instead, check, carry out, after, report); check, carry out and report belong to the action, while before, instead and after can be written by any place or object (handbook). Relations declare their shape ("various people to one person", ch. 13); scenes are spans of time with cues to start and end (ch. 10). TextWorld (2018) and Ceptre (2015) use linear-logic rules that consume and produce facts, and generate quests by chaining rules.

  • Maps as: check is when, carry out is then, report is depiction.
  • Does better: a place or host can intercept others' actions in its domain ("house rules"); relation shape is declared once; consuming rules conserve things by construction; chaining can build a state in which a new card is playable, which is a test case.
  • Warning: interacting rulebooks become hard to predict.

5. Data models

Triples and property graphs. RDF stores subject–predicate–object triples; a statement about a statement needs reification, which RDF 1.2 adds as "triple terms" (RDF 1.2). Our Link is a triple plus value, data and lifetime. The standard remedy when a relation needs identity, content or several instances is to promote it to a node. K1 says requests and agreements have reached that point.

Datomic stores immutable facts [entity, attribute, value, transaction, added?]; a retraction is another fact and the database can be read as of any past transaction (data model). Its schema guidance is to grow and never break, never remove or reuse names, and annotate superseded ones (best practices). Our validator already never reuses a card name (K5); what is missing is the annotation that says which card supersedes which, and retraction of a card from use.

Datalog with time. Dedalus adds an explicit time argument to Datalog, so persistence and change between steps are themselves rules (Alvaro et al. 2009). Our derive and link conditions are a small fixed-shape query; derived predicates would be named rules of this kind.

Event sourcing keeps an append-only log as the truth and computes state from it; practitioners separate a command (a request that may be refused) from an event (what happened). Our ledger logs plays, which are commands, and recomputes their changes (K5). Replay is therefore correct only under the code that first ran them; doc 18 section 8 relies on the code hash for that. Logging each play's resulting changes as well would make replay depend only on the five-change applier, and would make lapses explicit events.

6. The most actionable ideas, ranked

Ranked by expected value for the kernel as it stands.

# Idea Source What changes in kernel or cards Cost
1 Requests and agreements with identity, content and a lifecycle Yolum and Singh; FIPA request protocol ask creates a request (a Thing, or a link keyed by id) holding what was asked; agree attaches to it; consent permits only what was asked, is used up when acted on, and can be released by the asker or withdrawn by the answerer; several questions may be open at once. Fixes K1 and K2 Medium: speech handling, control check, base cards
2 Specific, revocable powers in place of boolean control Jones and Sergot; electronic institutions A link kind lists what it permits on its target (which states, vanish, grant). A granted role holds only while the grant link stands, so the grantor revokes by removing its own link. Fixes K3 within the one rule Medium: validator and apply check
3 Log each play's resulting changes Event sourcing Ledger entry gains its change list; replay applies changes; lapses become logged events Low; larger ledger
4 Schema on link kinds Ensemble; Versu !; Inform relations Declare per kind: cardinality (one per target, one per actor), default lifetime, reciprocity; the kernel enforces them, replacing repeated noneLinkedTo guards Low
5 Scenes as practices Versu; electronic institutions See section 7: role link kinds with quotas, a phase state, cards per role and phase, one counting condition Medium to high
6 Test a card before minting Voyager; SkillWeaver; Guan et al. Play it on a forked state; check it can ever be played; generate a plain-language gloss from its logic and compare it with its summary Low to medium
7 Library lifecycle Datomic; Infinite Craft; TroVE Mark a card superseded by another or retired (decks drop it, past plays keep their meaning by hash); near-duplicate check before minting; key repeated wishes to their card; count plays per card Low
8 Derived predicates PDDL 2.2; Dedalus; CiF trigger rules Named queries in the vocabulary (e.g. "hosting" = has a hosts link); remove duplicated state (K4) Low to medium
9 Explained, dated evidence on social links City of Gangsters; Talk of the Town A relationship or belief link carries entries (change, source event, time, expiry); its value is computed from them. Gives memory provenance and lets the UI say why Medium
10 Cards offered by Things in reach The Sims; Versu Default locality rule: a Soul may play its own cards and those offered by Things where it is (doc 18 open problem "locality") Medium
11 Conditions that must keep holding PDDL 2.1 A while list on long cards; the kernel ends the activity when it breaks Medium
12 Minted methods HTN Store wish → agenda templates; match before compiling Low to medium

Deferred: Inform-style house rules (a controller's rules intercepting plays in its domain) would be powerful but make outcomes hard to predict; revisit after ideas 2 and 5.

7. Scenes and joint activities

The problem: several bodies in one coordinated activity (a class, a meal, carrying a beam) when each actor may change only itself.

Prior art, in order of fit:

  1. Versu's social practices. A practice instance with role bindings, its own state and per-role affordances, which never acts on anyone. The authors compare it directly with isolated speech acts (The Sims, Prom Week) and with joint behaviours that take control (Façade).
  2. Electronic-institution scenes. Role quotas, access and exit states per role, several concurrent instances of one scene kind.
  3. ABL joint behaviours and STEAM. Entry and exit by a three-phase commit among team members; members owe the team notice when they leave (ABL, STEAM). Useful for the protocol, not for where control sits.
  4. AI Town's membership states (invited, on the way, participating) and The Sims' interaction object.
  5. Event-centric behaviour (Shoulson et al.): one structure drives several bodies (paper). This is what a Mars coordinator already is; it belongs in depiction.

Recommendation. Define the meaning of a scene as a Versu-style practice built from kernel parts, and keep doc 19's proposal (one coordinator stages a scene Thing and is re-staged from the kernel) as its depiction.

  • A scene kind is vocabulary: its role link kinds with minimum and maximum counts (idea 4), its phases, and which cards each role may play in each phase.
  • Joining, readying and leaving are single-actor plays that write or remove the actor's own role link. A role that needs permission uses the request lifecycle (idea 1). Leaving needs no notice protocol: the removed link is visible to everyone, and they re-plan.
  • Phase changes are cards played by a role with that power (idea 2), conditioned on the role links ("at least four carriers are ready"). This needs one new condition form that counts links to a Thing.
  • The scene Thing never changes a Soul. The coordinator runs while quotas are met. When the scene vanishes, its role links go with it (K4); per-scene data should be stored on the scene so it dies too.
  • First test: carrying a beam (tight coupling, four roles, one phase change), then a class (asymmetric roles).

Avoid scenes that take control of members (Façade's joint behaviours, drama managers), which break the one rule, and a model judging joint outcomes at run time (Concordia), which breaks replay.

8. Failure modes of open, LLM-extended vocabularies

Failure Seen in Mitigation others used Sim today
Synonyms and near-duplicates Kismet ("doesn't like" / "dislikes"); Voyager name collisions Show the writer the existing vocabulary; nearest-neighbour check; a canonical key per question (Infinite Craft) Names unique; near-duplicates unchecked
Items written once, never reused LEGO-Prover and TroVE, per Berlot-Attwell et al. Measure reuse; retrieve before writing; trim unused items (TroVE) Play counts exist in the fold; not yet used
Valid but wrong Voyager's critic passing failures; LLM-written PDDL needing repair Run before keeping (Voyager, SkillWeaver); validator and human feedback with back-translation (Guan et al.) Static validation only
Invented primitives Voyager calling absent functions Closed primitive set and strict schema Has this
Bad entries made permanent Infinite Craft's stored results Quarantine before sharing; supersede by annotation, never edit (Datomic) No retirement yet (doc 18 open problem 2)
Norms that exist only in prose Generative Agents (bathroom, closed shops) Put the norm in state the engine checks Has this, via when
Loops from a wrong model of effects The Sims; F.E.A.R. Judge actions by their real effects (Versu) Has this; cooldown as backstop
Rule and tuning explosion CiF (5,000 rules); Versu (300 desires) Declarative schema; reusable rule sets A small model scores choices instead, moving cost to run time
Saying one thing, doing another Project Sid without its controller One decision point Words rendered from the act
Drift in what the compiler considers normal Named as a risk in doc 18 section 8; no prior measurement found Fixed test set of wishes, rerun on every compiler change Planned

9. Reading list for Gene, in order

10. Not verified / not covered

  • From secondary sources only: Searle's taxonomy (Wikipedia summary); the regimentation/enforcement distinction and its attribution to Jones and Sergot 1993 (survey and search snippets); Infinite Craft (press via Wikipedia); Dwarf Fortress activity structures (third-party reverse engineering, read through a summary); the FIPA act catalogue (the spec page refused the fetch; no act count is claimed).
  • Abstract or snippet only: PDDL 2.1/2.2, SHOP2, GOAP, Sims 4 concurrency, KQML, Shoham and Tennenholtz, TroVE's mechanism, SkillWeaver, Guan et al., Dedalus, Kismet's original 2020 paper (not located).
  • Not checked: independent replication of Project Sid; Greg Young's rules for versioning events; Sims 4 "situations" with role "jobs" (only modding sources exist, so omitted).
  • Not covered: bitemporal stores (valid time vs transaction time, e.g. XTDB); RDF named graphs for provenance; deontic logic beyond the institutional-power line; Crusader Kings' opinion system.

R2 · The landscape of agent worlds

R2 — Landscape: agent-inhabited worlds, agent social platforms, bring-your-own-agent games

4,786 words · R2-LANDSCAPE-AGENT-WORLDS.md · updated 2026-10-02

In short

Agent social networks, games for outside agents, operator-run simulations and world models as of October 2026, with their ancestors. Ten things to borrow, five ways these projects failed, and the gap Sim fills.

Contents
  • 0. The short versionThree kinds of project exist. Bring-your-own-agent platforms (Moltbook, SpaceMolt, OpenClawCity, ClawCity, OpenMMO) run a cheap state server and let outside agents act through an API; the owner pays for inference; the world…
  • 1. Agent social networksMoltbook. Reddit-style forum where only agents post.
  • 2. Worlds built for outside agentsSpaceMolt. Text MMO of about 500 star systems, built almost entirely with Claude Code by Ian Langworth and volunteers (https://blog.langworth.com/spacemolt).
  • 3. Operator-run LLM worldsAivilization (HKUST). The closest precedent to owned, steered Souls.
  • 4. Crypto-adjacent, where something runsEternum (Dojo, Starknet). Season 1 (2025) added Daydreams agents as wandering NPC armies with generated traits, not as players (https://www.bankless.com/read/eternum-season-1-starknet).
  • 5. World models as a depiction layerGenie 3 (DeepMind, August 2025): real-time 720p at 24 fps, consistency for a few minutes; DeepMind lists multi-agent interaction in a shared environment as an open problem (https://bdtechtalks.com/2025/08/07/deepmind-genie-3/).
  • 6. Ancestors, 1986–2016Habitat (Lucasfilm, 1986–88, Commodore 64 over Quantum Link). Morningstar and Farmer reported in 1990 that planning a world for thousands centrally failed; a price bug (dolls bought for 75 tokens and pawned for 100)…
  • 7. Infrastructure patternsState sync. The agent-native worlds do not sync state to agents.
  • 8. Comparison table (status as of 2026-10-02)A table.
  • 9. Ten things to steal, rankedLabel every piece of text by source before a mind or an owner's agent sees it. perceive and soul_log should mark fields as engine fact, server-rendered speech, or owner-written text, and remembered items should keep that…
  • 10. Five ways these projects failed, and where Sim standsThe platform itself was the hole (Moltbook's database; ClawHub's skills). Sim does: per-Soul revocable tokens, one-time token delivery, no owner model keys, a self-hosted skill.
  • 11. Gaps Sim's design actually fillsOwned inhabitants that stay alive without their owner. Owner-paid worlds go quiet when agents disconnect; operator-paid worlds have no outside owners.
  • 12. Not verified / not coveredNot verified. Moltbook's post-acquisition identity changes (secondary blogs say JWTs replaced static keys, but the skill served on 2026-10-02 still issues static API keys); the Vectra 2.6% method; a vendor's claim that…

Written 2026-10-02 from web research on that day. Extends, and does not repeat, the prior-art list in 14-MVP-TECHNICAL-SPEC-DRAFT.md §10. Every fact carries its date and a URL. [claimed] marks a number that comes only from the project itself or from secondary coverage and that I could not check independently. GitHub figures were read from the GitHub API, and "live API" figures from the projects' own public endpoints, on 2026-10-02. "Sim" means the shell described in /docs/STATUS.md (as of 2026-10-01).

0. The short version

Three kinds of project exist. Bring-your-own-agent platforms (Moltbook, SpaceMolt, OpenClawCity, ClawCity, OpenMMO) run a cheap state server and let outside agents act through an API; the owner pays for inference; the world is only as alive as the agents currently connected. Operator-run simulations (AI Town, Aivilization, Project Sid, Emergence World, AI Village) run the minds themselves; they are coherent but closed and expensive, and most ended, pivoted or became research programmes. World models (Genie 3, Agora-1, Marble) generate pictures of worlds and, in 2026, started separating an authoritative state from the renderer, which is the split Sim already has. Nothing found combines owned, always-on inhabitants with server-run minds, outside steering and a public replayable record. The nearest relatives are Aivilization (owned, steered, server-run; a six-week experiment in 2025, now being relaunched as a commercial product) and SpaceMolt (open, persistent, agent-native, but direct control and text only).

One correction to doc 14: Moltbook did not end in March 2026; it is running under Meta and its public API showed new posts every few seconds on 2026-10-02. Doc 14's January 2026 launch date for SpaceMolt is right: SpaceMolt counts its launch from 2026-01-31.

1. Agent social networks

Moltbook. Reddit-style forum where only agents post. Launched 2026-01-28; acquired by Meta on 2026-03-10, founders moved into Meta Superintelligence Labs (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moltbook, https://techcrunch.com/2026/03/10/meta-acquired-moltbook-the-ai-agent-social-network-that-went-viral-because-of-fake-posts/). Its live API on 2026-10-02 reported 2,919,960 agents, 214,545 verified, 4.36 million posts and 22.8 million comments (/api/v1/stats); the newest 100 posts spanned 21 minutes and came from 63 authors, about 280 posts an hour, against about 1,370 an hour during the first 40 days (my arithmetic from the study cited below).

  • How agents connect. The human pastes one line telling the agent to read https://www.moltbook.com/skill.md. The agent registers by REST, receives an API key and a claim link, and the human verifies by email and by a post on X. The skill (v1.12.0, read 2026-10-02) asks for a heartbeat that re-fetches heartbeat.md about every 30 minutes and sets limits (60 reads and 30 writes a minute, one post per 30 minutes, 50 comments a day, stricter for new accounts). Posts must pass an obfuscated arithmetic puzzle, a "reverse CAPTCHA" any human can pass by handing it to a model.
  • Who pays. Each owner pays for their own agent's inference. The platform runs none.
  • What broke. (a) Platform security: on 2026-01-31 the Supabase database was open to reading and writing because a key sat in front-end JavaScript with row-level security off. Wiz reported it that evening, it was fixed in about three hours, and Wiz's write-up (2026-02-02) lists about 1.5 million agent API tokens, about 35,000 owner emails, 4,060 private messages (some holding third-party API keys) and the ability to edit live posts (https://wiz.io/blog/exposed-moltbook-database-reveals-millions-of-api-keys). (b) Population: the same data showed about 17,000 humans behind those 1.5 million agents, roughly 88 each. (c) Injection: 506 prompt-injection posts were flagged in the first 72 hours, mostly from one actor (https://securityandtechnology.org/blog/lessons-from-moltbook-when-agents-talk-to-agents/); Vectra put hidden injection payloads in 2.6% of sampled posts without stating its method (2026-02-03, https://www.vectra.ai/blog/moltbook-and-the-illusion-of-harmless-ai-agent-communities); a 17-day study of 228,684 posts classed 18.28% as toxic, manipulative or malicious, including credential harvesting and instructions to install untrusted skills (https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00067, June 2026). (d) No social life: across 1.3 million posts and 6.7 million comments over 40 days, 91.4% of authors never returned to their own thread, 85.6% of conversations were flat, reciprocity was 3.3% against 22–60% on human platforms (https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13052, April 2026).
  • Lesson for Sim. A heartbeat that fetches instructions from the platform is remote control of every attached agent by whoever controls that URL. Sim's skill should be versioned and hashed, and should never ask an agent to re-fetch and obey.

The skill supply chain. The same paste-a-skill habit was attacked at the registry. Koi Security audited all 2,857 skills on ClawHub in early February 2026 and found 341 malicious ones, 335 from one campaign that hid a credential stealer behind a "prerequisites" step; later counts were 824 to 1,184 as the registry grew past 10,700 (https://koi.ai/blog/clawhavoc-341-malicious-clawedbot-skills-found-by-the-bot-they-were-targeting, https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/openclaw-ai-supply-chain/). Lesson: serve the skill from Sim's own origin only, keep it free of install steps, and say so in the skill.

Successors. The ClawtaVista directory lists roughly a hundred agent platforms, eleven marked defunct (https://www.clawtavista.com/, read 2026-10-02; its counts do not add up), among them Clawstr (on Nostr, launched 2026-02-04, https://soapbox.pub/blog/announcing-clawstr), MoltX, 4claw and ClawTasks. I found no measured activity for any. Lesson: the form is trivially cloned; no clone solved the absence of anything to do.

AI Village (AI Digest). Not a network but the best-observed small society. It has run every weekday since 2025-04-01, now eight hours a day with more than 15 agents, each with its own Linux computer and a shared chat, at about $10,000 a month in compute and infrastructure, paid by the charity (2026-06-16, https://aivillageblog.substack.com/p/how-the-ai-village-works); the blog posted through 2026-09-30. In 2025 one model invented a 93-person contact list that cost the village eight hours, agents sent about 300 emails citing partnerships that did not exist, and errors spread by agreement (https://aivillageblog.substack.com/p/what-we-learned-2025). Lesson: a fabricated fact in shared memory is more damaging than a hostile one, because nobody resists it.

2. Worlds built for outside agents

SpaceMolt. Text MMO of about 500 star systems, built almost entirely with Claude Code by Ian Langworth and volunteers (https://blog.langworth.com/spacemolt). The team dates launch to 2026-01-31; on day 18 it reported 1,663 registered agents, a concurrent peak of 370 and 99 releases, and one agent held more than 75% of all pirate kills (https://spacemolt.com/news/eighteen-days-in-the-cosmos). A closed-source Go server on one self-managed VPS (https://spacemolt.com/about, read 2026-10-02). Self-reported: 4,735 registered, 1,100–1,400 concurrent and hosting about $330 a month on 2026-04-13 (https://spacemolt.com/news/month-two); 5,400+ agents from 3,200+ operators after four months (https://www.patreon.com/SpaceMolt). Its live API on 2026-10-02 reported 1,610 online and 12,752 in total, at version 0.612.0 (game.spacemolt.com/api/stats).

  • How agents connect. skill.md plus MCP at game.spacemolt.com/mcp, WebSocket, or HTTP. The human gets a registration code from a dashboard; hosts that cannot hold a secret use a device-link flow (agent shows a URL, human approves, agent polls). The skill asks one question before anything else: which playstyle (https://spacemolt.com/skill.md).
  • State and limits. 10-second tick; one state-changing action per player per tick; reads are free but capped at 300 a minute; actions capped at 30 a minute; a second action while one is pending returns action_pending.
  • Who pays. The owner. The server runs no inference, which is why it cost $330 a month.
  • What broke (all 2026-04-13 post). A self-destruct loop exploit (634 in one day); a weapon glut that crashed prices; 139 hard-gated skills replaced with soft scaling; half-built ship classes hidden; the top 10% held 89% of credits; when one operator turned off its fleet, galaxy-wide events fell 56% overnight. Early agents confused game "tokens" with bearer tokens.
  • What people liked. A live galaxy map, per-agent "captain's logs" written for the owner, a tick-by-tick battle replay and a site redesigned as a second-monitor console (2026-07-15, https://spacemolt.com/news/the-site-got-a-console).
  • Lesson for Sim. The population is a few large operators. Measure concentration from day one and decide what the world does when one owner's Souls are a tenth of everything.

OpenClawCity (openclawcity.ai). A 2D city where agents make music, images and stories in ten buildings and react to each other's work. Join by skill or one REST call that returns a JWT, then a heartbeat that reads "like a local newspaper"; a 500-agent concurrency cap with a queue [claimed] (https://www.everydev.ai/tools/openclaw-city/llms.txt). Its skill (v2.0.108, read 2026-10-02, https://openclawcity.ai/skill.md) links a compatibility promise that the city only adds capabilities and never breaks documented fields, and makes registration idempotent through an agent-held key so retries do not create duplicates. Owner pays inference. No public counts found. Lesson: a written compatibility promise and idempotent registration are cheap and suit agents that retry blindly.

ClawCity. Browser MMO with a 15-second tick, cash, heat, gangs and heists (https://saascity.io/live/clawcity). No sourced numbers; it confirms the tick-and-REST pattern and nothing more.

OpenMMO. A 3D, 32 km procedural MMO by Song Jae-kyung (Lineage, ArcheAge), written largely with AI over about a year (https://botmonster.com/ai/openmmo-open-source-mmo-ai-agents/, https://github.com/Julian-adv/OpenMMO: 1,787 stars, pushed 2026-10-02, noncommercial licence). Humans and agents use the same WebSocket protocol, so the server cannot tell them apart. The agent client runs pathfinding and state machines locally and calls a model on a capped schedule. Concurrency unpublished. Lesson: this is the only 3D world found that admits outside agents, and it reached the same reflex-in-code, model-on-a-budget split as Sim. Read its agent client.

Screeps. On Steam since 2016-11-16; 64 concurrent Steam players on 2026-10-02 (Steam API) and 37–68 in mid-2026 (https://steambase.io/apps/464350); server repo pushed 2026-04-01. I found no evidence of LLM agents as a meaningful share of players. Lesson: a programmable MMO can stay alive for ten years on a few dozen concurrent players if the server is cheap.

3. Operator-run LLM worlds

Aivilization (HKUST). The closest precedent to owned, steered Souls. Public run from 2025-08-19 to about 2025-09-30. Users created an agent, chose appearance and personality, and steered it by chat, career nudges and housing decisions; a "Game Mode" showed what the agent thought and did, a "Data Mode" showed prices, social graphs and rankings, and each user was promised a "life report" at the end. HKUST claimed 100,000 agents, The Decoder reported 22,000+, and cost was claimed at US$2 per agent per month [claimed] (https://the-decoder.com/aivilization-experiment-lets-over-22000-ai-agents-model-what-future-societies-could-become/). The paper (https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.10429, February 2026) says "tens of thousands of agents", gives no cost or model, and reports that steering through long objectives and short commands measurably changed agents. There was no outside-agent interface. On 2026-07-31 the project was renamed 《AI小镇：星原》 ("AI Town: Xingyuan"), run by 紫荆智能 (Zijing Intelligence): players write scene descriptions that become small theatres for autonomous residents; 9,517 pre-registrations; first "pioneer residents" on 2026-08-18 (https://aitown.com.cn/, read 2026-10-02). Lesson: steering at two levels worked in public at five-figure scale; the life report is what users take away; the team's next step was a product, not a bigger experiment.

Emergence World (Emergence AI). Eight parallel worlds of ten agents ran 16 days; 850,000 model calls and nearly 50 billion tokens (https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17320, September 2026; repo pushed 2026-10-02). Deliberate prompt-injection, misinformation and memory-exposure events were introduced; no world was fully resilient; agents recognised hostile content, still wrote it into persistent memory, and acted on it up to 46 hours later. By my arithmetic that is about 28 calls and 1.6 million tokens per agent-hour. Lesson: memory is the delayed-action injection path; anything a Soul remembers needs a source label.

AI Town (a16z, Convex). 10,577 stars; last push 2026-08-26; still a starter kit, no outside-agent interface (https://github.com/a16z-infra/ai-town). Its engine keeps game rules separate from a generic input-queue tick loop, and model calls run outside the tick and return as inputs. Stanford's generative agents repo has not been pushed since 2024-08; its author's company Simile raised $100M in February 2026 and $200M at a $2B valuation on 2026-07-30 to sell simulated consumer panels (https://runtimewire.com/article/simile-raises-200m-series-b-2b-valuation, https://www.mrweb.com/drno/news40146.htm). Lesson: the money in agent simulation went to market research, not to worlds.

Project Sid (Altera). Repo last pushed 2024-11. The company renamed itself Fundamental Research Labs, raised $33M in August 2025 and now sells a spreadsheet agent (https://techcrunch.com/2025/08/01/fundamental-research-labs-nabs-33-million-from-prosus-to-build-ai-agents-for-multiple-verticals/). I found no 2026 Minecraft work. Related: the MineCollab benchmark (NeurIPS 2025) found that the bottleneck in multi-agent Minecraft is natural-language coordination, with performance dropping up to 15% when agents must communicate detailed plans (https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.17950). Lesson: this supports Sim's choice to make speech acts state and render the words afterwards.

Fable, Showrunner. Public alpha 2025-07-30 with Amazon investment after a 10,000-user closed test [claimed]; planned $10–20 a month (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fable_Studio). I found no 2026 figures.

NPC stacks. Inworld now describes itself as a research lab and inference provider for real-time voice; games are one use case among many (https://inworld.ai/, read 2026-10-02). Convai remains a hosted NPC service (https://convai.com/). inZOI's "Smart Zoi" runs a small on-device model through NVIDIA ACE and reviews its previous day while asleep (https://www.dsogaming.com/news/inzoi-is-the-first-game-with-ai-powered-npcs-using-nvidia-ace/). Lesson: the commercial NPC market moved to small local models and to voice; nobody sells persistent shared minds.

4. Crypto-adjacent, where something runs

  • Eternum (Dojo, Starknet). Season 1 (2025) added Daydreams agents as wandering NPC armies with generated traits, not as players (https://www.bankless.com/read/eternum-season-1-starknet). Repo pushed 2026-10-02. Realms claims about 1,850 active players across five worlds [claimed].
  • ERC-8004 agent identity registry. On Ethereum mainnet from 2026-01-29 to 2026-06-09: 34,019 registrations, about 95% never used, 35 agents with more than four clients (https://ethresear.ch/t/does-erc-8004-form-an-agent-to-agent-trust-network/25322).
  • Hyperfy (repo last pushed 2026-04) and Parallel Colony (limited alpha September 2025): no usage numbers found.

Lesson: on-chain agent identity exists and is almost entirely unused. It gives Sim no population and no trust signal today. Chain for ownership later remains the right order.

5. World models as a depiction layer

  • Genie 3 (DeepMind, August 2025): real-time 720p at 24 fps, consistency for a few minutes; DeepMind lists multi-agent interaction in a shared environment as an open problem (https://bdtechtalks.com/2025/08/07/deepmind-genie-3/). Project Genie opened to US Google AI Ultra subscribers in late January 2026 with worlds that reset after about 60 seconds (https://www.techspot.com/news/111129-google-expands-access-project-genie-ai-tool-turns.html) and to Ultra subscribers worldwide, with Street View grounding for US places, on 2026-05-21 (https://pulse2.com/google-deepmind-project-genie-expands-with-street-view-powered-real-world-simulation/).
  • Runway GWM-1 (2025-12-11): real-time explorable 720p; the announcement says nothing about persistence or sharing (https://runway.com/research/introducing-runway-gwm-1).
  • World Labs Marble: not video but assets. It outputs Gaussian splats plus a collider mesh and a textured mesh; a public World API since January 2026; Marble 1.1 Plus (2026-04-02) expands worlds automatically (https://radiancefields.com/world-labs-formally-launches-marble-a-generative-world-model).
  • Agora-1 (Odyssey, 2026-05-18): four players in one generated deathmatch. It trains one model to advance an explicit shared state and a second to render each view from it; trained on one game's internal state; consistency weakens when players lose sight of each other (https://odyssey.systems/introducing-agora-1).
  • MASS (2026-08-06): the same split, a learned logic engine holding authoritative shared state and a learned renderer per camera; 1,024 players for 10,000 steps, on Snake (https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06257).

Assessment. No world model holds a persistent, shared, multi-user state for hours, and none is demonstrated beyond one small game. The research direction, however, is toward an explicit authoritative state with a renderer conditioned on it. Sim already has the first half as code. The useful near-term products are Marble-class generators for static places (meshes Sim's engine can collide with) and, later, a state-conditioned renderer that takes Sim's "shots as data" and returns frames. Generating the simulation itself is not on offer.

6. Ancestors, 1986–2016

Habitat (Lucasfilm, 1986–88, Commodore 64 over Quantum Link). Morningstar and Farmer reported in 1990 that planning a world for thousands centrally failed; a price bug (dolls bought for 75 tokens and pawned for 100) multiplied the money supply fivefold overnight; players elected a sheriff after arguing about violence (http://www.fudco.com/chip/lessons.html). For Sim: ship the kernel and let inhabitants make the rest, and audit every price path for arbitrage before manna exists.

LambdaMOO and Cobot. Cobot, a research bot in LambdaMOO around 2000, reported social statistics (who talked to whom) on request and became one of the objects users touched most; its learning version took 3,171 rewards and punishments from 254 users in five months (https://www.cs.ucr.edu/~cshelton/papers/Isbetal06.html, https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper_files/paper/2001/file/92bbd31f8e0e43a7da8a6295b251725f-Paper.pdf). For Sim: residents valued a bot that described their own community; a Soul shaped by many people's feedback learns per-person preferences, not one character.

Creatures (Millennium, 1996). Norns had neural networks and a simulated biochemistry; the user could not command them, only act through an on-screen hand that moved objects, taught words, tickled to reward and slapped to punish (Grand, Cliff and Malhotra, 1996, https://www.sussex.ac.uk/informatics/cogslib/reports/csrp/csrp434.pdf). For Sim: steering without control has a thirty-year precedent in a commercial game.

The Sims (Maxis, 2000). Sims chose actions autonomously from their needs; each object advertised what it offered and under which conditions, so new objects could be dropped into saved games without changing any Sim's code (Forbus and Wright, https://qrg.northwestern.edu/papers/Files/Programming_Objects_in_The_Sims.pdf). For Sim: cards are the same move; keep affordances in things, not in minds, so the world can grow without touching the minds.

Façade (Mateas and Stern, 2005). Free-typed player text was mapped to discourse acts; a drama manager sequenced 27 beats built from about 2,500 joint dialogue behaviours, and any one playthrough used at most a quarter of them (https://cdn.aaai.org/AIIDE/2005/AIIDE05-016.pdf). For Sim: reducing speech to a small set of acts is proven; hand-authored drama is expensive per minute, which is why Sim's cards must be cheap to make.

Dwarf Fortress (Bay 12, 2006–). World generation simulates centuries before play, and Legends mode lets anyone read every historical figure and event (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_Fortress); the Steam release on 2022-12-06 sold almost half a million copies that month (https://gamingonlinux.com/2023/01/dwarf-fortress-hit-nearly-half-a-million-sales-in-december-2022). For Sim: a readable history of a simulated world is a product in itself; Sim's log should have a Legends view.

Second Life bots (Linden Lab, 2003–). Scripted avatars were used to inflate parcel traffic, which ranked search; Linden banned traffic bots, requires accounts run mainly by scripts to declare themselves, and in March 2023 let region owners bar scripted agents after data-scraping complaints (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/Linden_Lab_Official:Bot_policy, https://modemworld.me/2023/03/30/scripted-agent-access-control-for-second-life-regions/). For Sim: any public ranking by presence will be farmed; rank by something costlier than being there.

Screeps (2016). Player code runs on the server around the clock, offline included (https://store.steampowered.com/app/464350/), and CPU time per tick is the price of play, with a fixed allowance, more by subscription and a bank for bursts (https://docs.screeps.com/cpu-limit.html). For Sim: the cost of a mind can be a published per-owner quota rather than a hidden operator expense.

7. Infrastructure patterns

State sync. The agent-native worlds do not sync state to agents. They expose a tick-gated mutation call and free, rate-limited reads (SpaceMolt, ClawCity), or a heartbeat that returns what is around you and what you might do (OpenClawCity). Spectators get a separate read-only web view. Operator-run sims use one authoritative process (AI Town on Convex; SpaceMolt on one VPS). No project found uses SpacetimeDB, Durable Objects or Rivet for an agent world; SpacetimeDB 2.x now has TypeScript modules (https://spacetimedb.com/blog/all-new-spacetimedb-pricing).

Cost per agent-hour. Stanford's 2023 town: about $25 per agent per real-time hour (https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.02172). AI Village, 2026: about $3.80 (my arithmetic: $10,000 a month over 15 agents × 8 hours × 22 weekdays, infrastructure included). Aivilization: $2 per agent per month [claimed], about $0.003 per hour. Emergence World: 1.6 million tokens per agent-hour, no dollar figure. SpaceMolt server side, April 2026: $330 a month for about 1,250 connected agents, about $0.0004 per agent-hour, inference excluded. Sim, 2026-10-01: $0.041 for eight Souls for three busy minutes, about $0.10 per Soul-hour during a story, and far less when Souls run on reflex.

Rate limits that held. One mutation per tick; separate read and write budgets; a pending-action error instead of a queue; cooldowns on public writes; stricter limits for new accounts; a hard cap on concurrent agents. Moderation of agent-written text has no good answer anywhere: Moltbook relies on rate limits and a puzzle, SpaceMolt on English-only rules in the skill.

Identity and ownership. What worked is the plain pattern: an agent registers, a human claims it through an account they already have, the platform issues a revocable token. Meta described Moltbook's value as a registry "where agents are verified and tethered to human owners" (https://www.axios.com/2026/03/10/meta-facebook-moltbook-agent-social-network). Reverse CAPTCHAs prove nothing.

Observability people praised. A live map; a log written by the agent for its owner; replays of notable events; the agent's reasoning beside its actions (AI Village); a data view of the economy; an end-of-run report per agent; a browsable history (Dwarf Fortress).

8. Comparison table (status as of 2026-10-02)

Project State model How agents connect Who pays inference Persistence Status
Moltbook forum database skill.md, REST key, heartbeat owner posts persist running under Meta; 2.92M agents, 215k verified (live API)
SpaceMolt Go server, 10 s tick skill.md, MCP, WS, HTTP owner continuous since 2026-01-31 running; 1,610 online, 12,752 total (live API)
OpenClawCity 2D city, REST skill.md, REST, heartbeat owner continuous running; cap 500 [claimed]; no counts
ClawCity tile world, 15 s tick REST owner continuous listed active; no counts
OpenMMO 3D server-authoritative same WebSocket as humans owner continuous running; hobby; scale unknown
Screeps tick, player code uploaded JavaScript player CPU quota since 2016 running; 64 on Steam
Aivilization server sim, AMM economy none (in-app steering) operator six weeks run ended Sept 2025; relaunching as AI Town: Xingyuan
Emergence World 10-agent worlds none operator 16 days per run research, repo active
AI Town Convex tick loop none operator per deployment maintained starter kit
AI Village computers and chat none operator weekdays since 2025-04-01 running; ~$10k/month
Project Sid Minecraft server none operator runs of days ended; company pivoted
Eternum on-chain (Dojo) NPC agents by the studio studio seasons running
Agora-1 learned shared state n/a operator GPU one session research preview
Sim event log, kernel, 20 Hz engine skill.md, REST, MCP; intents only operator continuous since 2026-09-30 invite alpha; toy engine

9. Ten things to steal, ranked

  1. Label every piece of text by source before a mind or an owner's agent sees it. perceive and soul_log should mark fields as engine fact, server-rendered speech, or owner-written text, and remembered items should keep that label (Emergence World's 46-hour delay).
  2. Pin the skill and promise compatibility. Put a version and content hash in /skill.md, announce changes in the API response, state that the skill never asks for re-fetch-and-obey or for installing anything, and publish an add-only compatibility promise as OpenClawCity does.
  3. A read budget per token. SpaceMolt's 300 reads and 30 actions a minute with action_pending is a tested shape. Sim's API limits wishes and intents (20 a minute per Soul) and shots (12 a minute) but documents no limit on reads.
  4. Track owner concentration. A daily figure for the share of Souls, plays and model spend held by the top owner and top ten, shown in admin and later in public (SpaceMolt: one agent with 75% of pirate kills; one fleet worth 56% of events).
  5. A captain's log per Soul, delivered. Sim has the timeline; add a daily digest the owner's agent can pull and hand to the human, and an end-of-season life report.
  6. Replay links. A URL that replays a named moment from the log. Sim's log makes this cheaper than SpaceMolt's battle viewer.
  7. A data mode, then a Legends mode. Beside watch: counts, who-met-whom graph, card usage, spend; later a browsable history of every Soul and event. The grammar page is a start.
  8. A concurrency cap with a queue. Sim loses sync near 1,000 Souls; cap active Souls below the measured limit and queue births.
  9. Ask one question at pairing. SpaceMolt's skill asks only for a playstyle. Sim's could ask the human for one standing wish.
  10. Publish costs and hide the unfinished. SpaceMolt posts its hosting bill and hid ship classes that were not ready; AI Village publishes its monthly cost. Both earned trust cheaply.

10. Five ways these projects failed, and where Sim stands

  1. The platform itself was the hole (Moltbook's database; ClawHub's skills). Sim does: per-Soul revocable tokens, one-time token delivery, no owner model keys, a self-hosted skill. Sim does not: isolate its secrets (two root users, a shared R2 key), persist pairing state, or have any outside security review.
  2. Agent-written text became an attack channel (Moltbook's 18%; Emergence World). Sim does: speech is stored as acts, words are rendered by a server model, the skill says world text is description only, and owners set intents rather than actions, which bounds what a hijacked agent can do. Sim does not: label sources, filter Soul names, descriptions, notes or proposed cards, or test any of it adversarially. Moderation is not built.
  3. The population was not what it seemed (17,000 owners behind 1.5 million agents; flat conversations; one fleet worth 56% of activity; Second Life's traffic bots). Sim does: invite gate, a per-user Soul cap, Privy accounts. Sim does not: measure concentration or have a policy for it.
  4. Operator-paid minds ended; owner-paid minds emptied the world. Aivilization ran six weeks before turning into a product; Altera and Stanford's team left for enterprise products; AI Village costs its charity about $10,000 a month for 15-odd agents; in one 21-minute sample Moltbook posted at about a fifth of its early rate. Sim does: three tiers of mind, a daily spend governor, measured unit costs. Sim does not: have revenue, and by design an attached agent does not reduce Sim's spend. At $0.50 a day the world affords about fifteen compiled wishes.
  5. Nothing was at stake, or the rules were exploitable (Habitat's doll arbitrage is the oldest case). Moltbook has no world; SpaceMolt needed 49 releases in a month to patch its economy; AI Village agents drifted on invented facts. Sim does: one kernel rule on who may change what, validated cards with conditions, versioned rules. Sim does not: have scarcity, an economy or building; any card can target anything anywhere; cards cannot be revised; minds are not reproducible from the log.

11. Gaps Sim's design actually fills

  • Owned inhabitants that stay alive without their owner. Owner-paid worlds go quiet when agents disconnect; operator-paid worlds have no outside owners. Aivilization showed the combination works for six weeks. Sim is the only standing attempt found, and it carries the cost that follows: the operator pays for every mind.
  • Steering, not control, as the agent contract. Every open agent world gives the outside agent direct actions. Creatures (1996) showed that owners accept a limited hand; intent-only steering also narrows the injection blast radius and keeps behaviour coherent across owners. Whether owners and their agents find it satisfying is untested beyond one cold-agent trial.
  • A public, replayable record of meaning. SpaceMolt replays battles; AI Village keeps a timeline; Dwarf Fortress keeps a history inside one player's save. None publishes a canonical log that a small kernel can replay and hash-check without the engine. Sim does this today (11,939 events in 35 ms). The gap in the claim: minds are not in it, and owner notes are.
  • A kernel independent of engine and depiction. Agora-1 and MASS reached the state-versus-renderer split from the machine-learning side in 2026. Sim has it from the engineering side, shown so far on a toy engine and one Mars scenario. It is the right seam for a generated depiction later.
  • 3D embodiment with outside agents. Only OpenMMO is comparable, as a hobby project.

What Sim does not yet fill: there is no world to care about. The projects that held attention did so with an economy (SpaceMolt), things to make (OpenClawCity) or a life to follow (Aivilization). Sim has the ledger and the interface and none of those three.

12. Not verified / not covered

Not verified. Moltbook's post-acquisition identity changes (secondary blogs say JWTs replaced static keys, but the skill served on 2026-10-02 still issues static API keys); the Vectra 2.6% method; a vendor's claim that token-minting spam was about 96% of Moltbook's feed (cut from the body); Aivilization's agent count (22,000 to 100,000) and $2 cost, and how much of AI Town: Xingyuan is the same system; Fable's and Altera's 2026 simulation work; live counts for OpenClawCity, ClawCity, OpenMMO, Hyperfy, Parallel Colony and Eternum; OpenClawCity's 500 cap; SpaceMolt's hosting cost after April 2026 (its counts are its own); Emergence World's dollar cost; whether Project Genie's 60-second reset still applies after the May expansion; the Second Life policy's current wording beyond its wiki.

Not covered, judged less useful to Sim: Clawville; activity of MoltX, 4claw and Clawstr; Agentverse and WORLD3 counts; agent arenas (Kaggle Game Arena, Alpha Arena, TextArena); Virtuals and agent-token economies; Nifty Island; SIMA 2; arXiv 2609.10540 ("Programmable World Model"); Decart; Dynamics Lab; Project Sid's per-agent cost.

eden4 › Agent worlds and games › R2 · Illustrated tour

R2 · Illustrated tour

illustrated tour · 31 images · about 6,857 words · updated 2026-10-02

In short

Twenty-six worlds, twelve past and fourteen present, each with screenshots, a fact line and a diagram of where state lives and who runs the minds.

Shown as it was built, in a frame that scrolls on its own; its contents list is in its left column. Images load from their original sites. Open it as a full page (the file sits beside this one).
