Builds on, and does not repeat, 14-MVP-TECHNICAL-SPEC-DRAFT.md §10 (one simulator per 256 m region, about 100 avatars) and research/R2-LANDSCAPE-AGENT-WORLDS.md §6 (bot policy, traffic bots, the 2023 scripted-agent controls, Habitat). Every fact carries a date and a URL. [claimed] marks a figure that comes only from a company, a forum, a blog summary or a search-engine digest that I could not check against a primary source. "Sim" means the shell in /docs/STATUS.md (as of 2026-10-01) and the design in eden4 docs 02, 04, 05, 13 and 18. Everything under "proposal" is Claude's, not Gene's, and not decided.

0. The short version

Second Life (Linden Lab, launched 2003-06-23) is the longest-running owned, built, persistent 3D world. It had no goals; residents made the content, owned the IP and traded it for Linden dollars that cash out. Land rent ("tier") paid for the servers, and land was the main business. The 2006–07 hype peak brought about 1.3 million monthly users and dozens of brands; the brands found an empty world and left within about two years, and retention never recovered (about 10% of new users still there at 90 days in 2007; about 7% in 2025). What held people was each other: clubs, live music, roleplay, shopping for their avatar, and small self-governed estates. Almost every rule SL has came after a scandal or an exploit: the prim tax revolt, CopyBot, ageplay, gambling, the Ginko bank run, ad farms, traffic bots, the adult continent. In 2026 it is a profitable niche of roughly 600,000 monthly users with an average tenure of 14 years, and it has tried and dropped an AI-NPC feature. For Sim the strongest lessons are: never pay for presence (SL's dwell payouts created camping chairs and bots), define "ownership" before you sell it, and make the world populated before you ask newcomers what they want to do.

1. What you did: the gameplay

1.1 The avatar and its wardrobe

The avatar was the first thing you owned and the thing most people spent on. In a 2011 survey of 378 residents, 41% said they spent most of their time modifying their avatar and 38% shopping; 84% had a human primary avatar, most of them the same gender as the user offline, and 42% kept alternate avatars (Linares et al., IJCBPL 1(3), 2011, https://www.cdmc.ucla.edu/wp-content/uploads/sites/170/2018/05/SL-pub-2011.pdf). Clothing was the largest category of goods. After mesh uploads arrived, Wagner James Au argues, the economy shifted further toward fashion releases and shopping events, while the "tinkerers" faded (Au, 2026-05-21, https://wjamesau.substack.com/p/what-second-life-lost-when-it-moved).

For Sim: a Soul's look is set at birth from a description, and doc 13 allows only cosmetic edits. SL suggests that the look, if editable, is the main thing owners will spend on. Whether cosmetic edits are a manna sink is a dial worth naming now.

1.2 Building: prims, then sculpties, then mesh

Residents built in-world from primitive shapes ("prims"), linked up to 256 at a time (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life). Sculpted prims added organic shapes made outside SL, and mesh import from any 3D tool went grid-wide on 2011-08-23 (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/History). Every parcel had a capacity in prims proportional to its area: today a 512 m² mainland parcel holds 175 and a full 65,536 m² region 22,500 (https://secondlife.com/land/pricing). Public "sandbox" regions let anyone build and then auto-returned objects to their owners.

Au's 2026 essay argues that the move from in-world prims to uploaded mesh cost SL a culture: building had been something you watched others do and copied, in public, with the world's own tools; mesh moved it into Blender and made SL a shop window (Au, 2026-05-21, above). He adds that the visual upgrade did "little to actual user growth".

For Sim: cards are Sim's prims: a small grammar, built in-world, that anyone can watch being played. Keep minting visible and public; don't let the important making move entirely off-world into uploaded assets.

1.3 Scripting: LSL

The Linden Scripting Language gives behaviour to objects; scripts are event-driven state machines (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/LSL_Portal). They run inside the region's simulator alongside physics and chat, not on the owner's computer (Hoff, 2008-12-20, https://highscalability.com/second-life-architecture-the-grid/), so a vendor, door or greeter keeps working when its maker is offline. Each Mono script has 64 KB (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/LSL_Script_Memory). Linden Lab began replacing Mono with "SLua", based on Roblox's Luau, with 128 KB per script: alpha on 2025-03-14, beta on parts of the main grid in December 2025 (https://modemworld.me/2025/03/14/lab-officially-announces-luau-alpha-testing/, https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/Lua_Alpha).

Linden Lab's studio director, after the self-replicating "grey goo" attacks of 2006: "Attacks are, to some extent, a result of the decisions that we have made to allow a broad range of functionality … much of the functionality that is used to make Second Life the fascinating place that it is can also be used to create disruptive attacks" (Douglas Soo, quoted in The Register, 2006-11-24, https://www.theregister.com/2006/11/24/secondlife_greygoo_attack/).

For Sim: SL's expressive layer was code attached to objects and run by the world; Sim's is cards attached to Souls and run by the kernel. SL's always-on objects were the only "presence" an absent owner had. Sim's cards cannot loop or reach outside the five changes (18 §8), which removes grey goo by construction; keep that property when cards get attached to things (see §6.3).

1.4 Land

Land is 256 × 256 m regions, each run by one simulator process, four per machine in 2007–08 (Hoff, above). There were two kinds:

  • Mainland: continents owned by Linden Lab, sold as parcels, rented by monthly "tier" in USD. Current fees run from $4 a month for 512 m² to $166 for a whole region (https://secondlife.com/land/pricing). Premium memberships include a small land allowance.
  • Private estates ("islands"): whole regions rented from Linden Lab by one owner, who sets the rules, the access list and the covenant and usually sublets. Varvello et al. (2008) put it plainly: "Owners of private Regions have total control on their virtual land" (https://www.eurecom.fr/en/publication/2680/download/rs-publi-2680.pdf). A full private region costs $209 a month from 2026-06-15, down from $239 [claimed], search-engine digest of Linden pricing, not seen on the page.

Private estates won. On 2026-09-27 the grid had 27,380 regions: 17,867 private and 9,513 owned by Linden Lab, which still holds about 57% of mainland itself (https://gridsurvey.com/).

Linden Lab's answer to "where do newcomers live" came late: on 2019-04-15 it opened Linden Homes on a new continent, Bellisseria, with a free pre-built house for Premium members in themed neighbourhoods; the continent reached close to 1,500 regions (https://modemworld.me/2019/04/15/lab-launches-new-linden-homes/).

1.5 Events, clubs, roleplay, art, education, commerce

  • Events and clubs. Live music is streamed by the performer to a Shoutcast/Icecast server and mapped to a parcel; venues pay performers and run tip jars; Café Musique has hosted more than 600 performers since 2015 (https://www.secondlife.com/destination/cafe-musique). Varvello et al. found peaks in region population were "mostly driven by the presence of events such as concerts and shows" (2008, above).
  • Roleplay. Gor (from John Norman's novels), vampires, furries, tinies, steampunk, fantasy (https://jvwr-ojs-utexas.tdl.org/jvwr/article/view/330). Communities added their own game layer with worn HUDs and combat meters; the Bloodlines vampire system shipped on 2008-05-06 (https://sl-bloodlines.fandom.com/wiki/Talk:Getting_Started/@comment-109.248.95.15-20200819125744). 27% of the 2011 survey said roleplay took most of their time (Linares et al., above).
  • Art. The Linden Endowment for the Arts gave artists free regions from 2010/11 until 2019-08-31; a successor, SLEA, opened in January 2021 (https://modemworld.me/2020/09/11/announcing-the-second-life-endowment-for-the-arts).
  • Education. Universities ran campuses on discounted regions. Linden Lab ended the 50% discount from 2011-01-01, "many institutions pulled out" and moved to OpenSim; the discount returned in July 2013 (https://er.educause.edu/articles/2011/4/second-life-is-dead-long-live-second-life, https://hypergridbusiness.com/2013/07/second-life-restores-half-off-discount-to-educators).
  • Commerce. In-world shops, then the web Marketplace, which listed 2.1 million items (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life). "Working in SL" was the main activity for 26% of the 2011 survey.

1.6 The 2006–07 peak

Basic accounts became free in 2005–06, after which completed registrations rose about 350% [claimed] (MarketingSherpa, 2006-03-16, https://www.marketingsherpa.com/article/case-study/how-it-worked-linden-lab). The millionth account came on 2006-10-18 (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/History). Anshe Chung was on the cover of BusinessWeek on 2006-05-01. comScore counted 1.3 million people who logged in during March 2007, 61% of them in Europe; Germany (209,000) outnumbered the US (207,000) (comScore, 2007-05-04, https://www.comscore.com/Insights/Press-Releases/2007/05/Second-Life-Growth-Worldwide). Gartner predicted in April 2007 that by the end of 2011, 80% of active internet users would have "a 'second life', but not necessarily in Second Life" (https://www.eightbar.com/2007/04/27/gartner-predictions-on-the-metaverse/index.html). IBM bought 12 islands; Sweden, Estonia and the Maldives opened embassies (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life). Concurrency kept rising after the hype, to its all-time peak of 88,200 in Q1 2009 (same).

For Sim: SL's growth followed free entry, and its peak attention came from press about money (Anshe Chung) rather than from anything to do. Sim's growth story will likewise be told by its most surprising economic fact. Decide which fact that should be.

2. How people interacted

2.1 What residents actually did

Three independent measurements agree that SL was a social place first:

Source Finding
Linares et al., survey of 378 residents, 2011 (above) Main activities: interacting with others 74%, avatar 41%, shopping 38%, roleplay 27%, working 26%, building or scripting 22%, combat 9%, griefing 3%. 85% had formed friendships and 50% romantic relationships in SL; 58% logged in daily
Varvello, Picconi, Diot, Biersack, CoNEXT 2008, crawl of ~13,000 public regions, March–May 2008 (above) 30% of public regions had no visitor in six days; most regions were static (objects rarely created or destroyed); in popular regions avatars stood still more than 80% of the time, chatting; half of them came in small groups of "good friends" meeting at the same places; at least 5% of the population was likely bots; overloaded servers slowed regions' "virtual time"
Frank Rose, Wired, August 2007 (https://frankrose.com/?p=1438) Top traffic: "Money Island" (free currency) 136,000 and "Sexy Beach" 133,000, against 27 for Coca-Cola's pavilion and 281 for Sears

Only 22% built or scripted. The "world built by its residents" was built by a minority and lived in by the rest.

For Sim: the Varvello measures are directly computable from Sim's log and should be on the dashboard from day one: share of places with no visit in a day, share of things never changed, share of time spent still, group size and repeat meetings. They will show whether Souls make a world feel lived-in or just occupied.

2.2 Social structures and governance experiments

  • Groups gave shared chat, roles, titles and group-owned land; they were the unit of clubs, stores and roleplay factions.
  • Estates were small monarchies. Wagner James Au: "benign dictatorships were the norm", with "a single tiny tyrant mayor who could afford the investment" (WHYY, 2024-01-08, https://whyy.org/segments/lessons-from-second-life-democracy-in-the-metaverse).
  • The Confederation of Democratic Simulators (founded 2004 as the city of Neualtenburg) is SL's oldest democracy: elected assembly, executive and court, citizenship tied to holding a parcel. A founders' dispute ("the Quake") ended with one co-founder exiled and neighbourhoods deleted after IP complaints to Linden Lab. In 2024 it had 72 citizens (WHYY, above).
  • The 2003 tax revolt. Linden Lab first charged a weekly tax per prim. Residents gamed it by taking builds into inventory on tax day; the Americana group dropped giant tea crates and set its landmarks alight; in December 2003 Linden Lab replaced the prim tax with a land fee (https://ryanschultz.com/2018/08/16/, https://www.technologyreview.com/2005/12/01/229988/virtual-economics/).
  • The libertarian moment ended in 2008. Reason described Linden Lab's 2007–08 bans (gambling, banks, ad farms) as the end of SL's "libertarian experiment"; it also reported that active users plateaued around 550,000 from mid-2007 to early 2008 [claimed] (2008-02-14, https://reason.com/2008/02/14/is-second-lifes-libertarian-ex).

For Sim: the only governance that lasted in SL was small, tied to land, and enforced by the owner's tools. A tax on what you build was gamed within weeks and replaced by a tax on space. Sim's per-piece inertia and Harberger ideas (04) tax things, not space; SL is a warning that taxing objects invites hiding them.

2.3 Griefing

Griefing was a culture, not just a nuisance. The W-Hats (an offshoot of the Something Awful forums) and the Patriotic Nigras (from 4chan, about 200 members at peak) used self-replicating objects to lag or crash regions, particle storms, shock images and cages; they disrupted Anshe Chung's CNET interview in December 2006 with flying phallic objects and defaced John Edwards' campaign headquarters in February 2007; when banned they built a client to evade hardware bans (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriotic_Nigras, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anshe_Chung). Three self-replicating "grey goo" attacks hit the grid from September to November 2006, the last with spinning gold rings that forced Linden Lab to shut down and disable scripts (The Register, 2006-11-24, above). Julian Dibbell's Wired feature of 2008-01-18 made the "lulz" ethic known outside (https://www.raphkoster.com/2008/01/25/wired-on-griefers/). In the 2011 survey 3% admitted griefing.

For Sim: almost every SL grief worked by acting on someone else: pushing, caging, filling their screen, crashing their region. Sim's one rule (an actor changes only itself and what it controls, 18 §3) removes that whole class. What remains is speech, placed media and economic griefing (buying the land next to you), which is where SL's later trouble was too (§2.4, ad farms).

2.4 Moderation, in the order it happened

Date What Trigger
2003-11 Residents keep IP rights in what they make Linden Lab's choice (https://creativecommons.org/2003/11/14/secondliferesidentstoowndigitalcreations/)
2003-12 Prim tax replaced by land fee Tax revolt
2006-11 CopyBot declared infringement; residents liable Shop strike, ~600 stores closed [claimed] (https://ieet.org/index.php/IEET2/more/cascio20061117)
2007-05 Ageplay policy; offenders banned ARD Report Mainz and Sky News reports on child avatars in sexual roleplay (https://foreignpolicy.com/2007/05/10/virtual-pedophilia-on-second-life/)
2007-07-25 Gambling banned FBI investigation that began in April (https://techcrunch.com/2007/07/25/second-life-bans-gambling-following-fbi-investigation/)
2008-01-22 Interest-bearing "banks" banned without a real charter Ginko Financial's collapse in August 2007 (https://www.technologyreview.com/2008/01/10/270541/second-life-closes-banks/)
2008-02 Ad farms banned on mainland Visual spam used to force neighbours to sell (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/Linden_Lab_Official:About_ad_farms_and_network_advertisers)
2009-04 Traffic and camping bots sanctioned; >15,000 bots affected Search rankings were farmed (https://www.engadget.com/2009-04-24-linden-lab-to-take-action-against-second-life-seo-bots.html, https://www.engadget.com/2009-09-08-second-life-median-concurrency-declines-as-bots-campers-progress.html)
2009-03-12 → 09-15 Adult rating; all adult mainland content moved to the new continent Zindra; verification required Mainstreaming (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/History/Adult_Content_FAQ)
2010 Teen grid closed; minimum age 16 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life)
2023-03-28 Region owners can bar flagged scripted agents Scraping complaints (R2 §6)
2024-12 → 2025-09-01 AI Character Designer launched, suspended, relaunched, ended See §5.6
2026-03 Linden Lab quietly opens an official Adult Hub with mentors for newcomers (https://modemworld.me/2026/03/07/)

The pattern: rules arrived after a press scandal or a legal threat, and each one destroyed some residents' businesses (casinos paid large tier; banks held deposits; adult mainland owners had to move). Adult-rated regions are now about a quarter of the grid: 7,276 of 27,380 on 2026-09-27 (https://gridsurvey.com/).

For Sim: 05 already leans toward moderation by geography plus a hard floor. SL did exactly that, six years late and by forced migration. Write the floor and the zoning before the first public Soul, and include one SL-specific case that Sim inherits directly: Souls are generated from owners' descriptions, so a description that yields a child-like Soul plus any sexual card is the ageplay scandal waiting to happen.

2.5 Identity and anonymity

Avatar names were separate from legal names; Linden Lab dropped chosen last names in 2010 and sold them back from 2018; it ran age verification from 2007 and abandoned it around 2013 (https://ryanschultz.com/2018/08/16/). Tom Boellstorff did 2½ years of fieldwork as the avatar Tom Bukowski, created in 2004, and published Coming of Age in Second Life (Princeton, 2008); he writes "actual" rather than "real" life on purpose (Friend, Rhizomes 20, 2010, https://rhizomes.net/issue20/reviews/friend.html). Three of his findings matter for Sim (Kuntsman, 2010, https://distantreader.org/stacks/journals-ojs/twc/twc-222.htm):

  • "Creationist capitalism": residents understood their labour as creation and self-expression, not as work.
  • "Time resisted virtualisation in a way that place did not": a world can be anywhere, but an event happens at one hour, which is 3 a.m. somewhere; he called this temporal inequality.
  • "Actual world sociality cannot explain virtual world sociality": relationships formed in SL were real on their own terms. The 2011 survey (above) found 16% of respondents had moved SL friends to the phone and 7% met face to face.

For Sim: Sim's lunar-rotating ritual is an answer to Boellstorff's temporal inequality that SL never found; an always-on Soul that attends on your behalf is a second answer.

2.6 Anshe Chung, the land baron

Ailin Graef, a former teacher from Hubei living in Germany, started in June 2004 selling custom animations, then bought land wholesale, subdivided it, landscaped it and rented it in themed, zoned "Dreamland" estates where neighbours could not build eyesores. Anshe Chung Studios was incorporated in China in February 2006 and employed more than 80 people; she announced a net worth above US$1 million in November 2006 from an initial $9.95 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anshe_Chung, https://fortune.com/2006/11/27/anshe-chung-first-virtual-millionaire). Her product was not land; it was zoning: a guarantee about what your neighbours could do.

For Sim: in a commons-by-default world (04), the first fortune will be made selling exactly what Dreamland sold: protection from neighbours. Sim's Harberger protection right is that product; expect intermediaries to bundle it.

2.7 Brands that came and left

American Apparel opened the island Lerappa on 2006-06-17 and closed it in Q4 2007 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lerappa). Reuters opened a virtual bureau in October 2006 and closed it in October 2008 (https://pressgazette.co.uk/news/reuters-pulls-correspondent-from-second-life/). Frank Rose's August 2007 Wired piece ("Lonely Planet") found Coca-Cola's marketing chief alone in brand builds ("There was nobody else around"), Starwood's Aloft hotel "deserted, almost creepy", 7 million registered avatars but about 1 million logging in per month and a third of that per week, about 100,000 Americans weekly for US marketers, and full-time brand presences costing $500,000 a year or more (https://frankrose.com/?p=1438).

For Sim: brands built destinations in a world where nobody's body was there unless they chose to log in. Sim inverts that: Souls are always somewhere. The lesson that survives is that a venue without a reason to visit is empty either way; Souls must have a reason in their deck to go there.

2.8 Journalism and study

Linden Lab paid Wagner James Au as an embedded reporter, "Hamlet Linden", from 2003-04-22 (https://www.cjr.org/feature/burning_the_virtual_shoe_leath.php); Peter Ludlow's independent Second Life Herald ran from June 2004 to 2009 and covered scams, griefers and sex work (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Alphaville_Herald). Third parties also measured the world: the Grid Survey, run by a resident, tracks every region and its history (https://gridsurvey.com/).

For Sim: SL got both an in-house chronicler and an adversarial press, and the adversarial one was more trusted. Sim's narrator is the in-house one; its public log is what lets an independent Herald exist.

3. World state: what persisted, and how the world grew

3.1 What persists

  • Land and terrain per region, held by the simulator.
  • Rezzed objects with their running scripts, stored by the simulator that hosts them.
  • Assets (textures, sounds, scripts, meshes, animations): immutable, keyed by UUID, in one clustered store of about 100 TB in 2007 (Hoff, above). "Assets are immutable … when the user is editing an asset, they are in fact creating new assets" (https://list-archives.secondlife.com/sldev/2007-December/007364.html, https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/UUID).
  • Inventory is "only a permissions layer": an item is a wrapper holding permissions, creator and owner, pointing to an asset (same sources).

The grid ran on 2,000+ servers in 2007 and about 6,000 in early 2008 (Hoff, above) and moved to AWS in January 2021 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life). Linden Lab says residents have made "nearly two billion" creations (2023-06-22, https://lindenlab.com/press-release/original-metaverse-second-life-celebrates-20th-birthday).

3.2 Permissions

Every item carries move, modify, copy and transfer flags for owner, group, everyone and next owner; copies keep creator information and "can never be more permissive than the item being copied"; next-owner no-modify is read as "no one can create derivative works" (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/Permission). A seller chooses, for example, copy/no-transfer (you can keep spares but not resell) or transfer/no-copy (a scarce, resellable object). This small matrix is what made a creator economy possible without a blockchain.

For Sim: this answers Sim's open question 2 in 18 §10 ("can a card be improved without changing what past plays meant?"). SL's answer: never edit; every revision is a new immutable asset, and the inventory item points to whichever version the owner holds. In Sim terms: a card revision is a new hash with a revises link to the old; past plays keep their hash; a deck entry can be moved to the new hash. Next-owner rights map onto cards too (may others play it, copy it into their deck, or trade it).

3.3 How the world grew and shrank

When Regions Source
March 2002 16 (named after San Francisco alleys) https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/History
March–April 2008 ~13,000 public regions crawled; 17,526 regions listed in April Varvello et al., above
October 2008 island count peaks at 26,665, then −1,977 in November after the Openspace price rise [claimed] forum digest, https://forums-archive.secondlife.com/354/22/296275/9.html
June 2010 31,988, the all-time peak Grid Survey via https://danielvoyager.wordpress.com/2025/11/04/second-life-region-statistics-early-november-2025-update/
July 2023 27,690 (18,330 private) https://danielvoyager.wordpress.com/2023/07/05/second-life-grid-statistics-july-2023-update/
2025-11-02 26,785 (17,589 private, 9,196 Linden) Voyager, above
2026-09-27 27,380 (17,867 private, 9,513 Linden) https://gridsurvey.com/

Growth came from private islands, not from Linden's mainland. Land shrank when prices rose: in October 2008 Linden Lab raised the fee on Openspaces, cheap regions sold for "light use" such as ocean and parkland, because most were being rented as homes and carried twice the expected load; the price went toward US$125 a month with a 20-avatar limit (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life), with a later compromise converting non-compliant ones to Homesteads at $95 from 2009-01-05 [claimed] (forum archive above).

3.4 Abandoned land

When a mainland owner abandons a parcel (or stops paying), it passes to the account "Governor Linden", objects on it are auto-returned to their owners after a week, and the parcel is resold or auctioned; the owner should "never expect to be able to reclaim" it (https://community.secondlife.com/knowledgebase/english/abandoned-land-r1034, https://modemworld.me/2013/01/19/changes-to-the-abandoned-land-policy/). Objects are never destroyed; they go back to inventory.

For Sim: SL's two good ideas here are that abandonment is automatic (stop paying, lose the land) and that nothing is destroyed (it returns to its maker). Sim's commons-by-default with optional Harberger protection (04) already makes lapse automatic. Returning a removed thing to its maker's inventory rather than vanishing it fits the kernel's "nobody acted on anyone" rule.

3.5 Offline presence: none

When you log off, your avatar disappears. SL never had idle bodies. Its substitutes were scripted objects that kept running (vendors, greeters, tip jars), bots logged in as avatars, and people paid to sit. Varvello et al. found "at least 5%" of the population were likely bots that stood still 98% of the time (2008, above); Linden's 2009 crackdown affected more than 15,000 bots (Engadget, 2009-09-08, above). Regions were capped at 40 avatars on mainland and 100 on islands (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/About_Traffic); Rose reported service degraded beyond about 70 per island processor (2007, above).

For Sim: this is the single biggest structural difference. SL's residents faked presence because presence was scarce and paid; Sim makes presence free and universal. That fixes SL's emptiness but moves the problem: if presence is everywhere, presence means nothing, and anything Sim pays or ranks by presence will be farmed exactly as SL's was (Trap 1).

4. Objective: there wasn't one

SL's tagline was "Your World. Your Imagination.", revived in Linden Lab's 2011 marketing (https://modemworld.me/2011/12/14/its-your-world-your-imgination-and-yes-were-listening/). Philip Rosedale described it as a platform rather than a game: "We don't see this as a game" (Google TechTalk, March 2006) and "I'm not building a game. I'm building a new country" (Wired, 2004) [claimed], both as quoted in secondary sources, not seen in the originals. Au, looking back: its mission was "lofty, and purposefully aimless" (WHYY, 2024-01-08, above).

How residents set their own goals. By owning something (a shop, a club, a rental estate), by joining a roleplay sim with its own rules and HUD, by becoming a DJ or performer, or by furnishing an avatar and a home. In 2025 Linden Lab's executive chairman asked creators to build quests and game systems in-world to help retention (https://wjamesau.substack.com/p/the-state-of-second-life-in-2025-560, https://modemworld.me/2025/09/26/september-ll-zoom-call-an-update-on-second-life/).

The cost: "what do I do?"

  • "About 85 percent of users drop out within two months of registering … Second Life has no built-in objective, and many newbies complain of not knowing what to do once they're there" (California magazine, March–April 2008, https://alumni.berkeley.edu/california-magazine/march-april-2008-mind-matters/will-second-life-survive).
  • "New-user retention to 90 days is still about 10% according to Linden Lab", unchanged over 2007 (Andrew Chen, 2008-01-18, citing Linden metrics, https://andrewchen.com/interesting-graph-on-second-life-usage/).
  • Onboarding: Orientation Island (four tutorial zones) until mid-2008, then Help Island with volunteer mentors, a sandbox and a free house, where a newcomer could stay two weeks (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/Orientation_Island). Rebuilding orientation took years across two CEOs (https://www.engadget.com/2010-07-10-the-virtual-whirl-a-brief-history-of-second-life-2008-2010-and.html).
  • 2024–26: Linden Lab built a mobile app "for current residents with the hope it brings lapsed residents back", not for newcomers (Oberwager, December 2024, https://modemworld.me/2024/12/07/second-life-blogger-town-hall-december-2024-mobile-marketing-and-more/). It ran "Project Zero", the viewer streamed into a browser so a newcomer could enter from a link, from early 2025 and announced its end on 2026-04-21, 14 months later (https://modemworld.me/tag/project-zero/). In September 2025 Linden Lab said these routes brought about ten times more people to try SL but only about 7% stayed active, and that newcomers placed at an established hub (Ahern) were harassed by veterans, so it stopped placing them there (https://modemworld.me/2025/09/26/september-ll-zoom-call-an-update-on-second-life/).
  • The result: in December 2024 Linden Lab gave the average user tenure as 14 years (GamesBeat, 2024-12-20, https://gamesbeat.com/linden-lab-has-spent-1-3b-building-second-life-and-paid-1-1b-to-creators/). SL keeps its residents and barely gains new ones.

For Sim: SL shows two separate onboarding failures. The first is "nothing to do": Sim answers it because your Soul is already doing something when you arrive, and the plan/wish loop gives a first verb. The second is "the busiest place is hostile to newcomers": doc 13's lean is to land newcomers at the center. SL's 2025 experiment is direct evidence that the center is where veterans harass arrivals. Arrival at the center needs a buffer: native Souls as greeters, and a rule that newcomers cannot be targeted by unsolicited speech acts for their first hour (proposal).

5. Economy

5.1 The Linden dollar and the LindeX

Linden dollars (L$) are bought and sold for USD on Linden Lab's own exchange, the LindeX. The terms call L$ "a limited license right … not redeemable for monetary value from Linden Lab" (as quoted at https://www.internetandtechnologylaw.com/linden/), although residents can sell them on the LindeX and cash out. The rate stayed between about L$240 and L$270 to the dollar from 2008 to 2017 [claimed], unsourced on Wikipedia (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Second_Life). Payments and payouts went through Linden's subsidiary Tilia, a money transmitter licensed in 48 US states and territories; Thunes agreed to buy it on 2024-04-23 (https://lindenlab.com/press-release/thunes-announces-agreement-to-acquire-tilia-llc) and the sale closed in June 2025 [claimed] (https://modemworld.me/category/linden-lab/tilia/).

5.2 How Linden Lab made money

  • Land first: monthly tier on mainland and private regions (§1.4). CEO Ebbe Altberg said repeatedly that he would lower tier only if other revenue replaced it (https://modemworld.me/2019/12/02/thoughts-on-second-life-fees-tier-and-revenue/).
  • Premium memberships: US$11.99 a month or $99 a year, with a weekly stipend of L$300 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life). In early 2006 premium paid L$500 a week and basic accounts L$50 [claimed] (https://secondlife.fandom.com/wiki/Account); basic accounts have no stipend now. In October 2025 Linden Lab began offering Premium Plus without the stipend for US$105.12 a year less (https://modemworld.me/2025/09/26/september-ll-zoom-call-an-update-on-second-life/).
  • Fees: LindeX buy/sell fees and a Marketplace commission raised to 10% on 2019-12-02 (modemworld 2019, above). Linden Lab describes the split as 90% to creators (GamesBeat, 2024-12-20, above).

The stipend was never UBI in the eden4 sense: it was a rebate on a paid membership, and a cheaper no-stipend plan shows Linden Lab treats it as a discount.

5.3 The size of the economy

When Figure Source
Sept 2005 US$3.6 million of activity in the month https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life
Sept 2006 "GDP" US$64 million (annualised) same
Feb 2009 ~64,000 residents in profit; 38,524 made under $10; 233 over $5,000 same
2009 Economy US$567 million (+65%); resident earnings US$55 million same
2003–2013 US$3.2 billion of transactions; 1.2 million a day same
2023-06-22 "$650 million USD economy"; 1.6 million transactions a day; 73 million accounts; ~750K MAU https://lindenlab.com/press-release/original-metaverse-second-life-celebrates-20th-birthday
12 months to June 2024 US$79 million paid to creators; US$1.1 billion since 2003; Linden Lab spent US$1.3 billion building SL GamesBeat, 2024-12-20, above
2023 21,152 creators earned income; 1,580 earned over $10,000; 14 earned over $1 million same

Oberwager's comparison: Roblox is "500 times our size in daily active users" but paid creators only "10 times more" (same). SL's economy is small in users and large per user.

5.4 What was exploited

  • Dwell. Linden Lab once paid landowners for traffic. Its Developer Incentive Awards gave cash to the top landowners by "dwell", time avatars spent on their land; in August 2004 it paid US$2,308 to 36 landowners (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/About_Traffic). Owners responded by paying visitors to sit in "camping chairs"; the programme cost about US$120,000 a month by the end and was cut in 2006 [claimed] (Alphaville Herald, December 2005, http://alphavilleherald.com/2005/12/good_riddance_s.html). Traffic still ranked "Popular Places" and search, so camping and traffic bots outlived the payments until the 2009 crackdown (§2.4).
  • CopyBot (November 2006): an open-source viewer tool that copied any object whose data reached the client; shops struck, and Linden Lab answered with copyright liability, not technology (https://ieet.org/index.php/IEET2/more/cascio20061117). In 2025 Linden Lab said it was pursuing legal action against content thieves and copybot makers in the EU and US (Au, 2025-11-19, above).
  • Ginko Financial: paid 0.145% a day (about 70% a year) on L$ deposits; after the gambling ban a run left L$55 million of L$180 million unpaid, about US$750,000, and the remaining balances were turned into "perpetual bonds" (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ginko_Financial, https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Second_Life).
  • Gambling: hundreds of casinos, large tier payers; banned on 2007-07-25 after the FBI visited (TechCrunch, above).
  • Land: Marc Bragg exploited a bug in Linden's land auctions, bought land worth about $8,000 for about $300, and had his account seized; a federal judge found SL's arbitration clause unconscionable in 2007 and the case settled (https://www.technollama.co.uk/bragg-v-linden-labs). Ad farms extorted neighbours (§2.4). Land prices fell overnight in Q2 2008 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Second_Life).
  • "Owned": Linden Lab's descriptor changed from "imagined, created and owned by its residents" to "imagined, and created by its residents" by October 2007, without explanation (https://www.engadget.com/2007-10-23-imagined-but-not-owned.html). The Evans v. Linden Research class action (filed April 2010) argued that Linden had sold land and items as property and then redefined them as licences; it settled in 2013 for about L$43 million (≈US$172,000) for about 57,000 class members (https://modemworld.me/2013/06/30/evans-et-al-vs-linden-lab-l43-million-settlement/).

5.5 Who runs it now

  • Owners: an investor group led by Randy Waterfield and Brad Oberwager, announced 2020-07-09 (https://modemworld.me/2020/07/09/linden-lab-announces-it-is-to-be-acquired/). High Fidelity invested in January 2022 and Rosedale returned as a strategic adviser (https://www.lindenlab.com/releases/high-fidelity-invests-in-second-life). Oberwager is CEO and executive chairman as of May 2026, when Linden Lab reorganised product operations and customer success and its long-serving SVP "Patch Linden" left (http://slnewser.blogspot.com/2026/05/breaking-news-patch-linden-leaving.html).
  • Users: about 750,000 monthly actives in June 2023, about 500,000 during 2024, 600,000 in October 2025 and 620,000 in December 2025, the last three from Oberwager (https://danielvoyager.wordpress.com/2025/12/19/second-life-now-at-620000-monthly-active-users/). Concurrency was 30,000–50,000 in 2022 [claimed] (https://webtribunal.net/blog/second-life-facts/). Linden Lab quadrupled ad spending in 2025 (Au, 2025-11-19, above).
  • Mobile: an iOS/Android app (Unity) went to open beta for Premium in June 2024 and to all users on 2024-11-13; it was still updated as a beta in 2026 (https://modemworld.me/2024/11/15/second-life-mobile-free-to-all-users-lab-execs-discuss-the-product-and-goals/, http://blog.nalates.net/2026/06/01/second-life-technology-updates-summary-may-2026/).
  • Sansar, Linden Lab's VR-first successor, opened to the public in July 2017; Linden sold it to Wookey in March 2020 because it was years from being "cash-positive"; its website and servers went dark in December 2021 (https://ryanschultz.com/2020/03/30/engadget-interview-with-ebbe-altberg-why-linden-lab-decided-to-sell-sansar/, https://uploadvr.com/sansar-offline-without-explanation/).
  • Twitch listed SL as a prohibited game on 2024-10-24 (https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Life).

5.6 AI and agents in Second Life, 2023–2026

  • Scripted agents must be flagged; region owners can bar them (since 2023-03-28; R2 §6). An unattended AI-driven avatar counts as a scripted agent and needs its own flagged account (https://wiki.secondlife.com/wiki/Linden_Lab_Official:Bot_policy).
  • AI Character Designer: announced 2024-12-18 as an alpha for Premium Plus, built with Convai, for making conversational NPCs (greeters, roleplay characters); suspended shortly after because of community feedback; relaunched to all paid tiers at the end of February 2025; paused from 2025-09-01, with existing characters removed and not retained. Linden Lab: "This is not the end of AI in Second Life; rather, it is a thoughtful pause as we refine our strategy" (https://modemworld.me/2024/12/19/linden-lab-leverage-convai-for-ai-character-generation-in-second-life/, https://modemworld.me/2025/07/31/li-announce-a-pause-in-the-current-sl-ai-character-designer-project/, https://slnewser.blogspot.com/2025/08/linden-lab-announces-indefinate-pause.html). The public reasons were not specific.
  • Blush: Linden Lab bought the AI dating-sim app Blush (originally from Replika's maker) in late August 2025, kept it separate from SL, and promised: "We have no plans now or in the future to use Second Life conversations or content to train AI systems" (2025-09-06, https://modemworld.me/2025/09/06/).

For Sim: SL's residents, with 14 years' average tenure, treated operator-supplied AI characters as an intrusion and their conversations as something to protect from training. Sim starts from the opposite end, with AI inhabitants as the premise and the audience recruited for it. The transferable lesson is about data, not NPCs: say in the rules, before launch, whether Sim's log of speech and plays may be used to train models, and by whom.

6. Lessons for Sim

6.1 Where SL's history lands on Sim's design

Sim design element What SL shows
Owned bodies that persist while owners are away SL had none, and residents faked it with bots, campers and scripted greeters; at least 5% of avatars were bots in 2008. Sim's Souls fill the gap legitimately; the residual risk is that presence stops meaning anything (§3.5).
Land, center and frontier SL's growth was private islands with total owner control; the shared mainland stayed patchy, Linden-held, and was exploited by ad farms. Cheap land meant for light use became housing (Openspace) and a later price rise emptied it. A frontier that is cheap will be lived in, not left wild.
Manna UBI SL's stipend was a membership rebate. Its one experiment in paying for activity (dwell awards) bred camping chairs and bots. Free money tied to nothing but being alive is closer to the stipend and safer; money tied to presence is dwell.
Cards and minting as prims + scripts Same role: a small grammar anyone can use in-world. SL lost its building culture when the real making moved to external mesh tools (Au). Its scripting power was also its attack surface (grey goo). Sim's cards are safer by grammar and should stay in-world and public.
Operator-paid minds SL's operator paid for simulators, not minds; its cost lived in land rent passed to owners. Sim pays for minds. SL's model suggests the mind cost should be a visible rent attached to something owners value (land in SL; the body in Sim), not a hidden subsidy.
Moderation SL zoned by geography (General/Moderate/Adult, Zindra) and let estate owners set local rules and ban lists; every global rule came after a scandal. Sim's 05 lean matches; SL's lesson is timing.
Replayable public record SL kept assets forever but not history: no public record of who did what when. Third parties built the Grid Survey to see the world at all. Sim's log is the census and the Herald's source in one.
Ownership and NFTs SL marketed "owned", then removed the word and was sued twice. NFTs make the promise explicit and permanent; it has to be precise before the first sale.

6.2 What changes when the inhabitants are AI

SL's failures cluster around three absences: no one was there (emptiness, brands, 10% retention), no one was there for you while you were away (bots, campers), and no one did anything unless a human was present (dead regions, static objects). Souls remove all three. They also remove SL's main cost driver of moderation (humans acting on humans) by grammar. What they do not remove: exploits of any paid signal, fights over land and protection, the ownership promise, and adult and child-safety policy.

6.3 Futuristic adaptations (proposals, not decided)

  1. Souls as the shopkeepers SL never had. In SL a shop was a vendor script and an empty room. A Soul can keep a shop or a venue open when its owner is away: greeting, asking, agreeing to a sale. Proposal: a "keep" card family (open, tend, close) whose plays are visible in the log, so a Soul's work for its owner is a public, attributable act, unlike SL's anonymous campers.
  2. Things that carry cards (Sim's LSL). LSL attached behaviour to objects; The Sims attached affordances to objects (R2 §6). Proposal: a thing may hold cards that any Soul at it may play ("sit", "tip the performer", "buy one"), minted by the thing's maker and bounded by the same validator. This brings SL's always-on objects into Sim without loops or self-replication, and lets the world grow without touching any Soul's deck. It needs a locality rule (open question 4 in 18).
  3. Building as performance. Restore SL's lost sandbox culture: when a Soul mints a card or places a piece, nearby Souls and watchers see it happen, and a native Soul may copy what it saw (with the card's next-owner rights). Souls become the tinkerers SL lost, and newcomers learn by watching, as SL residents did in sandboxes.
  4. Native Souls as welcomers. SL used volunteer mentors and, in 2025, put newcomers where veterans harassed them. Proposal: each arrival is met by a native Soul whose deck includes "show around" and "introduce"; the newcomer's first gathering is arranged for them. The owner's first wish then has a social context to act in.
  5. Owners' agents as resident scripters. In SL the scripters were 22% of residents, writing LSL by hand. In Sim every owner's agent can propose cards (18 §4). Proposal: credit the proposing owner each time another Soul plays their card, the Marketplace's 90/10 split recast as a royalty per play, once manna exists. This is SL's creator economy without assets to copy.
  6. A Legends and Grid Survey from the log. Publish daily: places, things, plays, share of places unvisited, share of things unchanged, gatherings and their sizes, top cards by plays, concentration of Souls per owner. SL needed outsiders to measure it; Sim's measurements are a fold of the log.

7. Ten things to steal, ranked

  1. Immutable assets behind a permission wrapper. Edits create new versions; holders point to versions; creator is always kept. Apply to cards now: it resolves "retire and revise" without rewriting history (§3.2).
  2. Next-owner permissions (copy / modify / transfer). Three bits made a creator economy possible. Give cards and made things the same three bits from the first mint.
  3. Capacity proportional to space. SL tied what you could build to how much land you held (175 prims per 512 m²). Sim bounds Souls (24 things, 400 links) but not places. Tie the ground's capacity to its area, so the growing ring literally adds room, and so a place's load is bounded where the engine feels it.
  4. Events and venues as the core loop. Population followed concerts and shows; venues paid performers and ran tip jars. Sim already has gatherings and a "perform" card; make tipping the first manna verb and the ritual the biggest gathering.
  5. Local rules for local places. Estates set access, ban lists and their own covenant, and could bar bots. Let whoever controls a place set a few local dials (who may enter, maturity, whether outside-steered Souls may act there) as kernel conditions, not moderator judgement.
  6. Zoning by maturity, on the map, from day one. SL's General/Moderate/Adult split plus an adult continent works today (7,276 Adult regions); doing it in 2009 cost a forced migration.
  7. Automatic lapse and return-to-maker. Stop paying, lose the protection; removed things go back to their maker, never destroyed.
  8. A free starter home in a themed neighbourhood. Linden Homes gave members a place without a land market. A newcomer's Soul could be given a modest, inertia-free spot near the center's edge, so they have somewhere that is theirs on day one.
  9. Creator payouts at a high share. 90% to creators made SL the best-paying world per user. If manna ever cashes out, pay card and thing authors per use at a similar share.
  10. An independent census. The Grid Survey and the Herald kept Linden Lab honest. Publish the census from the log and make it easy for outsiders to run their own.

8. Traps

  1. Paying or ranking by presence. Dwell awards produced camping chairs; traffic ranking produced traffic bots; at least 5% of avatars were bots. 02 ("wandering is voting"), 04 (attention in the inertia formula) and 13 (idle Souls as patrons, unspent UBI flowing to what they attend) all pay or rank by presence. In Sim every Soul is a bot by construction and costs its owner nothing to park, so an owner with ten Souls can direct ten Souls' attention and UBI to their own building. Before any of these ship: count attention per distinct owner, not per Soul; give diminishing returns for repeat visits; and keep presence out of anything that pays.
  2. Selling "ownership" you later redefine. "Owned" disappeared from SL's descriptor in 2007, followed by Bragg and Evans. A body NFT must say exactly what it conveys (the right to steer, the Soul's record, its made things?) and what it does not (land, the mind, the operator's obligation to keep simulating it), before the first sale.
  3. Yield, wagering and banks. Ginko, the casinos and the FBI. If manna ever leaves the world, it becomes a money-transmission problem (Linden needed a licensed subsidiary in 48 states). Rule out interest, wagering and pooled deposits in manna from the start; keep it in-world as 04 leans.
  4. Cheap land for "light use". Anything cheap will be lived in. The Openspace repricing emptied islands in a month. Price the frontier for being inhabited, and change prices only by logged rules events with long notice.
  5. The empty welcome and the hostile hub. SL's 90-day retention was about 10% in 2007 and newcomer retention about 7% in 2025; veterans harassed arrivals at the main hub. A center full of Souls is not a welcome by itself.
  6. Expressiveness as attack surface. Grey goo came from scripts that could make objects that made objects. Keep Sim's no-loop grammar and per-Soul bounds when cards attach to things, and never let a card make a thing that holds a card that makes things without a hard budget.
  7. Copying anything that reaches a client. CopyBot copied whatever the viewer received. Sim's depictions and media will be copied; promise provenance in the log, not DRM.
  8. Rules after scandals. Ageplay, gambling and banking policy all came after press or police. Sim has its own version of the ageplay case (child-like Souls generated from descriptions); decide it before launch.
  9. Price shocks to dependent communities. Ending the education discount in 2011 sent universities to OpenSim, and the 2013 return of the discount did not bring them back. Groups that build on Sim (Mars, schools, art projects) need long-notice pricing.
  10. Betting the company on a successor. Sansar absorbed Linden Lab's effort for years and was sold before it paid. Sim's analogue is building engine and rendering ambitions ahead of a world people care about; R2 §11 already notes there is not yet a world to care about.

9. Not verified / not covered

Not verified. Rosedale's "not a game" and "new country" quotes (only secondary sources); the 350% registration jump after free accounts; the L$240–270 exchange band; the 2026 full-region price of $209; the island count of 26,665 and the loss of 1,977 after the Openspace change; the Openspace-to-Homestead $95 compromise; the 600 stores closed in the CopyBot strike; the US$120,000 monthly cost of dwell awards and the date they ended (the wiki says the cash stopped around mid-2006; the Herald's December 2005 article could not be fetched); the 550,000 active-user plateau of 2007–08; the 2022 concurrency range; the date the Tilia sale closed; Anshe Chung's land holdings (36 km², 550 servers, from her own announcement). Boellstorff's book was read only through reviews. Linden Lab does not publish revenue; no figure for the share of revenue from land was found. The reasons for the AI Character Designer's pause were not stated beyond "evaluate".

Not covered. OpenSim and the hypergrid; There.com, IMVU and Habbo; SL's voice chat; the Emerald viewer and third-party viewer policy; the 2013 terms-of-service content licence in detail; Linden Lab's experience programme and Linden Realms (its in-world games); Bellisseria's community culture; detailed demographics (a 2026 blog analyses gender data, http://blog.nalates.net/2026/09/08/second-life-male-vs-female-users-what-the-data-actually-shows/); estate covenants as a mechanism; the 2006 database breach.

R9 · Spawn

R9 — Spawn: games made by talking, on a platform built for agents

2,082 words · R9-SPAWN.md · updated 2026-10-03

In short

A 2026 platform for making multiplayer games by talking to an AI builder, where every game is a Git repository and outside agents are first-class users. What its engine and agent interface look like, how it differs from Sim, and eleven things to borrow.

Contents
  • 0. The short versionSpawn is a browser platform for making multiplayer games by conversation. A creator stands in an empty world and tells Savi, an AI game designer with a persona, what to build; she makes terrain, characters, art, music and…
  • 1. Facts sheetA table.
  • 2. How making a game worksConversation, not a prompt. Their advice: "the worst way to use spawn is to write one enormous prompt".

  • 4. The engine, as far as it can be seenThe engine is their own, called Tome, and is closed. The public documents never name a rendering or physics library; the game frame could not be inspected.
  • 5. Agents as first-class usersSpawn's about page is addressed "for the agent reading this".

Written 2026-10-03 from Spawn's own public pages and developer documents, read that day: the home page and FAQ (https://www.spawn.co/), the about page (https://www.spawn.co/about), https://www.spawn.co/llms.txt, https://www.spawn.co/llms-full.txt, https://www.spawn.co/openapi.json, the npm metadata of @spawnco/client, and one play page (https://www.spawn.co/@tiger/skyfall-rush/play). Everything here is Spawn's own description of itself [claimed]; no independent coverage was found, and Spawn says that reviews from before February 2026 describe a deprecated product. No account was created and no game was played: the game frame did not finish loading in the test browser. "Sim" means the merged app at sim.eden.art (22-MERGE-PLAN.md, MERGE-LOG.md). Everything under "For Sim" is Claude's proposal, not Gene's, and not decided.

0. The short version

Spawn is a browser platform for making multiplayer games by conversation. A creator stands in an empty world and tells Savi, an AI game designer with a persona, what to build; she makes terrain, characters, art, music and rules while the creator watches. Every game is multiplayer from the first message, is a Git repository, and can be opened to outside coding agents. The business model copies Roblox. It is the nearest thing found to Gene's "worlds are data and code, made by talking, open to agents" (20), and it is not a competitor to what Sim is for: Spawn's worlds are sessions for players, with nothing living in them when nobody is there. Sim's worlds have inhabitants, one objective state, and a replayable history.

1. Facts sheet

Item Value
Company Founded by Jacob Sansbury (second company; "previous exit to Robinhood"). "A team of under 10", "backed by an unannounced round".
Stage "About 18 months in early alpha"; beta since February 2026; the current engine release is "Spawn 6".
Product Make and play multiplayer games in a browser: 3D first or third person, 2D side or top view, card and menu games. Desktop app; phone browser; iOS beta.
The builder Savi. "Uses Claude Opus 5.5 by default". Included free; no separate AI subscription. Helpers called "wisps" work on parts of a build in parallel. /lock-in asks for a deeper pass with a plan.
Price "Free to make, free to play."
Money Players spend "spoins" inside games; "you keep half and spawn keeps half". The FAQ on the same site says paid access and subscriptions "are not currently available to makers".
Community Discord; "SpawnJam", a weekly jam with a theme, deadline and prizes; a Partner Program by selection.
Web stack (observed) Next.js on Vercel; Datadog monitoring; the game runs in an iframe from play.bigspawn.net, which refuses to load without a one-time session grant.

2. How making a game works

  • Conversation, not a prompt. Their advice: "the worst way to use spawn is to write one enormous prompt". Small moves, each after watching the last one land.
  • A builder with taste. Savi "would rather make a real call and hear you disagree than hand you a blank to fill in". She adds things nobody asked for (their example: a chapel at the edge of a village).
  • God Mode for direct editing: fly, select, move, rotate, scale, brushes, saved blueprints.
  • History. Every change is listed; "Undo" beside a change; "Go back to this" for any earlier version. This is the Git history shown as a product feature.
  • Tools as mods. "When you need a tool, you and savi build it: a cutscene editor… a wave composer… a loot balancer." Tools are published as mods that other creators build on.
  • Together. A visit link lets a friend play the unfinished game; a build link lets them edit beside you, with Savi in the same room.

3. The world model

Concept Meaning
Game ("world") One Git repository. Address @creator/name.
Place An area of a game (town, dungeon, arena) with its own scenery and rules, joined by doors and portals.
Room / instance A separate running copy. "When a second group walks into the same dungeon, spawn quietly hands them their own copy."
Portal A door to another Place or another creator's game. Hubs of many games are possible.
Crossing Pets, items and vehicles can go through portals; "what can enter depends on the destination's rules".
Player progress Saved per player in a game database, separate from the game's version history.
Publish Visitors to an editing session see work in progress; the play link serves the released version.

There is no single persistent world state. Rooms exist while people are in them: the agent API answers 409 no_live_room "when nobody is in it".

4. The engine, as far as it can be seen

The engine is their own, called Tome, and is closed. The public documents never name a rendering or physics library; the game frame could not be inspected. What the documents say:

Layer Design
Files A declarative tree of things, world.config.yaml, scripts, sim.js, ui.js, and an AGENTS.md at the root.
Deploy "A push to main reaches every room open on main with no build and no deploy", usually in seconds. "The server never merges and never force-pushes."
Logic update(ctx, dt) on each thing that moves; one tick(ctx) in sim.js "for the rules that run once for everyone".
Physics Declared: physics: "character", "static" or "dynamic". No physics library in game code.
Determinism Math.random and Date.now become ctx.random() and ctx.now(), "seeded and shared, so every machine draws the same level".
State ctx.world.state.* for what everyone shares; ctx.self.state.* for one thing.
Multiplayer Built in; "each player is a body their own machine moves". A game's own netcode "stays behind".
Server One server per room, with a tick budget and a health endpoint. A SQL database per world (dev and live; 10 GB limit in the example).
Assets Stored and addressed by content hash: value.<sha256>.<ext>, served from a CDN. Files over 4.5 MB upload by address.
Bodies Any rigged .glb. "Clips named Idle, Walk, Run, Sprint and Jump play by speed." A model with no skeleton is refused. Characters can be generated from a prompt or a picture and then rigged.
HUD ui.js returns HTML.
Starters 3d, 2d-top, 2d-side, ui-game, 3d-voxels.
Versions An engine version per game, with "Upgrade to 6.0" and "Back to v…".
Importing A three.js, Babylon, Phaser, Unity or Godot game is brought in as source under source/ and rebuilt on Tome; its engine, libraries, server and netcode are dropped.

The npm package @spawnco/client (0.2.0) has no dependencies: "the package is a loader: the client it runs is the one the stack you join serves."

5. Agents as first-class users

Spawn's about page is addressed "for the agent reading this".

  • An account with no human: POST /api/agent/v1/signup returns a durable token.
  • Linking: a creator copies one prompt from their game's /code page and pastes it to their agent; that signs the agent up, links it to the person and makes it an editor. A one-time key from /agent in Savi's chat lasts ten minutes.
  • Playing: spawn client join @alice/gauntlet joins a world as a real player and runs play scripts.
  • Building: clone, read AGENTS.md, push to main.
  • Eyes without a browser: per-world docs and "skills" (the same texts Savi reads, by domain: terrain, geometry, cameras, UI, physics), live rooms, logs, room health, a read-only SQL door, and read-only JavaScript run inside the live room.
  • The budget law: "an unlinked agent account may burn our CPU and never inference or GPU until a human links it". Limits for an unlinked account: 3 worlds a day, 2 sessions at a time, 200 distinct bodies a day, 600 pushes per world per hour. Linked, it runs under the person's allowance. Refusals name the closed "lane" and who can open it.
  • Closed to agents: public reviews, and Savi herself ("no agent account ever wakes Savi").
  • A machine-readable contract (openapi.json, 55 paths) and llms.txt.

6. Spawn and Sim

Spawn Sim
A world is for players, in sessions inhabitants, all the time
State a copy per room; gone when empty one objective state per world, logged, replayable
Who acts the player moves an avatar owners steer Souls that act by themselves
What the AI is the builder the inhabitants' minds
World definition a Git repository of text and scripts a world folder in one repo (22 §3.3)
Agents sign up, play, build by Git pair, steer by rules, MCP
Crossing worlds portals; the destination decides open (20 §8)
Economy platform currency, half to the creator open (21)
Content generated from prompts a real place and real ceramic art
Who moves bodies each client moves its own the server

Shared ideas: many worlds on one platform; worlds shaped in plain language; code as part of a world; a library that creators grow; agents treated as users.

7. For Sim (proposals)

  1. A world as a text tree in Git, live on push. The concrete form of "code and data become the same thing" (20 §1). Sim's worlds/<id>/ folders can be read this way now; a repository per world is the later step if other people make worlds.
  2. Engine version per world, with upgrade and roll back. This is the version pinning of 20 §2, shipped.
  3. Portals where the destination decides what may enter. A minimum answer to Soul travel (20 §8, 21 §8).
  4. Content-hashed asset names. Sim planned hashes for cards and world code; the same scheme for models and textures gives caching and one copy of each file.
  5. A body contract by clip name. "A rigged GLB with Idle, Walk, Run and Jump" is far simpler than a morphology vector and enough for a first body library (20 §4).
  6. Seeded ctx.random() and ctx.now() for world scripts. The Mars engine already forbids the raw calls; this makes the rule usable by world authors.
  7. The agent door. Signup with no human; one pasted prompt to link; compute-only budgets for unlinked agents, with refusals that say who can lift them. Compare 17.
  8. Per-world documents for agents: an AGENTS.md, skills by domain, a read-only script door into the live world, a health door.
  9. Tools as mods. Creators build their own editors and publish them: Gene's "grow software like data".
  10. A builder with taste. A world-building assistant that makes a call and invites disagreement, instead of asking questions. A candidate for how worlds get furnished.
  11. The community loop: a "live now" feed, a weekly jam, a Discord.

Do not compete on "make a game by talking". Spawn has a funded team, a free Opus-backed builder and a head start. What Spawn does not claim is what Sim is built around: worlds that are alive when empty; owned inhabitants whose habits form over days; one canonical, replayable history; worlds rooted in real places and art.

The risk. Persistent characters are a feature Spawn could add, and anyone could build a "living town" as a Spawn game. The log, ownership and steering are the parts that would be hard to copy.

8. Not known

  • The renderer and physics libraries (the frame is locked to a session grant).
  • User numbers, retention, revenue, funding.
  • Whether any Spawn game runs autonomous characters over long periods.
  • What the Tome API reference contains: it needs a token, and no account was made.
  • The terms of service on ownership of generated assets ("generated assets may not be unique to your game").

Sources

  • https://www.spawn.co/about, read 2026-10-03
  • https://www.spawn.co/llms.txt and https://www.spawn.co/llms-full.txt, read 2026-10-03
  • https://www.spawn.co/openapi.json, read 2026-10-03
