R3 · The ledger on chain

R3 — Putting the ledger on chain: options, costs, and what to decide now

3,590 words · R3-LEDGER-ON-CHAIN-OPTIONS.md · updated 2026-10-02

In short

What it costs to make the ledger immutable, verifiable and ownable, from free timestamps to fully on-chain worlds, at 7 to 2,000 inhabitants; a one-week plan for this month and the decisions it needs.

Contents
  • 1. Short answerThe goal is "a ledger which records actions over time, one that we can potentially put on chain … immutable".
  • 2. The log today and its growthRead from the Sim repository on 2026-10-02 (apps/server/src/migrations.ts, packages/engine-contract/src/hash.ts, packages/kernel/src/cards.ts):
  • 3. Price snapshot (2026-10-02, about 18:30 UTC)Derived costs (standard EVM gas figures, not measured deployments):
  • 4. The optionsCovers (a) Anchoring: commit to the log, store the data elsewhere; (b) Cards as on-chain objects; (c) Fully on-chain "autonomous world" frameworks; (d) Verifiable replay without consensus; (e) Appchain or rollup as the ledger.
  • 5. ComparisonYearly cost at today's prices for 7 / 200 / 2,000 inhabitants (1.9 / 53 / 530 GB raw, all events).
  • 6. What in the log format matters todayTimestamps cannot be backdated. History before the first anchor can only be proven to have existed by that anchor's date.
  • 7. Staged recommendationThis month: near-zero cost, keeps every door open. Roughly a week of engineering, under $15 a year in fees.
  • 8. Decisions for the ownerDo decisions belong in the public record? They are 61% of events.
  • 9. Not verifiedGas per anchor, registry entry and on-chain play are standard-cost estimates, not measured deployments; no primary figure for an EAS attestation on Base was found.
  • SourcesEthereum: https://etherscan.io/gastracker ; https://ethereum.org/roadmap/glamsterdam/ ; https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7825 ; https://ethresear.ch/t/worst-case-block-size-and-calldata-repricing-for-glamsterdam/23895

Research date: 2026-10-02. Prices were read on that date from primary pages, public APIs or chain RPC calls unless marked otherwise. Builds on 03-SIMULATOR-CHAIN-AND-NEUTRALITY.md (one simulator, versioned rules, the rollup analogy) and 18-KERNEL-CARDS-AND-SELF-CHANGE.md (plays and mints) without repeating them.

Terms used below. Event log: Sim's append-only record; the world state is whatever a deterministic fold (the kernel) computes by replaying it. Play: an event recording that someone used an action card (card hash, who, on what, manner). Mint: an event creating a new action card, a small content-hashed JSON program with a first author. Anchor: publishing a short digest of the log somewhere the operator cannot rewrite. L1: Ethereum mainnet. L2: a cheaper chain that settles to Ethereum (Base is used for prices). Checkpoint: a signed statement "after N events the log root is X and the state hash is Y".

1. Short answer

The goal is "a ledger which records actions over time, one that we can potentially put on chain … immutable". That sentence contains three properties with very different prices:

  • Immutable and publicly verifiable: almost free. A hash-chained log, a signed daily checkpoint, a free Bitcoin timestamp, and a public copy of the log plus the replayer let anyone check that history was not rewritten. Under $15 a year at any scale considered here, plus storage.
  • Ownable (a card as an object someone holds): about a tenth of a cent per card on an L2, and it can be added later without touching history, provided cards are identified today by a full-length content hash and a recorded first author.
  • Neutral execution (nobody, including Eden, can cheat or stop the world): expensive. It requires a fully on-chain world, a fraud-proof or validity-proof system, or an appchain. Most teams that built this for games stopped in 2025–2026. Defer it; nothing in the cheap path blocks it.

Two things cannot be added later: proof that a record existed on a given date, and a signature by the person who originated an action. Those should start this month.

2. The log today and its growth

Read from the Sim repository on 2026-10-02 (apps/server/src/migrations.ts, packages/engine-contract/src/hash.ts, packages/kernel/src/cards.ts):

  • Events are SQLite rows (world_id, seq, tick, kind, author, payload JSON, at), replicated to object storage by Litestream. The kernel folds birth, play, mint, deck, retire and rules; there is also a decision kind.
  • A canonical serialiser exists (stableStringify: sorted keys, undefined dropped, -0 normalised, non-finite numbers rejected) and hashOf = sha256(stableStringify(x)).
  • A card's hash is that digest cut to 16 hex characters (64 bits); the kernel state hash is compared at the same length.
  • Events carry no hash, no link to the previous event and no signature. author is a server-side identifier; the users table has a wallet column.
  • infra/verify-kernel.ts replays the log without the engine and compares the result with the live hash.

Measured volume (live world, 2026-10-02): 50,970 events in about 2.5 days with 7 inhabitants, of which 30,952 are decisions and 19,609 plays. That is about 2,900 events per inhabitant per day (1,770 decisions, 1,120 plays, about 20 other). A play is 150–300 bytes of JSON and a minted card 0.5–2 KB; 250 bytes is used as the average below. Kernel-only replay of all 50,970 events took 2.8 s, about 18,000 events per second.

Inhabitants Events / year Raw size / year Without decisions Full replay of one year
7 (today) 7.4 M 1.9 GB 2.9 M, 0.7 GB 7 min
25 27 M 6.6 GB 10 M, 2.6 GB 25 min
200 213 M 53 GB 84 M, 21 GB 3.2 h
2,000 2.1 B 530 GB 840 M, 210 GB 32 h

Two consequences. First, decisions are 61% of events. Whether they belong in the public, hashed record (they are model choices that lead to plays) is a decision with a 2.5× effect on every storage figure (section 8, decision 1). Second, at 200 inhabitants and above, replay from genesis stops being something every verifier does casually; verifiers would start from a checkpointed state, which is why checkpoints should carry a state hash from the start. Compression of JSON logs is typically several-fold but was not measured here.

3. Price snapshot (2026-10-02, about 18:30 UTC)

Item Value Source
ETH; BTC; AR $2,677.53; $84,566; $4.24 CoinGecko /simple/price API
L1 gas price; blob base fee 0.229 gwei; 0.0118 gwei per blob gas eth_gasPrice, eth_blobBaseFee on a public RPC; Etherscan showed 0.21 gwei at 18:11 UTC
Base L2 gas price; L1 data fee 0.006 gwei; under $0.00002 for a 200-byte transaction eth_gasPrice on mainnet.base.org; getL1FeeUpperBound(200) on Base's gas price oracle (0x42…0F)
Bitcoin fee rate 4–5 sat/vB mempool.space API
Arweave 13.40 AR per GiB → $56.8/GiB, one payment arweave.net/price/1073741824
Arweave via Turbo (card payment) $88.67/GiB payment.ardrive.io/v1/rates
Cloudflare R2 $0.015 per GB-month, egress free R2 pricing
Celestia / EigenDA / Ethereum blobs, 30-day average about $0.021 / $0.038 / $0.058 per MB growthepie API, fees paid ÷ data posted (unit assumed GB)

Derived costs (standard EVM gas figures, not measured deployments):

  • L1 anchor (store a 32-byte root, emit an event, about 45,000 gas): $0.028. Gas has been 100× higher in past years; at 20 gwei it is $2.40.
  • Base anchor (about 50,000 gas): $0.0008 including the L1 data fee.
  • Direct Bitcoin anchor (about 150 vB): $0.51. Through OpenTimestamps it is free: public calendars aggregate submissions into one Bitcoin transaction. All four default calendars returned HTTP 200 on 2026-10-02; the server repository was last updated 2026-08-15.
  • L1 calldata: 40 gas per non-zero byte under EIP-7623, about 41.9M gas or $26 per MiB. One transaction is capped at 16,777,216 gas (EIP-7825, live since the Fusaka fork of 2025-12-03), about 400 KB. The next fork, Glamsterdam, is "testing on devnets", Sepolia fork 2026-10-06, mainnet "Q4 2026, date not yet confirmed"; a further calldata repricing (EIP-7976) is among the proposals under consideration (ethereum.org).
  • One blob (128 KiB): $0.004, so about $0.03/MiB plus the carrying transaction. Nodes prune blobs after 4,096 epochs (about 18 days); blobs prove data was published, they do not store it.

4. The options

(a) Anchoring: commit to the log, store the data elsewhere

Compute a Merkle root over event hashes, sign a checkpoint containing it, and publish the root where nobody can rewrite it. The data itself lives in ordinary or permanent storage.

  • Buys: immutability from the first anchor onward (any later rewrite is provable) and public proof of when each record existed. It does not buy correct execution, neutrality or ownership.
  • Where to anchor: OpenTimestamps (free, Bitcoin-backed, confirms within hours); an L2 contract (under a cent, readable by other contracts, depends on that L2 surviving); L1 (cents, the strongest EVM-readable option). They combine freely.
  • Where to keep the data: object storage (already in place; under $1 a month at 200 inhabitants after a year; not credibly permanent); Arweave (one payment, built for permanence); Celestia, EigenDA or blobs (cheap, but retained for days to weeks, so they suit a rollup, not an archive); L1 calldata (permanent, about 450× the Arweave price).
  • Engineering: days: a checkpoint job, a 30-line contract if an EVM anchor is wanted, an upload script. Lock-in: none.

A chain-free relative is the transparency log behind Certificate Transparency and the Go checksum database (C2SP tlog-tiles): signed checkpoints that independent witnesses co-sign only if consistent, which catches an operator showing different histories to different people. A chain anchor is a witness nobody has to recruit. Publishing log segments in the tlog-tiles layout costs nothing and reuses existing verifiers.

(b) Cards as on-chain objects

A minted card is already a content-hashed program with a first author. Three levels:

  1. Registry entry contentHash → (firstAuthor, mintSeq, checkpoint): about 65,000 gas, $0.04 on L1, $0.001 on Base. The Ethereum Attestation Service can record the same without a custom contract; no primary per-attestation gas figure was found.
  2. Full card bytes on chain (0.5–2 KB): roughly $0.04 as L1 calldata, $0.13–0.40 in L1 contract storage, about a cent on Base.
  3. Token: ERC-721 for a unique card, ERC-1155 if copies in decks are the unit.

Lazy claiming is cheaper still: the operator anchors a root covering all mints, and a card becomes a token only when someone pays to claim it with an inclusion proof. Cost is zero until there is demand.

Royalties. ERC-2981 only reports a royalty; nothing enforces it (ERC-2981), and large marketplaces made creator fees optional from 2023 (The Block). ERC-721C enforces fees by restricting transfers to cooperating venues, a shrinking set: Magic Eden ended EVM marketplace support on 2026-03-09 (crypto.news). What works is paying creators at the point of use inside one's own system. For Sim that is convenient: every play passes through one sequencer, so "a play credits the card's first author" can be a kernel rule and a ledger entry with no chain. A chain is needed only to make that credit withdrawable or tradable. Attribution registries such as Story Protocol (IP registry plus a royalty module that splits revenue between parent and derived works, docs) model the same idea on their own chain; their adoption was not assessed.

Precedents for "valuable declarations". Loot (2021, 8,000 free text lists of items) saw about $280M of secondary volume and 100-plus derivatives within a month, then a 95% fall in monthly volume a month later (Decrypt, 2022); a builder community (Realms, Eternum) survived. Nouns auctions one token a day into a treasury its holders govern; on 2026-10-02 the contracts showed 2,011 Nouns and a treasury of 81 ETH, 2,249 wstETH and 163 rETH (read by RPC; other assets not counted). On-chain item and recipe tables (MUD worlds, Pirate Nation, Dojo games) were composable in principle; few outsiders built on them and most hosts have closed. Value followed scarcity by rule plus a treasury or continuing use, not being on chain.

Buys: ownership and composability. Constrains: a card's bytes become frozen public content (decision 6). Engineering: one to two weeks for registry and claim flow. Lock-in: low if the token only points at the content hash.

(c) Fully on-chain "autonomous world" frameworks

These put state and rules in contracts so that the world runs without its makers.

  • MUD (Lattice): state in on-chain tables, logic in Systems behind a World contract, an indexer for clients (mud.dev). Lattice announced on 2026-04-15 that it was winding down; its chain Redstone stopped on 2026-05-15 at 23:59 UTC; after that, only plain-wallet withdrawals through L1 remained possible; MUD stays open source; the DUST world moved to its own chain supported by 0xPARC (TechFlow, ChainCatcher; the original post on X was not read). MUD's main branch was last committed 2025-09-30.
  • Dojo (Cartridge, Starknet): Cairo models and systems, still maintained (core release 2026-05-06, Cartridge Controller 2026-09-08; Eternum and Loot Survivor repositories active in September–October 2026).
  • Others: CCP's EVE Frontier left MUD/Redstone for Sui (announced 2025-10-08, Decrypt); Proof of Play (Pirate Nation) announced its closure on 2026-08-05 and open-sourced its contracts (Cointelegraph).

For Sim this means the kernel rewritten in Solidity or Cairo, card programs interpreted on chain, and every play a transaction. At an assumed 300,000 gas per play on Base (about $0.005), plays alone cost about $14,000 a year today, $49,000 at 25 inhabitants, $390,000 at 200 and $3.9M at 2,000. Models cannot run on chain, so the mid-brain and card compiler stay off-chain actors submitting transactions.

Buys: the strongest neutrality and composability. Costs: a rewrite, the highest lock-in (language, chain, indexer), and dependence on a host that may shut, as Redstone did.

(d) Verifiable replay without consensus

Publish the log, the rule versions and a deterministic replayer; anyone recomputes the state hash and compares it with the signed checkpoint.

  • Plain re-execution. Already works internally. To make it public: pin the replayer by commit and lockfile hash, publish segments and checkpoints. A year of history replays in minutes today and hours at 200 inhabitants; verifiers can start from any checkpointed state. Cost: storage only. A dispute is social: someone shows a mismatch.
  • Optimistic challenge with a bond. The operator posts state roots and a bond; a challenger who proves a wrong root, by bisecting to one event executed on chain, takes the bond. Cartesi's fraud-provable RISC-V machine could run the JavaScript replayer unchanged; its mainnet test deployment is Stage 2 on L2BEAT but secures about $1,500. Engineering: months.
  • Validity (zk) proof of the fold. zkVMs (SP1, RISC Zero, ZisK, Jolt) prove Rust or C programs; ethproofs.org shows about $0.006 per proof of a whole Ethereum block. No zkVM example running JavaScript was found, so this means a Rust port of the fold that must never diverge from the TypeScript one: months of work; proving cost unmeasured.

Buys: verifiability, rising to trust-minimised correctness. Not neutrality of ordering: the operator still chooses what enters the log. Merkleised state commitments can be added later, because state is derived from history.

(e) Appchain or rollup as the ledger

Run a chain whose blocks are event batches: an OP Stack or Arbitrum Orbit rollup through a provider, or a sovereign rollup posting to Celestia (ev-node and the Sovereign SDK both had commits on 2026-09-30).

  • Cost: Conduit's plans are "contact for pricing" plus 5% of sequencer profit; its last public price (2023) was $3,000 a month for mainnet. Caldera's pricing page returned 404. Self-run: a node, data-availability fees (about $1,100 a year for 53 GB at Celestia's 30-day average) and operations.
  • Why it is premature: a chain adds consensus on ordering and a bridge for assets. Sim has one sequencer by design and no assets; forced inclusion and withdrawals matter only once cards carry money. The same log format can later become a rollup's input.

5. Comparison

Yearly cost at today's prices for 7 / 200 / 2,000 inhabitants (1.9 / 53 / 530 GB raw, all events).

Option Immutable Neutral Ownable Cost per year Effort Lock-in
Daily anchor: OpenTimestamps + Base (+ L1) yes no no $0.30 (+ $10 for L1), flat days none
Log copies on R2 no no no under $1 / about $5 / about $50 in year one done none
Log on Arweave (Turbo is 1.56×) yes no no $100 / $2,800 / $28,000 raw; divide by the compression ratio days none
Log as L1 calldata yes no no $47,000 / $1.3M / $13M days low
Card registry on Base, lazy claim cards no yes near zero until claimed; $0.001 per claim 1–2 weeks low
Fully on-chain world on Base yes yes yes $14,000 / $390,000 / $3.9M rewrite high

Bonded challenge or zk proof yes partly no small compute plus contracts months moderate
Appchain yes partly yes provider fee or a self-run node months high

6. What in the log format matters today

Ranked by how hard each is to fix later.

  1. Timestamps cannot be backdated. History before the first anchor can only be proven to have existed by that anchor's date.
  2. Signatures cannot be added to the past. Without them the ledger says "the operator states that X did Y". Actions chosen by server-side models can only ever carry the operator's signature; the record should say so.
  3. Short hashes become public identities. A 64-bit hash can be collided with about 2^32 work, trivial on a laptop: harmless on a trusted server, unacceptable for something ownable. Full hashes of existing cards can be recomputed now at no cost; it gets awkward once anything external refers to the short form.
  4. Encoding must be byte-identical everywhere. stableStringify is close to RFC 8785 (JSON Canonicalization Scheme); every later proof system or second implementation depends on it being frozen.
  5. Event hashes and ordering can be back-filled over the existing log at any time, so they are cheap now and cheap later; an RFC 6962-style Merkle tree gives compact inclusion and consistency proofs and supports lazy card claims.
  6. What is inside the hash. In: seq, tick, kind, author, payload, rules version. Out: rendered words and model transcripts (depiction). Wall-clock at: in and never read by the fold, or out.
  7. User-written text inside hashed, published payloads becomes permanent (decision 6).

SHA-256 suits all of this: Bitcoin and OpenTimestamps use it, and the EVM has a cheap precompile for it.

7. Staged recommendation

This month: near-zero cost, keeps every door open. Roughly a week of engineering, under $15 a year in fees.

  1. Canonical serialisation. Specify stableStringify as the canonical form, add cross-language test vectors, ban non-integer numbers in hashed payloads.
  2. Content addressing. Switch card and state hashes to the full 32 bytes; recompute existing ones; keep the 16-character form for display only.
  3. Hash chaining. Compute an event hash for every event (back-filled over the existing log) and maintain a Merkle tree over them; record the root in each snapshot.
  4. Author signatures. Add an optional sig field to the event envelope; sign owner-originated events once the key type is chosen (decision 3); mark model-originated events as operator-attested.
  5. Periodic anchoring. A daily checkpoint (event count, log root, state hash, rules version, replayer hash) signed by the operator, stamped with OpenTimestamps (free) and written to a minimal contract on Base (about $0.30 a year). Add an L1 write (about $10 a year) only if decision 5 favours Ethereum.
  6. Publication. Publish checkpoints, proofs and log segments (tlog-tiles layout) at a public address, and turn verify-kernel.ts into a documented public verifier. Keep R2 as the store.

Defer, with the trigger for each:

  • Arweave archive until outside parties depend on the record; at today's 7 inhabitants it would be about $100 a year raw.
  • Card registry with lazy claims on one L2 until there is an audience that wants to hold cards. Before then, attribution credit per play lives in the kernel.
  • Bonded challenge or zk proof of the fold until the world holds value someone would cheat for.
  • A rollup only if neutrality of ordering becomes a requirement.
  • Not recommended: porting the kernel to MUD or Dojo. The price is a rewrite, a per-play fee rising to hundreds of thousands of dollars a year at 200 inhabitants, and dependence on an ecosystem that contracted sharply in 2025–2026.

8. Decisions for the owner

Needed before this month's work:

  1. Do decisions belong in the public record? They are 61% of events. Keeping them hashed and published preserves full auditability of model behaviour; keeping only plays, mints and other kernel events cuts storage 2.5×.
  2. Publish the full log, or only commitments? Public replay requires public payloads; commitments alone give immutability without verifiability.
  3. Identity and key type for authors. Wallet keys (secp256k1 with EIP-712 signatures; chain-friendly; the users table already has a wallet column) or Ed25519 keys held by owners' agents. Which event kinds must be signed.

Needed before any registry or token:

  1. Who is a card's first author when the compiler model wrote it: the Soul, the owner whose wish triggered it, or both.
  2. Anchor venues. OpenTimestamps only, or also an EVM chain, and which one. The choice signals where cards would later live.
  3. Free text in the permanent record: accept permanence, or separate text from hashed payloads.
  4. Attribution versus ownership. Attribution can be permanent and non-transferable while ownership is tradable; which one earns credit per play.
  5. Retiring an owned card (open in doc 18) needs a rule.
  6. Succession (open since doc 03): who may sequence if Eden stops. Publishing log, rules and replayer makes a fork possible; it does not say which fork is canonical.

9. Not verified

  • Gas per anchor, registry entry and on-chain play are standard-cost estimates, not measured deployments; no primary figure for an EAS attestation on Base was found.
  • growthepie's data unit was assumed to be GB.
  • Lattice details come from two secondary reports (ChainCatcher's 2026-05-16 07:59 is probably the same instant in UTC+8); the original post was not read.
  • JSON compression ratio and zk proving cost are unmeasured; the replay rate is one measurement on one machine.
  • Rollup-provider prices are not public; the $3,000 a month figure is from 2023.
  • Glamsterdam's mainnet date and scope are not fixed. Nouns figures exclude other treasury assets and staking-token exchange rates.

R4 · Cheap dialogue

R4 — Cheaper in-character dialogue

3,054 words · R4-CHEAP-DIALOGUE.md · updated 2026-10-02

In short

How to make in-character speech 10–100× cheaper: trimmed requests, rendering only what is seen, two model tiers, line banks, batching. A ranked plan for 200 inhabitants and a $5 experiment.

Contents
  • 1. Short answerOne exchange costs about $0.0022 on Claude Haiku 4.5 (about 1,250 input tokens at $1 per million plus 190 output tokens at $5 per million): $2.20 per 1,000.
  • 2. The current requestFrom the Sim repository (packages/brain/src/talk.ts, apps/server/src/brain.ts, apps/server/src/governor.ts), read 2026-10-02:
  • 3. OptionsCosts are per 1,000 exchanges. "Full" is the current request (1,250 in / 190 out); "trimmed" is the estimated plain-text request of section 3.2 (550 in / 90 out).
  • 4. Ranked plan for 200 inhabitants on $1–5 a dayTrim the request (3.2). Hours of work; 2.2× on every call; no change of model.
  • 5. One cheap first experimentAim: choose the ambient model and measure the three unknowns that set the budget.
  • 6. Not verifiedThe share of plays that are speech acts, the fraction of exchanges ever seen and the 10% live-render assumption; demand at 200 inhabitants depends on all three.
  • SourcesAnthropic: https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/about-claude/pricing ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-caching ; https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/batch-processing

Research date: 2026-10-02. Prices were read on that date from the providers' own pages or APIs unless marked otherwise. Context: 18-KERNEL-CARDS-AND-SELF-CHANGE.md section 5.

Terms used below. A speech act (greet, ask, agree, decline, invite, tell, chat) is a play recorded in Sim's event log; it is world state. The words are written afterwards by a language model, stored beside the record and never replayed, so they are decoration: skipping, delaying or replacing them never changes the world. An exchange is one rendering call: one line for most acts, three or four alternating lines for chat. A native is an inhabitant with no human owner; an owned Soul has one. Token prices are in US dollars per million tokens (input / output).

1. Short answer

One exchange costs about $0.0022 on Claude Haiku 4.5 (about 1,250 input tokens at $1 per million plus 190 output tokens at $5 per million): $2.20 per 1,000. A budget of $1–5 a day buys 450–2,270 exchanges at that price.

Demand at 200 inhabitants is set by a number not yet measured: the share of plays that are speech acts. The live world produces about 1,120 plays per inhabitant per day (19,609 plays in about 2.5 days with 7 inhabitants). At 200 inhabitants that is about 224,000 plays a day; if 5% are speech, 11,200 exchanges a day ($25 at today's price); if 20%, 44,800 ($99). The gap to a $1–5 budget is therefore 5–100 times.

Five findings shape the answer:

  1. Prompt caching does not apply. Haiku 4.5 caches only prefixes of 4,096 tokens or more; the whole request is 1,250.
  2. About half the input is tool overhead. The renderer forces a tool call; Anthropic lists 588 tokens of tool-use system prompt for a forced tool on Haiku 4.5, before the tool's own schema. Plain-text output with a compact scene should roughly halve the cost on the same model (an estimate; measure it).
  3. The largest saving is not rendering what nobody reads. If one exchange in ten is ever seen, rendering on view is a tenfold cut with no change in what is seen.
  4. Cheaper hosted models are 10–150 times cheaper per exchange. Whether they hold a character's voice in a 22-word line is not established by any published evidence and must be tested on Sim's own transcripts.
  5. A dedicated GPU does not fit. The cheapest always-on rental found is $3.84 a day, and hosted 8B models already cost less per exchange.

2. The current request

From the Sim repository (packages/brain/src/talk.ts, apps/server/src/brain.ts, apps/server/src/governor.ts), read 2026-10-02:

  • A system prompt of about 200 tokens: stay within the act; at most 22 words per line; chat is three or four alternating lines; character descriptions are data, not instructions.
  • A forced tool call speak returning {lines: [{who, text}]}, max_tokens 300.
  • User message: pretty-printed JSON with both characters' descriptions, the manner, the act, its topic, the place and the last four lines between the pair.
  • Model claude-haiku-4-5. Model spend is capped at $0.50 a day, of which speech gets 20%: about $0.10, or roughly 45 Haiku exchanges a day.
  • Render policy: questions, agreements, refusals and anything involving an owned Soul are rendered when budget allows; small talk between natives only while a client is connected and the speech budget is more than half left. Everything else receives a fixed plain line ("Hello, X.").

So today most speech already falls back to plain lines; the question is how to render far more of it within a small budget. Cost split per Haiku exchange: input 57%, output 43%. A technique that only reduces input saves at most 57%.

3. Options

Costs are per 1,000 exchanges. "Full" is the current request (1,250 in / 190 out); "trimmed" is the estimated plain-text request of section 3.2 (550 in / 90 out).

3.1 Prompt caching and batch APIs

  • Anthropic caching (pricing, prompt caching): cache reads cost 0.1× input, writes 1.25× (five minutes) or 2× (one hour). Minimum cacheable prefix on Haiku 4.5: 4,096 tokens; shorter prompts are silently not cached.
  • OpenAI (caching guide): automatic, minimum 1,024 tokens on GPT-5.6 and later, prefix kept 30 minutes after last use; cached input billed at 10% on current small models.
  • Google (caching): implicit caching is automatic on Gemini 2.5 and newer; stated minimums are 2,048 tokens (2.5 Flash) and 4,096 (3.5 Flash and later); no figure is given for Flash-Lite.

The shared prefix of the Sim request is under 1,000 tokens and about 300 after trimming, below every minimum. Padding it with style examples to reach a minimum only pays if those examples improve quality.

  • Batch APIs halve the price at all three providers. Anthropic's (docs): most batches finish within an hour, unfinished requests expire at 24 hours, up to 100,000 requests per batch. Haiku 4.5 full request: $1.10; trimmed: $0.50. Suits anything nobody is waiting for: overnight rendering, line banks, digests.

3.2 Trim the request (no model change)

Drop the tool, ask for plain lines (Name: words) parsed by the existing cleanLines, send the scene as compact text rather than pretty-printed JSON, and shorten descriptions. Estimated result: 550 input and 90 output tokens, $1.00 on Haiku 4.5 (2.2× cheaper). Risk: occasional format slips, caught by the parser, with the plain line as fallback. Effort: hours. The token counts are estimates until measured.

3.3 Smaller hosted models

Model $ per million in / out Full Trimmed Source
Claude Haiku 4.5 1.00 / 5.00 $2.20 $1.00 Anthropic
Gemini 3.5 Flash-Lite 0.30 / 2.50 $0.85 $0.39 Google
Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite 0.25 / 1.50 $0.60 $0.27 Google
GPT-5.4 nano 0.20 / 1.25 $0.49 $0.22 OpenAI
Fireworks serverless, any 4–16B model 0.20 / 0.20 $0.29 $0.13 Fireworks
GPT-6 luna 0.10 / 0.50 $0.22 $0.10 OpenAI
Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite 0.10 / 0.40 $0.20 $0.09 Google
GPT-OSS 20B on Groq (1,000 tokens/s) 0.075 / 0.30 $0.15 $0.07 Groq
Qwen3.5-9B 0.10 / 0.15 $0.15 $0.07 DeepInfra
GPT-5 nano 0.05 / 0.40 $0.14 $0.06 OpenAI
Gemma 3 27B 0.08 / 0.16 $0.13 $0.06 DeepInfra
Gemma 3 12B 0.05 / 0.15 $0.09 $0.04 DeepInfra
Mistral Small 24B 0.05 / 0.08 $0.08 $0.03 DeepInfra
Llama 3.1 8B 0.02 / 0.04 $0.03 $0.015 DeepInfra
Mistral Nemo (12B) 0.019 / 0.03 $0.03 $0.013 DeepInfra

Sources: Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, Fireworks, Groq, DeepInfra; all read 2026-10-02. The OpenRouter model list (same day) shows similar or slightly higher lowest prices for the open models.

Notes. GPT-5 nano and GPT-OSS are reasoning models: hidden reasoning tokens are billed as output, so their real cost must be measured with reasoning set to its minimum. "GPT-6 luna" is on OpenAI's price list with no description. Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite has no announced shutdown date, but Google's deprecations page recommends 3.5 Flash-Lite for new projects and already restricts other 2.5 models to past users; treat it as a model that may disappear. Groq lists Llama models under enterprise pricing.

Latency: well under the current 20-second timeout; Groq lists 560–1,000 tokens per second. Effort: about a day to add a second provider behind the existing callTool interface. The open question is quality (section 3.4).

3.4 Self-hosted small models

  • GPU rental (RunPod): RTX A5000 $0.16 an hour (community cloud), RTX 3090 $0.22, RTX 4090 $0.34, L4 $0.44. Always on, that is $3.84–8.16 a day. Serverless 24 GB workers cost $0.69–1.10 an hour, billed per second, with cold starts. Hetzner's dedicated GPU servers (GEX45 with 24 GB, GEX131 with 96 GB) did not show prices on its page; secondary sources give about €184 a month for the older GEX44.
  • Throughput (secondary benchmarks): about 1,490 tokens per second in total for Llama 3.1 8B on one RTX A6000 with 50 concurrent requests (databasemart); about 2,800 on a 4090 at batch 32 (a source that could not be fetched). That is 8–15 full exchanges a second, so 45,000 exchanges need 50–95 minutes of GPU time a day.
  • Cost: an always-on GPU alone takes most or all of the budget, while hosted 8B models cost $0.015–0.03 per 1,000. Self-hosting has no price advantage at this scale. Its value is serving custom weights and control. CPU inference (about 10–15 tokens per second for 2–3B models, secondary) is too slow, and the production box runs the simulation. An already-owned GPU machine would change this arithmetic; none was assessed.
  • Persona quality in 1–8B models: published evidence is thin and addresses harder tasks. A 2025 paper fine-tunes DistilGPT-2, TinyLlama 1.1B and Mistral 7B to fixed personas with LoRA on about 150 synthetic pairs per character (arXiv 2511.10277). Persona-Pruner (ICML 2026, arXiv 2606.14695) reports that persona-guided pruning recovers most of the role-play score lost by naive pruning. Persona fidelity declines over 100-plus-turn dialogues (arXiv 2512.12775). Sim's task is easier: one short line whose content is fixed by the act.

3.5 Fine-tuning a small model

Every rendered exchange is already stored with its request, so the world produces a training set as a by-product.

  • Training: Fireworks charges $0.50 per million training tokens for LoRA on models up to 16B and states that fine-tuned models are served "for the same price as base models" (pricing). Together charges $0.34 per million for models up to 9B with a $4 minimum per job; its pricing page does not mention serverless serving of fine-tunes (pricing). OpenAI fine-tunes GPT-4.1 nano at $1.50 per million training tokens, with inference at $0.20 / $0.80, double the base model's $0.10 / $0.40.
  • Five thousand exchanges are about 7.5 million training tokens: $2.55–3.75 on Together or Fireworks, $11 on OpenAI.
  • A tuned model no longer needs the instruction block, so the request shrinks to the scene (about 300 in / 90 out): $0.08 per 1,000 on Fireworks, $0.13 on tuned GPT-4.1 nano.
  • Risks: the student copies one teacher's style, so voices converge; newly minted acts fall outside the training set; one more model to version and re-evaluate. Worth doing only after section 3.3 shows where stock small models fail.

3.6 Line banks and grammars

  • Line banks. For each character, a larger model writes a few hundred lines keyed by act, manner and familiarity, with slots for listener, topic and place. Estimated one-off cost on Haiku 4.5 (1,500 in / 4,000 out): about $0.02 per character, half through the batch API; about $2 to refresh 200 characters by batch. Runtime cost and latency: zero.
  • Grammars in the Tracery style expand a few fragments into many surface forms (8 openers × 8 bodies × 6 closers is 384 lines from 22 fragments). A small local model can choose among expansions under constrained decoding, so output stays inside the authored space while reacting to context (Boris the Brave, 2025, with a 0.5B model).
  • What makes it read as canned is meeting the same line twice. Track lines seen per viewer and per pair, refresh banks weekly, and keep a model for chat and for topics a bank has no slot for. Topic strings need a fixed grammatical form for slots to read correctly; that is a rule for card authors and the compiler.

The existing plain-line fallback is already a one-entry bank. Effort: about a week.

3.7 Render on view

  • Render at play time only when a client is watching that place or a participant's owner is online.
  • Otherwise store the act and render on first read (one to two seconds, with the bank line shown meanwhile), or never.
  • For an owner returning after hours, summarise unseen conversations in one call: about 60 input and 20 output tokens per summarised exchange, $0.16 per 1,000 on Haiku 4.5, $0.08 by batch.

Cost then scales with the fraction of exchanges ever seen, which is unknown and the most valuable number to measure. Quality risk: none for what is seen. Effort: days.

3.8 Several exchanges in one call

Render the exchanges in one place over a short window in one structured call, paying the instruction block once. With about 900 shared tokens, 300 per scene and 120 output tokens each, eight exchanges cost about $1.00 per 1,000 on Haiku 4.5: the same saving as trimming, overlapping with it. Research on batch prompting reports up to five times fewer tokens at comparable accuracy with up to six items per call (Cheng et al., 2023). Risks: voices bleeding between conversations, one failure losing several results, waiting for the window.

3.9 Generation in the viewer's browser

WebLLM on WebGPU runs 1–3B models (a 4-bit Llama 3.2 1B is a 695 MB download) at 20–90 tokens per second on recent laptops (secondary, pinggy.io; arXiv 2412.15803 reports up to 80% of native speed). Chrome's Prompt API runs Gemini Nano on desktop only, needing 22 GB free disk and more than 4 GB of video memory or 16 GB of RAM (Chrome docs). Cost to Sim: zero. Limits: no phones, a large first download, the weakest models, and different viewers seeing different words for one act. An optional enhancement at most.

4. Ranked plan for 200 inhabitants on $1–5 a day

  1. Trim the request (3.2). Hours of work; 2.2× on every call; no change of model.
  2. Render on view (3.7). Live rendering only for exchanges a client is watching or that involve an online owner; bank or plain lines for the rest; digests for returning owners. Saves a factor equal to one over the fraction seen.
  3. Two model tiers (3.3). Trimmed Haiku 4.5 for exchanges that matter (questions, agreements, refusals involving an owned Soul); a model at $0.013–0.10 per 1,000 trimmed for everything else, chosen by the experiment in section 5.
  4. Line banks (3.6) for natives' formulaic acts (greetings, agreements, refusals), authored and refreshed through the batch API.
  5. Batch everything nobody waits for (3.1): banks, digests, any back-fill of words.
  6. Later: a small model fine-tuned on Sim's own transcripts and served at base price (3.5); scene calls for crowded places (3.8); in-browser rendering on capable desktops (3.9).

Not recommended now: an always-on GPU, CPU inference on the production box, prompt caching.

Worked budget, the hard case of 44,800 exchanges a day (20% of plays are speech), assuming 10% are rendered live:

Item Volume / day Unit cost $ / day
Owned-Soul exchanges that matter, trimmed Haiku 1,000 $1.00 / 1,000 1.00
Other live exchanges, cheap model 3,480 $0.015–0.10 / 1,000 0.05–0.35
Unseen exchanges, bank or plain line 40,320 0 0
Digests for returning owners, batch about 5,000 summarised $0.08 / 1,000 0.40
Weekly line-bank refresh for 200 characters, batch 1/7 $2 0.30
Total about 1.8–2.1

The same policy with every exchange rendered (owned-Soul ones on trimmed Haiku, the rest on a $0.015–0.10 model) costs about $1.6–5.4 a day, at or above the top of the budget. At 5% speech share everything scales down by four. The gap from today's $99 to about $2 is a factor of about 50, most of it from rendering on view and moving ambient talk to a cheaper model.

5. One cheap first experiment

Aim: choose the ambient model and measure the three unknowns that set the budget.

  1. Take 200 stored speech requests from the brain_calls table, stratified across acts and across natives and owned Souls.
  2. Re-render each with six arms: the current Haiku request; trimmed Haiku; Gemini 2.5 Flash-Lite (trimmed); GPT-5 nano or GPT-6 luna (trimmed, minimal reasoning); Llama 3.1 8B or Mistral Nemo on DeepInfra (trimmed); a per-character line bank.
  3. Score each line automatically: - cost: real input, output and reasoning tokens per call, converted to dollars per 1,000; - format: share of responses cleanLines cannot parse; - act fidelity: a judge model asked whether the line adds a promise, plan or fact not in the act; report the violation rate; - voice: a judge shown the line and four character descriptions picks the speaker; report the hit rate; - latency: median and 95th percentile.
  4. The owner ranks 40 randomly chosen sets blind, without model labels.
  5. Adopt a cheap model for ambient talk if its format failures are at most 2%, its act violations at most 3% and no more than twice trimmed Haiku's, and its voice hit rate within 10 points of trimmed Haiku's.
  6. In the same pass, query the database for: the share of plays that are speech acts; the share of stored talks rows ever fetched by a non-admin client (add a fetch counter if this is not recorded); and the real token counts of today's calls.

Cost: under $5 in model calls and about an afternoon. The result fixes the demand figure, the fraction seen and the per-exchange price in section 4.

6. Not verified

  • The share of plays that are speech acts, the fraction of exchanges ever seen and the 10% live-render assumption; demand at 200 inhabitants depends on all three.
  • The trimmed token counts (550 / 90) and the saving from dropping the tool.
  • Any quality measurement of cheap models on this task; the cited papers address longer, harder role-play.
  • Self-hosted throughput (secondary benchmarks) and Hetzner GPU prices.
  • Gemini caching minimums for Flash-Lite models; reasoning-token overhead on GPT-5 nano and GPT-OSS; whether Fireworks serves LoRA fine-tunes of every 4–16B model serverlessly.
  • Chrome Prompt API's stable version for web pages (sources say 138 or 148).

R5 · The Mars campus, measured

R5 — The Mars engine on the real 2026 campus

3,131 words · R5-MARS-CAMPUS-PROBE.md · updated 2026-10-02

In short

The real 2026 campus through the engine: geography, walks between buildings, 25 to 200 people, furnishing, places, existing scenarios, and a ready adapter configuration.

  • 4. FurnishingUsable fixtures with body slots: bench-6ft (3 seats), camp-chair (1 seat), campfire (8 fire-circle places on a 1.6 m ring), speaker-lectern (1 standing presenter place).

Mars commit at start and at end: ad58b58 Fri Oct 2 11:49:56 2026 -0400 (unchanged during the probe). Nothing in ~/Mars or was modified. Scripts (Node 26.8.2, Apple-silicon laptop, one process at a time except where noted) are copied with their JSON outputs to eden4/research/_handoff/campus-scripts/. Every number below names the script that produced it.

1. Geography

g1-geography.ts, g1b-labels.ts, points.ts. The file viewer/public/engine/campus-2026.json (1.42 MB) covers x −162…96, z −147…69 (258 × 216 m), ground y = −0.16. It holds 9,497 static obstacles (4,657 solid, 3,104 fixture, 1,174 vehicle, 518 wall, 44 glass), 9 regions, and after loadCampus2026 six generic people and no fixture entities. There is one level (0, ground), one surface and no links: no decks, stairs or walkable upper floors; the 2,419 "upper" prisms are roofs and lofts, obstacles only. The 0.1 m grid is 2,580 × 2,160 = 5.57 M cells and takes 735 ms to build. 53,408 m² is standable (clearance ≥ 0.34 m); all of it is one connected area except 27 cells.

Region Kind Centroid Area m² Standable m² Verified arrival point Clearance
chiba place 0.00, −1.54 711.5 229.1 (32 %) 0.45, −1.35 0.80
north-workshops place −32.86, −69.68 590.8 316.2 (54 %) −29.85, −67.95 0.81
north-rack-building place 4.54, −83.28 63.7 4.0 (6 %) 4.55, −77.95 0.85
northeast-compound place 46.29, −88.87 320.1 145.4 (45 %) 46.25, −88.85 1.16
west-rack-building place −116.91, 0.30 569.7 409.3 (72 %) −116.95, 0.25 0.80
staging-east staging 28, −9 352 352 (100 %) 27.95, −8.95 18.9
pile-uprights / -beams / -timber material-pile 28,9 / 27,−26 / 15,−26 76 / 42 / 77 100 % centroids 5–12.7

All regions are level 0 with no audience layout. Place regions are convex hulls of building assemblies; the other four are hypothetical clearings for the flagship build. Inside the hulls, roofed interiors are blocked: Chiba's open ground is its east court (x 1…12, z −6…4) and an east lane. The 4 m ASCII map is printed by g1-geography.ts; in short, Chiba sits at the origin, staging and the piles just east of it, the north workshops, the Dojo (north rack building) and the Freeside/CoLab compound 70–100 m north, the west rack building (OG) 117 m west.

2. Movement

g2-movement.ts, g2b-routes.ts, g6-spawn.ts. All 30 ordered walks among the five place regions and staging, one walker at a time, arrival point to arrival point: 30/30 planned and completed, 0 violations, end error 0.00 m.

Measure Value
Path length avg 99.6 m, max 194.7 m (northeast ↔ west)
Length / straight line avg 1.07, max 1.29 (Chiba → west, round Chiba's south side)
Smallest route clearance 0.35 m
Ticks to arrive avg 1,493 (75 s), max 2,902 (145 s); 1.28–1.34 m/s
walk_to.plan, exact planner avg 56.9 ms, median 29.6, p95 209, worst 263 ms (908,573 expansions)
Corridor planner, longest route 33 ms vs 144 ms exact, same length

Routes go round buildings; none crosses a blocked footprint. Refusals seen: route.goal-blocked at the centroids of north-workshops (blocked by wood_component-1948) and north-rack-building (interior:wood_component-1902) and outside the bounds; route.unknown-level for level 1 or 2; entity.unknown; action.invalid-params (speed > 1.4); and route.search-limit (4 M expansions, 1.2 s per plan) for goals in cut-off pockets — the early unreachability proof only runs on grids of ≤ 4 M cells. On a 1.49 M-cell crop the same goal is refused as route.unreachable in 316 ms (g6-spawn.ts).

Nobody can reach an upper level as the campus stands. Upper levels exist only when a scenario builds them: the flagship takes (takes/flagship-campus-2026.json, build-bay-v3-campus-2026.json) build a rack bay, deck and stair at staging-east and end with a visitor on the deck (asserted in test/campus-flagship.test.ts, read, not run). climb_frame was not tried: campus racks are static CAD prisms, not rack entities.

3. People

g3-crowd.ts <N> <local|campus> 5 [planner]: N people round six hubs, each walking again and again (2–8 s pauses) to a random standable point — local: within 25 m of its own hub; campus: within 18 m of a random hub, so most walks cross the campus. 5 simulated minutes = 6,000 ticks; the probe does not prune records or events.

Tick worst ms 160 314 520 226 504 888 628

Restore (new Spatial + grid) took 900 ms (25) and 1,073 ms (200 local); both restored copies matched the original hash after 1 and 201 ticks. Snapshots grow with the unpruned record/event history; the Sim adapter already prunes events above 20,000 and records two minutes after they end, which will cut them.

The profiler (25 local) shows every tick over 50 ms is a people-replan: spatial.gridsAround copies the whole campus rasters (~50 MB per copy, up to 8 cached), ~150 ms each, which also explains the memory. At 200 people the average tick (29–36 ms) is over half the 50 ms budget and 9–15 % of ticks overrun it, so real time holds to roughly 100 people on the full grid. Deadlock recovery reported stalls and cycles at 200 people but nobody collided. The corridor planner cuts submit cost by 2.6× but produced far more deadlock.stalled time.

A person added the adapter's way (human(id, x, z) with no y, so y = 0 on a −0.16 campus) raises one motion.teleport violation ("moved 0.16 m in one tick") when it first walks, then stays at −0.16 (g6b-y0.ts). Spawned at y = −0.16 there is none. A person placed inside an obstacle raises collision.human-static every tick (g4-furnish.ts), so spawn points must be checked.

4. Furnishing

g4-furnish.ts, g6-spawn.ts, g6b-y0.ts.

Usable fixtures with body slots: bench-6ft (3 seats), camp-chair (1 seat), campfire (8 fire-circle places on a 1.6 m ring), speaker-lectern (1 standing presenter place). Obstacle fixtures without body slots: table-6ft (18 surface slots), computer-workstation (workstation capability, needs a chair), standing-display, garden-planter, and construction stock bundles/stacks. chess-board and chess-tray are non-obstacle surfaces.

Placed: a campfire, two benches and two chairs in staging-east; a bench and table in Chiba's east court; a chair at the northeast-compound arrival point (clearance 1.16 m).

Test Result
Fixture added to world.entities mid-run picked up with no rebuild: sp.epoch 1 → 2, clearance at the table 18.8 → 0, a route through it re-routed (16.0 → 16.3 m, passes 1.96 m away), first sync 49 ms; removing it restored 18.8
Fixture y = 0 instead of −0.16 navigation identical; but the validating editor refuses it (below)
sit on bench-n#seat1 occupied after 387 ticks; second claim of the seat → reservation.conflict; stand releases it
sit {fixture: chair} (staging and the tight NE point) both occupied
campfire_circle, 10 people 8 occupied after 492 ticks; 2 failed slot.none-free
gather {fixture: bench}, 2 people gathered after 342 ticks
gather {region: 'chiba'} slot.none "region chiba (place) has no audience layout"
Same region given layout: {focus: [6, −1]} gathers (slot chiba#r7c13, at 9.81, −7.62) in 182 ticks
New place region with layout over the fire (8 × 8 m) 12 of 12 gathered in 511 ticks, 0 violations
converse, 3 people in Chiba's east lane talking
converse at a point 0.6 m from a wall converse.clearance
Violations in the furnished run (1,954 ticks) none except a deliberately misplaced person

world.obstacleEdits is read only when services are built: setting { 'wood_component-1948': null } on a live world changed nothing (still route.goal-blocked); a new Runtime built from the same world (986 ms) cleared the cell and the centroid walk planned. The validating path, api/edit.ts applyEdits, behaves so:

Edit Result
spawn on the canonical world edit.inventory-finite — needs inventory: 'unlimited'
spawn campfire at y −0.16 ok
at y 0 edit.unsupported (floor tolerance 0.05 m)
bench overlapping a CAD prism edit.blocked
bench over the bounds edit.off-floor
region with layout ok
static_add wall ok, lands in obstacleEdits
static_remove wood_component-1948 edit.static-support (it supports another piece)

Each spawn/static edit costs 830–980 ms because applyEdits builds a fresh whole-campus Spatial.

A furnishing file should therefore be a list of applyEdits edits applied once at genesis: spawn ops (id, catalog def, pose {p: [x, −0.16, z], yaw}), region ops for gathering places (polygon, kind: 'place', layout {focus, spacing ≥ 0.7, rowGap ≥ 0.7}), and optional static_add/update/remove. Constraints: inventory must be unlimited for the edit (or the result kept with finite afterwards); y on the floor within 0.05 m; footprint clear of every CAD prism and inside the bounds; seat yaw = entity yaw + π/2 with a free approach 0.35–0.5 m beyond the front edge; static edits need a Spatial rebuild (~1 s) and cannot remove supporting pieces.

5. Regions as places

g5-regions.ts, g5b-subplaces.ts, g5c-reach.ts. 40,000 uniform samples: 95.9 % standable; of those, 96.95 % lie in no region and 97.97 % in no place region; no point lies in two regions. The median distance from an uncovered point to the nearest place region is 36 m (p90 73 m, max 137 m). The regions are islands, and two of five place centroids cannot be stood on (g6-spawn.ts: the adapter's centroid walk is refused for both).

Finer names exist in the obstacles' assembly field (33 non-context assemblies). Standable ground and a reachable arrival cell (clearance ≥ 0.6 m, all planned from staging):

Sub-place Standable m² Arrival point Plan
auditorium-2026-furnishings (Chiba) 8.9 3.93, 0.95 26 m

Places should be derived as: an explicit list, each with a verified arrival point (not a centroid), drawn from the five place regions plus the reachable sub-assemblies above; the open ground between them stays "the grounds"; the hypothetical piles are hidden; gathering places get their own small layout regions.

None of these furnishes 2026 with seats, fires or tables; every furnishing must be authored.

7. Proposed adapter configuration and limits

The adapter's MarsWorldConfig.scene is 'proving-ground' | 'meal' and genesis/restore are hard-wired to those, so the campus needs a new branch (not written here):

const MARS_2026_CAMPUS = {
  scene: 'mars-2026',
  sceneFile: 'viewer/public/engine/campus-2026.json',      // loadCampus2026(file, { flagshipInventory: false })
  removeContextPeople: true,                               // the six generic people
  crop: [-50, -110, 60, 25] as [number, number, number, number], // 1.49 M cells, 562 ms build; excludes the west rack building
  planner: 'corridor' as const,                            // 2.6x cheaper submits on long walks
  groundY: -0.16,                                          // pass to human() on spawn
  arrival: [27.95, -8.95] as [number, number],             // staging-east, clearance 18.9 m
  arrivalPlace: 'staging-east',
  reference: [0.45, -1.35] as [number, number],            // Chiba court, for the spawn reachability check
  grounds: { id: 'campus', name: 'the Mars campus' },
  hideRegions: ['pile-uprights', 'pile-beams', 'pile-timber'],
  places: [                                                // explicit points, not centroids
    { id: 'chiba', name: 'Chiba', point: [0.45, -1.35] },
    { id: 'chiba-auditorium', name: 'Chiba auditorium', point: [3.93, 0.95] },
    { id: 'mimos', name: 'Mimos lounge', point: [3.84, -11.04] },
    { id: 'kitchen', name: 'Kitchen', point: [-10.04, -12.42] },
    { id: 'north-workshops', name: 'North workshops', point: [-29.85, -67.95] },
    { id: 'tool-library', name: 'Tool Camp library', point: [-28.88, -81.8] },
    { id: 'dojo', name: 'Dojo (outside)', point: [4.55, -77.95] },
    { id: 'freeside', name: 'Freeside', point: [41.6, -92.56] },
    { id: 'northeast-compound', name: 'Northeast compound', point: [46.25, -88.85] },
    { id: 'staging-east', name: 'The clearing', point: [27.95, -8.95] },
  ],
  furnishing: [                                            // applyEdits ops on an 'unlimited' fork, y = -0.16
    { op: 'spawn', id: 'fire', def: 'campfire', pose: { p: [28, -0.16, -9], yaw: 0 } },
    { op: 'spawn', id: 'bench-n', def: 'bench-6ft', pose: { p: [28, -0.16, -12.2], yaw: 0 } },
    { op: 'spawn', id: 'bench-s', def: 'bench-6ft', pose: { p: [28, -0.16, -5.8], yaw: Math.PI } },
    { op: 'spawn', id: 'chair-w', def: 'camp-chair', pose: { p: [24.8, -0.16, -9], yaw: Math.PI / 2 } },
    { op: 'spawn', id: 'court-bench', def: 'bench-6ft', pose: { p: [6, -0.16, -1], yaw: 0 } },
    { op: 'region', id: 'fire-circle', kind: 'place', polygon: [[24, -13], [32, -13], [32, -5], [24, -5]], layout: { focus: [28, -9], spacing: 1, rowGap: 0.8 } },
  ],
};

With the crop, the West rack building and CoLab huts drop out; including them means the full grid and its costs.

Engine limits, in the order they will bite:

  1. Re-planning round people copies whole-campus rasters: ~150 ms per replan, up to 888 ms ticks and 1–1.5 GB resident at 100–200 people; real time holds to about 100 people on the full grid (§3).
  2. Long exact plans cost 60–260 ms, and the adapter plans 1–8 times before submit plans again.
  3. Unreachable goals stall 1.2 s (route.search-limit) on the full grid; a crop under 4 M cells turns this into a 316 ms route.unreachable.
  4. Centroids are not arrival points; regions cover 3 % of the ground; no region has a layout, so gather on a region fails until one is added.
  5. No fixtures at all; furnishing goes through applyEdits (~1 s per spawn on the full campus) and must use y −0.16; the adapter's own y = 0 spawn raises motion.teleport.
  6. One flat level: no stairs, decks or interiors; upper levels exist only if a build scenario makes them.
  7. obstacleEdits and restores need a new Spatial: 0.9–1.1 s each.

What did not work

  • Walking to the north-workshops and north-rack-building centroids: route.goal-blocked.
  • Any upper level: route.unknown-level; the scene has no links.
  • Plans into cut-off pockets on the full grid: 1.2 s, route.search-limit.
  • Editing world.obstacleEdits on a live world: no effect until services are rebuilt.
  • Spawning a fixture at y = 0 through applyEdits (edit.unsupported), or at all on the canonical finite inventory (edit.inventory-finite); removing a supporting CAD piece (edit.static-support).
  • gather on any campus region (no layout); converse beside a wall (converse.clearance).
  • At 200 people: deadlock stalls (up to 44,806 person-ticks with the corridor planner) and 36–66 people still blocked at the end, though with zero violations.
  • A shell quirk in the first batch run (zsh does not split $a) produced empty runs; they were re-run.
  • Not run: climb_frame; the flagship build test; a crowd run on the cropped scene (g3c-crowd-crop.ts is ready).

1,190 words · STATUS.md · updated 2026-10-01

In short

The single page that says what is true now: live services, what is built, faked or absent, measurements, known limits and next steps.

As of 2026-10-01 (the live build id is at /healthz). Update this file when the state changes; it is the single place that says what is true now.

Live
Production https://sim.eden.art (eden2 Hetzner box), also https://
Staging https:// (fake brain, separate data)
Repo github.com/genekogan/sim (private); CI green
World toy-1 on the toy engine, running continuously since 2026-09-30 05:09 UTC; six native Souls (Oda, Pim, Rowan, Tamsin, Juno, Basil) plus owners' Souls
Access invite codes required to create or steer a Soul; watching is open; one admin (Gene)
Model spend capped at $0.50/day by the governor
Durability Litestream → R2 every 10 s (restore tested), hourly on-box backups, watchdog timer, release folders with rollback

Built

The shell

  • Canonical event log, deterministic fold, replay from genesis, snapshots, restore.
  • 20 Hz tick loop with clock-rate dial, pause, time dilation.
  • WebSocket sync; view-hash heartbeat; resync; resume from a ring buffer.
  • Privy sign-in, invite gate, Souls (several per account), ownership, retirement.
  • Agent interface: installable /skill.md, pairing both ways, per-Soul tokens, REST and MCP.
  • Operations: numbered DB migrations, retention, CI, staging-then-production deploys, rollback, watchdog, R2 replication.

The meaning layer (2026-10-01; design in eden4/18)

  • Kernel: things, links, time and five kinds of change (appear/vanish, set, move, link/unlink, say). Everything else (kinds of thing, kinds of link, manners, cards) is open vocabulary. One rule: an actor changes only itself, what it controls and what it made. The kernel replays from the log with no engine.
  • Cards: named actions with conditions, changes and a depiction. Fifteen base cards; a deck and three manners per Soul; a shared library.
  • Plans: an owner's wish becomes an agenda of cards (of for things that do not exist yet). One-step lookahead finds travel and unblocking by itself; unplayable steps are passed over; a plan impossible for ten minutes is given up.
  • Minting: a compiler (Haiku) writes missing cards; owners' agents can propose cards; every card is validated before it enters the library.
  • Speech: acts (ask, agree, decline…) are state; words are rendered afterwards by a small model and stored beside the record.
  • Minds in three tiers: reflex (code, free), mid-brain (Jev), compiler (Haiku). Plus the narrator per region and the director.
  • Pages: grammar (plays as sentences, things, vocabulary, card library, animation to-do), notebook (open design questions beside live evidence), deck/plan/speech on my soul, overheard speech on watch.

A second engine (2026-10-01; design in eden4/19)

  • packages/engine-mars: the real Mars engine behind Sim's engine contract, imported read-only from a local checkout (never copied into this repo). ENGINE=mars runs the whole shell on it, and one of Mars's own authored scenarios (the meal) runs through it unchanged. Not deployed: the Mars code exists only on Gene's laptop.

Faked or absent

  • Production runs the toy engine: a 100 m plane, seven fixtures, straight-line walking.
  • No rendering. The map is text; camera shots are data.
  • Scenes (several bodies in one coordinated motion: a class, a meal, a carry) are designed (eden4/19 §5), not built.
  • Layered knowing (atlas, news, memory) and self-change proposals (eden4/18 §6, §8) are not built.
  • Not built from the eden4 vision: manna and any economy, building and materials, ground growth, rooms and doors, the daily ritual, generated bodies, NFTs or any chain component, asset storage, moderation.
  • Minds are not canonical. Mood, energy, plan position and think-timers are snapshotted but not reproducible from the log.

Measured

What Value When

One exchange of words (Haiku) ~$0.002 10-01
Narrator plan (Haiku, cache warm) ~$0.003, 3–5 s 09-30

Replay check on production (2026-10-01): the kernel replayed from the live world's 11,939 events in 35 ms and matched the running server's hash (infra/verify-kernel.ts).

Tests: 52 (kernel 6, engine contract 4, toy engine 8, Mars adapter 13, brain 6, server 15). The Mars tests skip where there is no Mars checkout (CI).

Known limits and risks

  • The R2 key in use is eden3's media-bucket writer key (shared bucket, sim/ prefix).
  • An eden3 deploy can drop the Caddy import line that serves Sim.
  • Secrets on eden2 are readable by both root users (Gene and a second admin).
  • Privy free tier ends at 499 monthly users.
  • node:sqlite is still marked experimental in Node 22.
  • Budget and brain mode changed in the admin tab are lost on restart; pairing codes and pending approvals are in memory and are cancelled by a restart.
  • A wish costs about a cent. At $0.50/day the compiler's share covers roughly fifteen wishes a day across all owners; each owner is limited to twelve an hour, after which a cheaper translator handles plain goals.
  • Any card can target anything anywhere; only its own conditions limit reach.
  • Cards cannot be retired or revised once minted. Base cards can be revised for an existing world only by adopting a new base vocabulary (an admin action, logged).
  • The old goal follow now means "go and talk with"; nothing follows continuously.
  • On the Mars engine the director's shot list and the map still assume the toy world's layout.
  • On the Mars engine, only eight verbs resume exactly from a snapshot; the body language uses only those (eden4/19 §6).

Next

  1. Take over the Mars engine (Gene, from 2026-10-02): decide where its code lives; run staging on ENGINE=mars; a furnished campus world; the first scene; a Mars vocabulary pack. Plan in eden4/19 §8.
  2. Layered knowing and memory; self-change proposals (eden4/18 §6, §8).
  3. Cost: a cheap matcher for wishes existing cards already cover; audience-aware cadence.
  4. From the hardening sprint (eden4/16): protocol version negotiation, hot/cold database split, structured metrics, asset store on R2, load Privy only on sign-in.

Waiting on Gene: the questions at the end of eden4/18 and eden4/19. The earlier design discussion is in frontier/.

Reproduced and not yet fixed (do these first)

  1. F1 replay divergence: lapsed links are swept only in the live broadcast path (world.ts:398), changing link order, so a card with a derived slot can bind a different Soul live than in replay (cards.ts:43).
  2. F6 replay divergence: a thing made by a card shares the card's own states object (cards.ts:122, kernel.ts:85); a look-ahead for another Soul can overwrite live state without any event.
  3. F2 decks and manners dealt at birth come from code constants, not the world's pinned vocabulary (fold.ts:48).
  4. F3 a play waiting in the queue at shutdown is lost; the snapshot already marks it applied (world.ts:216, main.ts:70).
  5. F4 a program that fails during its approach sets no avoid; the reflex repeated one impossible decision 1,355 times in 15 simulated minutes (world.ts:225-234).
  6. F5 the plan-blocked timer is computed before the avoid filter, so a plan is never given up (brain.ts:203-208).
  7. F7 Mars adapter: a refused decision leaves the old walk running while the host cleared pending (engine-mars/engine.ts:310).
  8. F8 a plan step with of whose card removes its target never advances (world.ts:294).
  9. F9 a blocked step is skipped when a later one is playable (deck.ts:81): intended, but confirm it is what you want.
