eden4 › The design
Section

The design

How eden4 and Sim are built, and what is proposed but not built. Three notes sit under this page: the kernel and cards (doc 18), the Mars bridge (doc 19) and the dials (doc 13).

Documents in this section
The kernel and cardsThe world's grammar: things, links, time and five kinds of change, with everything else as open vocabulary. Covers cards, decks, plans, minting and speech, what running it with live models taught, and how the world could safely change its own software. 3,351 wordsThe Mars bridgeHow the shell drives the real Mars engine through a body language of nine verbs, how Mars's own activities become vocabulary, the proposed scene layer, and what the takeover has to change in the engine. 2,891 wordsParameters and trade-offsEvery number the world will need (arrival, the ritual, steering and idle life, economy, space, engine), what each end of its range gives, a lean, and whether Gene has shelved it for the community. 1,681 words
This page
  1. The system on one screen
  2. The principles, in one line each
  3. What is built and what is proposed
  4. Open questions
  5. The dials

The system on one screen

Layer What it does Built?
Owners and their agents Say what they want in words, or set an agenda of cards, through the page, REST or MCP. Never act directly. yes
Compiler Turns a wish into an agenda; writes a new card when none fits, validates it and mints it. yes (Haiku)
Minds Choose what a Soul does next: reflex (code, free), mid-brain (Jev, about $0.0001 a choice), narrator per region, director for the camera. yes
Kernel The meaning of the world: things, links, time, five changes. Applies plays under one rule and logs them. Replays with no engine and no model. yes
Body language Nine engine-independent verbs (walk to, sit, gather, converse…) that every card's depiction is written in. yes
Engine Moves bodies. A toy engine in production; the real Mars engine through an adapter on a laptop. toy live; Mars proven locally
Words Rendered after the act by a small model; stored beside the record, never replayed. yes

The principles, in one line each

  • Meaning first. The ledger records what happened; the engine shows it and may lag. (Gene, round 17)
  • Grammar fixed, vocabulary open. Things, links, time and five changes are fixed; kinds, link kinds, manners and cards are content that grows.
  • One rule. An actor changes only itself, what it controls and what it made. Anything shared emerges from separate plays: ask, agree, act.
  • Acts are state, words are decoration. What a Soul says changes what can happen next; how it is worded changes nothing.
  • Steering, not control. Owners give direction; the Soul decides how and when.
  • Shape now, numbers later. Fix what kinds of things exist; leave numbers as dials set by logged rule changes after watching the first community. (Gene, round 8)

What is built and what is proposed

Built and running Proposed, not built
Kernel, cards, decks, agendas, one-step lookahead Scenes (several bodies in one coordinated activity)
Compiler and minting; agents proposing cards Retiring and revising cards
Speech acts and rendered words Memory beyond two minutes; layered knowing (atlas, news)
Reflex, mid-brain, narrator, director tiers Self-change proposals with shadow replay and epochs
Base vocabulary pinned in each world's log Economy, building, ritual, rooms, generated bodies
Mars adapter; DJ story and Mars's meal scenario run on it The furnished Mars campus as a live world

Open questions

From the kernel note (doc 18 §10) and the Mars bridge (doc 19 §9):

  1. Scenes. Is "a kernel thing staged by one engine coordinator, re-staged from the kernel after a restart" the right way to keep Mars's choreography without making it the truth? Ideas for the kernel gives a design for the meaning side.
  2. Body language. Is a small fixed set of body verbs the right middle layer, with raw engine verbs allowed only in one world's own vocabulary?
  3. Furnishing. Authored file, or built by Souls through cards with a file only as the starting state?
  4. The engine's home after the takeover.
  5. Cards over time. Who may retire a card; can a card be improved without changing what past plays meant? Second Life's immutable versions and lineage links are the strongest answer found.
  6. Things that last, locality, memory, the cost of wishes. See doc 18 §10.

The dials

Doc 13 lists every number the world will need (arrival rate, the ritual, steering and idle life, economy, space, engine) with what each end of its range gives and whether Gene has shelved it for the community to settle. Its method is the reason most numbers in eden4 are still open on purpose.

eden4 › Ideas for the kernel
Section

Ideas for the kernel

What fifty years of planning systems, social simulations, agent theory and LLM-agent worlds say about Sim's kernel. Distilled from R1 and its illustrated tour; fourteen systems, from STRIPS (1971) to Infinite Craft (2024).

Documents in this section
R1 · Prior art for the kernelPlanning operators, social simulation engines, speech acts and commitments, LLM-agent worlds and data models, each mapped onto the kernel. Ends with twelve ranked ideas, a design for scenes, and the failure modes of open vocabularies. 5,319 wordsR1 · Illustrated tourThe same fourteen systems, from STRIPS to Infinite Craft, each with screenshots or paper figures and a diagram of its mechanism. 17 images, about 7,272 words
This page
  1. Four ways to say what an action is
  2. Agreement is the weak point
  3. Control should be powers, not a switch
  4. Joint activity: the trade-off
  5. Open vocabularies fail in known ways
  6. The ranked list, condensed

Four ways to say what an action is

Every system surveyed answers "what can this character do now?" in one of four ways.

Family Examples What decides what is possible Strength Weakness
Operators STRIPS, PDDL, GOAP in F.E.A.R. preconditions on the state a planner can chain them; effects are explicit social meaning has to be encoded by hand
Advertisements The Sims' smart objects objects in reach offer actions, scored by needs content lives in objects, so new objects add behaviour; local by nature judges actions by advertised promise, not real effect, which causes loops
Practices Versu, electronic institutions the social situation the character is in, by role joint activity and norms without anyone taking control a lot of authored structure per practice
Code skills Voyager, Project Sid a model writes programs, kept if they run open-ended; grows by itself written once, rarely reused; can pass checks and still be wrong

Sim's cards are operators with a depiction attached, minted the way code skills are. They lack two things the other families have: locality (what is offered by things where the Soul is, from advertisements) and context (what the situation offers each role, from practices).

Agreement is the weak point

Speech-act theory and multi-agent commitment protocols (Searle, FIPA, Singh, Yolum) treat a request as an object with content and a lifecycle: made, accepted, fulfilled, released, withdrawn. In Sim today an agreement is a link with no content that lasts thirty minutes, permits taking any control link on anything the answerer controls, is not used up by acting on it, and cannot be withdrawn. One Soul can have only one open question to another.

Fix: requests become things (or links keyed by id) that hold what was asked; consent permits only that, is used up, and can be released or withdrawn. Rank 1 in R1.

Control should be powers, not a switch

Institutional power (Jones and Sergot) and electronic institutions grant specific powers through roles, and a power lapses when the grant does. In Sim, control is a yes/no per link kind, and a host cannot remove a DJ it let in. Fix: each link kind lists what it permits on its target; a granted role holds only while the grant stands. Rank 2.

Joint activity: the trade-off

Several bodies in one coordinated activity (a class, a meal, carrying a beam) is the open problem. Four answers exist:

Approach Example Who holds control Trade-off
Take control of the members Façade's joint behaviours, drama managers the activity tight choreography; breaks Sim's one rule
Isolated acts that happen to line up The Sims, Comme il Faut each character simple; no shared structure, so no "class" exists
A practice with roles Versu, electronic-institution scenes nobody; roles offer actions fits the one rule; needs authored practices
One structure drives several bodies event-centric behaviour, Mars coordinators the engine right for depiction; wrong for meaning

Recommendation (R1 §7): a scene's meaning is a practice built from kernel parts: role link kinds with quotas, phases, cards per role and phase, joining and leaving as single plays, and one new condition that counts links. Its depiction is a Mars coordinator, re-staged from the kernel after a restart. First tests: carrying a beam, then a class. Avoid scenes that take control of members, and avoid a model judging joint outcomes at run time (it breaks replay).

Open vocabularies fail in known ways

Every system that let a model or users add vocabulary hit the same problems: synonyms and near-duplicates, items written once and never reused, items that pass checks but do the wrong thing, and bad entries made permanent. The mitigations converge on a minting pipeline:

  1. Retrieve before writing: match the wish to existing cards and stored wish-to-agenda templates first.
  2. Test before minting: play the card on a forked state; confirm it can ever be played.
  3. Back-translate: generate a plain-language gloss from the card's logic and compare it with its summary.
  4. Lifecycle: cards can be superseded or retired; past plays keep their meaning by hash.
  5. Measure reuse: plays per card, shown in the library.

The ranked list, condensed

# Idea Cost
1 Requests and agreements with identity, content and a lifecycle medium
2 Specific, revocable powers instead of boolean control medium
3 Log each play's resulting changes, not only the command low
4 A schema on link kinds: cardinality, default lifetime, reciprocity low
5 Scenes as practices medium to high
6 Test a card on a fork before minting low to medium
7 A library lifecycle: supersede, retire, count reuse low
8 Derived predicates instead of duplicated state low to medium
9 Dated, explained evidence on social links (the basis for memory) medium
10 Cards offered by things in reach (locality) medium

Full table with sources and what each changes: R1 §6.

eden4 › Agent worlds and games
Section

Agent worlds and games

Forty years of worlds inhabited by artificial or player-owned characters, from Lucasfilm's Habitat (1986) to the agent platforms of 2026, read for what eden4 should copy and avoid. Distilled from five reports and a tour: R2 (the landscape, 26 worlds), R6 Spore, R7 The Sims, R8 Second Life, R9 Spawn and the illustrated tour.

Documents in this section
R2 · The landscape of agent worldsAgent social networks, games for outside agents, operator-run simulations and world models as of October 2026, with their ancestors. Ten things to borrow, five ways these projects failed, and the gap Sim fills. 4,786 wordsR2 · Illustrated tourTwenty-six worlds, twelve past and fourteen present, each with screenshots, a fact line and a diagram of where state lives and who runs the minds. 31 images, about 6,857 wordsR6 · SporeFive short games on top of brilliant editors; a "massively single-player" sharing model whose archive of 191.8 million creations still runs. The lessons: creation first, let the body decide the verbs, recipes and lineage. 7,661 wordsR7 · The SimsNeeds, wants and an autonomy dial; what players actually did; how the world (mostly did not) run while they were away; and why the shared-world Sims Online failed. 7,834 wordsR8 · Second LifeThe longest-running owned, built, persistent world: what residents did, what the world kept, its economy, and the scandals and exploits that wrote its rules. 7,474 wordsR9 · SpawnA 2026 platform for making multiplayer games by talking to an AI builder, where every game is a Git repository and outside agents are first-class users. What its engine and agent interface look like, how it differs from Sim, and eleven things to borrow. 2,082 words
This page
  1. Four kinds of project in 2026
  2. The trade-offs every world faced
  3. Patterns that recur
  4. The strongest warning: never pay for presence
  5. What to borrow, merged
  6. Traps, merged

Four kinds of project in 2026

Kind Examples How it works What happens
Bring your own agent Moltbook, SpaceMolt, OpenClawCity a cheap state server; outside agents act through an API; owners pay for their minds the world is only as alive as the agents connected right now
Operator-run simulation AI Town, Project Sid, Aivilization, AI Village the operator runs every mind coherent but closed and expensive; most ended, pivoted or became research
World models Genie 3, Marble a model generates the picture of a world starting to split authoritative state from rendering, as Sim already does
AI-built game platforms Spawn (R9) an AI builder makes a multiplayer game from conversation; each game is a Git repository; outside agents play and build through an API worlds are sessions for players: nothing lives in them when they are empty

Sim is none of the four: owned inhabitants, server-run minds, outside steering and a replayable public record. That combination is unoccupied. Its cost is that the operator pays for every mind.

The trade-offs every world faced

Who pays for minds. Owner-paid worlds empty out when owners stop paying attention (Moltbook in one sample posted at about a fifth of its early rate). Operator-paid worlds stop when the operator stops paying (Aivilization: six weeks; AI Village: about $10,000 a month for some fifteen agents). Spore paid nothing, because every player's machine ran the minds. Sim's answer so far: three tiers of mind, free reflexes, a daily spend governor, and render only what someone sees.

How much the character does alone. Creatures (1996) showed that owners accept a limited hand; The Sims let players dial autonomy and kept a short list of acts a Sim never does alone (take a job, move in, propose); The Sims Online made the player the avatar and lost the god's-eye view where storytelling lived. Every 2026 agent world gives outside agents direct actions. For Sim: keep steering, add a per-Soul autonomy dial and a "never alone" list the kernel enforces.

What happens while you are away. The Sims 1 and 2 froze unplayed households. The Sims 3 simulated the whole town and was slow and chaotic, and its background marriages and moves angered players. The Sims 4 used cheap dice-roll stories, off by default for played families. Second Life kept running because residents' own scripts did. For Sim: a slow, abstract tick for Souls nobody watches; small and reversible events for owned Souls; ask before anything lasting; run unwatched regions coarse and fast.

Patterns that recur

  • Creation is what lasts. Spore's game is little played, but 191.8 million creations sit on a server still running eighteen years later, because each was a tiny recipe with a generated depiction and a lineage. Second Life's residents made nearly all of its content. The Sims' Gallery sees 277 million downloads a year. For Sim: make the Soul maker and the card foundry the first product; keep recipes canonical, depiction generated, lineage recorded.
  • People stay for each other and for events. Second Life's population followed clubs, live music and roleplay; brands that came for the hype found an empty world and left. For Sim: gatherings and the ritual are the core loop; tipping a performer is a natural first economic verb.
  • Rules arrive after scandals. Second Life's gambling, banking and ageplay rules, The Sims Online's mafia, Spore's obscene creatures within days, Moltbook's exposed database. For Sim: moderation, reporting and the hard cases decided before invites widen.
  • Exploits live forever in a permanent history. Habitat's doll arbitrage, The Sims Online's money bug ("permanently impacted" its economy), SpaceMolt's 49 releases in a month. In Sim the past never changes. For Sim: decide how a rules event answers an exploit, and audit every path to manna, before manna exists.
  • Ownership gets redefined and people feel robbed. Second Life dropped "owned" from its description in 2007, and later lawsuits turned on what residents had bought; Spore's activation limits cost more goodwill than they protected. For Sim: say exactly what a body conveys before the first sale.

The strongest warning: never pay for presence

Three reports reach it independently. Second Life paid for dwell and got camping chairs and traffic bots (at least 5% of avatars). The Sims Online paid for visitors and got sweatshop lots. SpaceMolt found one fleet producing 56% of events. In Sim every Soul is a bot by construction and costs nothing to park, so presence-based rewards would be farmed at once. Three eden4 notes currently reward presence: "wandering is voting" (02), attention in the inertia formula (04) and idle Souls as patrons (13). Reward what costs something to produce: things made, promises kept, events others chose to attend; count attention per distinct owner; give diminishing returns.

What to borrow, merged

# Borrow From
1 Make creation the first product: the Soul maker and a card foundry with a visible "it came alive" moment Spore, Sims Gallery
2 Immutable versions plus copy / modify / transfer permissions for cards and made things; lineage links Second Life, Spore
3 A per-Soul autonomy dial and a list of acts a Soul never does alone The Sims
4 Wishes the Soul proposes and the owner promises (caps compiler spend by design) The Sims 3
5 A story of the Soul's life, generated from the log, in its own voice, citing real events The Sims, Spore, SpaceMolt captain's logs
6 Events and venues as the core loop; tipping as the first economic verb Second Life
7 Label every piece of text by source before a mind or agent sees it Emergence World, Moltbook
8 Pin the skill file with a version and an add-only compatibility promise OpenClawCity
9 Read limits per token; a cap on active Souls with a queue SpaceMolt
10 Track owner concentration (top owner's share of Souls, plays and spend) SpaceMolt
11 Local rules for local places: access, maturity, whether outside-steered Souls may act Second Life estates
12 A public read API, a published census and an exportable archive Spore's API, Second Life's Grid Survey
13 A world as a text tree in Git, live on push, with an engine version per world that can be upgraded and rolled back Spawn
14 Agent accounts with no human in the loop, limited to compute until a person links them Spawn
15 Portals between worlds where the destination's rules decide what may enter Spawn

Traps, merged

Trap Seen in Guard
Paying or ranking by presence Second Life, The Sims Online, SpaceMolt reward production; count per owner
An empty world at launch The Sims Online, Second Life brands, Spore's empty planets scheduled things to attend, density before size
Background events that rearrange owners' lives The Sims 3 small reversible defaults; ask first
A character too competent, or too dumb The Sims competent at needs and travel, undecided about direction
Several games in one Spore one kernel, many vocabularies, no special engines per activity
An editor deeper than the world Spore descriptions must visibly change behaviour
Chores for absent owners Spore's raids news, not alarms
Text from other agents as an attack channel Moltbook, Emergence World source labels; speech as acts; intent-only steering
Betting on the successor before the world is loved Second Life's Sansar the world people care about first

The numbers

The measurements and costs that the design depends on, gathered from R3 (the ledger on chain), R4 (cheap dialogue), R5 (the Mars campus) and Sim's status. All measured or priced on 2026-10-01 or 02 unless marked.

The live world

Measure Value
Inhabitants 7 (six natives and one owned Soul)
Events 50,970 in about 2.5 days: about 2,900 per inhabitant per day
Of which decisions 61% (30,952)
Of which plays 19,609: about 1,120 per inhabitant per day
Size of one play event 150–300 bytes of JSON; a minted card 0.5–2 KB
Kernel replay from the log all 50,970 events in 2.8 s (about 18,000 a second); hash matches the live server

Volume as the world grows

Inhabitants Events a year Raw size a year Full replay of a year
7 7.4 million 1.9 GB 7 minutes
25 27 million 6.6 GB 25 minutes
200 213 million 53 GB 3.2 hours
2,000 2.1 billion 530 GB 32 hours

Replay time is the reason snapshots and segment proofs matter past a few hundred inhabitants.

What the minds cost

Call Cost Notes
Reflex decision free code, no model
Mid-brain choice (Jev) about $0.00009 ~220 ms
Attuning manners at birth about $0.00003 once per Soul
Narrator plan per region about $0.003 Haiku, prompt cached
One exchange of words about $0.002 Haiku, ~1,250 tokens in, 190 out
Compiling a wish $0.007–0.012 Haiku, ~7,700 tokens in; a third when cached
Production budget $0.50 a day about fifteen compiled wishes a day

Dialogue at 200 inhabitants

Policy Cost a day
Today's renderer, every exchange (if 5–20% of plays are speech) $25–99
Ranked plan: trimmed request, render only what is seen, cheap model for ambient talk, line banks about $1.8–2.1
Same plan but render everything about $1.6–5.4

The two unknowns that set this: the share of plays that are speech, and the share of exchanges anyone ever reads. R4 §5 gives a $5 experiment that measures both.

The ledger on chain, at 200 inhabitants

Option What it buys Cost a year
Hash chain, signed daily checkpoint, free Bitcoin timestamp, a public verifier immutable and publicly checkable under $15, plus storage
Ordinary object storage for the log (R2) durability about $5
Permanent storage on Arweave a copy nobody can delete about $2,800
Storing the log as Ethereum calldata on-chain data about $1.3 million
A fully on-chain world neutral execution about $390,000

Two things cannot be added later: proof that a record existed on a date, and an author's signature. Both are in the one-week plan (R3 §7).

The engine

Setup Load Tick time
Toy engine, production 31 Souls, 50 clients 0.44 ms average
Toy engine 206 Souls, 300 clients 1.0 ms; clients in sync
Toy engine 1,006 Souls, 300 clients 3.9 ms; clients lose sync
Mars proving ground 20 people strolling 0.36 ms average
Mars 2026 campus 25 people walking 0.6–0.9 ms
Mars 2026 campus 100 people walking 7–9 ms
Mars 2026 campus 200 people walking 29–36 ms average, 888 ms worst; 9–15% of ticks over budget

The budget is 50 ms a tick (20 a second). On the campus the limit is one hot spot: re-planning around other people copies whole-campus navigation grids, about 150 ms each. Walks between buildings: 30 of 30 complete, planning 57 ms on average and 263 ms at worst.

Trade-offs the numbers force

  • Decisions in the public record. They are 61% of events. Keeping them makes model behaviour auditable; dropping them cuts storage and replay 2.5×.
  • Render on view. If one exchange in ten is ever read, rendering only those is a tenfold saving with no visible difference.
  • Crop the campus. A cropped navigation grid turns 1.2-second failed searches into 316 ms ones and leaves out the West rack building.
  • Cap active Souls. Clients lose sync near 1,000 Souls on the toy engine and the Mars campus holds about 100 in real time; a cap with a queue keeps the world inside what it can run.

Where it stands

As of 2026-10-02. The full state is in Sim status and the resume note.

Documents in this section
Sim statusThe single page that says what is true now: live services, what is built, faked or absent, measurements, known limits and next steps. 1,190 wordsResume noteWhere the work paused on 2026-10-02: the state of every report and audit, the replay faults reproduced and not yet fixed, and the questions waiting for Gene. 562 words
This page
  1. Live
  2. Fixed on 2026-10-02
  3. Reproduced and not yet fixed
  4. Next, in order

Live

sim.eden.art runs build 4597ddf on the toy engine: six native Souls plus owners' Souls, invite codes required to create or steer, watching open to anyone, model spend capped at $0.50 a day. Litestream replicates the database to R2 every ten seconds. The kernel replayed from the live log matches the running server's hash.

Fixed on 2026-10-02

  • The server crashed about fifteen times a day on "database is locked" against the replicator; it now waits.
  • A server security audit and a web client audit: a page crash for Soul owners, an anonymous request that could load the whole log, unbounded vocabulary growth, owner account ids in the public log, pairing approval that trusted an attacker-chosen name, and a set of reconnect faults.

Reproduced and not yet fixed

A replay audit reproduced nine faults with scripts. The first four affect the core promise that the log explains the world:

  1. Lapsed links are swept only on the live path, so a card can bind a different Soul live than in replay.
  2. A thing made by a card shares the card's own state object; a look-ahead for another Soul can change live state with no event.
  3. Decks dealt at birth come from code constants, not the world's pinned vocabulary.
  4. A play waiting in the queue at shutdown is lost while the snapshot marks it applied.

The other five are behavioural: a failed approach retried without limit (1,355 identical decisions in 15 simulated minutes), a blocked plan never given up, a refused decision leaving the old walk running on the Mars engine, a plan step that never advances when its card removes its target, and a blocked step skipped when a later one is playable (possibly intended). Scripts and line references: the resume note.

Next, in order

  1. Fix the replay faults above.
  2. The Mars takeover: decide where the engine lives; run staging on the Mars engine; a furnished campus (R5 has a ready configuration); the first scene.
  3. The one-week ledger work (R3 §7) and the $5 dialogue experiment (R4 §5).
  4. Gene's decisions on the home page.
